--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 1 ---
Joe and I, wending our way to the house of prayer, presented a spectacle that might have moved the tenderest heart; yet the turmoil within my breast eclipsed any outward pity. The dread that seized me whenever Mrs. Joe ventured toward the pantry, or slipped from the room, was matched only by the remorse that gnawed at my soul for the deed my hands had wrought. Beneath the burden of this wicked secret, I mused whether the sanctuary of the church could shield me from the vengeance of the dreadful youth, should I divulge my crime to its holy ministers. I imagined that the moment when the banns were proclaimed and the clergyman intoned, “Ye are now to declare it!” would be the fitting hour for me to rise and seek a private conference in the vestry. I cannot say with certainty that I would not have startled our modest congregation by such an extreme recourse, had it not been Christmas Day, and no ordinary Sunday.

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 2 ---
The sudden apparition of a file of soldiers, striking the butts of their loaded muskets against our doorstep, threw the dinner-party into disarray, causing them to leap from their seats in bewilderment. Mrs. Joe, re-entering the kitchen empty-handed, halted abruptly, her eyes wide, and uttered a lament worthy of a startled housewife: “Gracious goodness gracious me, what’s gone—with the—pie!”

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 3 ---
“Pardon me, ladies and gentlemen,” said the sergeant, “though I have made mention at the door to this smart young shaver”—though he had not—“I am engaged in a pursuit upon the king’s business, and I require the blacksmith’s aid.”

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 4 ---
The sergeant took his courteous leave of the ladies, and bade farewell to Mr. Pumblechook as one might part from a comrade—though I doubt he fully appreciated that gentleman’s virtues in arid circumstances, as he would when some moisture was about. His men shouldered their muskets and fell into step. Mr. Wopsle, Joe, and I were given strict injunction to remain in the rear and to utter no word once we reached the marshes. As we stepped into the raw air and proceeded steadily toward our errand, I, treasonously, whispered to Joe, “I hope, Joe, we shan’t find them.” Joe, in turn, whispered back, “I’d give a shilling if they had cut and run, Pip.”

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 5 ---
“Tried to murder him?” exclaimed the convict, with disdain. “To attempt and not achieve? I seized him, and delivered him up; that is what I did. Not only did I thwart his escape from the marshes, but I dragged him hither—dragged him this far upon his return. He is a gentleman, if you please, this villain. Now the Hulks boast a gentleman once more, thanks to me. To murder him? It would be worth my while, too, to slay him, when I could do worse and drag him back!”

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 6 ---
“Fine fare, eh, sergeant?” remarked Mr. Pumblechook.

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 7 ---
I seized the leg of the table once more, pressing it to my breast as though it were the cherished companion of my youth and the confidant of my soul. I foresaw the impending doom, and felt, this time, that I was truly undone.

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 8 ---
My sister, Mrs. Joe, possessed sable hair and eyes, and a prevailing ruddiness of complexion that led me at times to wonder whether she cleansed herself with a nutmeg‑grater rather than soap. Tall and bony, she was seldom seen without a coarse apron, fastened behind her figure by two loops, and bearing a stout, impregnable bib before, studded with pins and needles. She regarded this apron as a signal merit of her own, and a pointed reproach against Joe, for she wore it incessantly. I could discern no reason why she should don it at all, nor, if she did, why she should not remove it each day of her life.

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 9 ---
“Say, Lord strike you dead if you do not!” the man declared.

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 10 ---
The sergeant, reflecting, observed, “Even if I were compelled to halt here for nigh two hours, that would suffice. How far, pray, would you estimate yourselves to be from the marshes, about these parts? Not more than a mile, I reckon?”

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 11 ---
He set off, made a brief sprint, then halted and glanced over his shoulder.

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 12 ---
I had fully anticipated finding a constable awaiting in the kitchen, ready to take me into custody. Yet not only was no constable present, but no trace of the robbery had yet been uncovered. Mrs. Joe laboured prodigiously in readying the house for the day’s festivities, while Joe had been stationed upon the kitchen doorstep to keep him clear of the dust‑pan—an article into which his destiny invariably led him, sooner or later, when my sister vigorously swept the floors of her establishment.

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 13 ---
In that assembled company I ought to have felt myself, even had I not pilfered the pantry, in a false stance—not because I was wedged at an acute angle of the tablecloth, with the table pressing against my chest and Mr. Pumblechook’s elbow jabbing my eye, nor because I was forbidden to speak (I had no desire to do so), nor because I was offered the scaly tips of the drumsticks and those obscure corners of pork which the living swine had little cause to pride itself upon. No; I would have endured that, had they but left me in peace. Yet they would not leave me alone; they seemed to think the opportunity lost if they failed to turn the discourse toward me at intervals, and to thrust their pointed remarks into me. I felt as an hapless young bull in a Spanish arena, so smartingly goaded by these moral prods.

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 14 ---
On such occasions we took our repast in the kitchen, and thereafter withdrew to the parlour for nuts, oranges, and apples—a shift as familiar as Joe’s transformation from his labouring garb to his Sunday attire. My sister was uncommonly animated on this present occasion, and indeed generally more gracious in the society of Mrs. Hubble than in other companies. I recall Mrs. Hubble as a petite, curly, sharp‑edged figure attired in sky‑blue, who held a conventionally juvenile station, owing to her marriage to Mr. Hubble—though I know not at what remote epoch—when she was considerably younger than he. Mr. Hubble I remember as a rugged, high‑shouldered, stooping old man, redolent of sawdust, with his legs set extraordinarily wide apart; thus, in my brief years, I always perceived a stretch of open country between them when I encountered him approaching up the lane.

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 15 ---
The assemblage murmured their approbation. Uncle Pumblechook, sensible of having merited well of his fellow‑creatures, declared—rather vivaciously, all things considered—“Well, Mrs. Joe, we shall endeavour our utmost; let us have a slice of this very pie.”

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 16 ---
“Has she been absent long, Joe?” I inquired, ever regarding him as a more mature sort of child, and scarcely more than my peer.

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 17 ---
“It is a pie,” announced my sister, rising, “a savory pork pie.”

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 18 ---
“And pray, what are the Hulks?” I inquired.

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 19 ---
“Yonder,” said I, gesturing, “over yonder, where I discovered him nodding asleep, and mistook him for you.”

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 20 ---
“Take heed, guard,” he began, “this man endeavoured to murder me.”

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 21 ---
Joe emitted a reproachful cough, as though to say, “Well, I told you so.”

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 22 ---
My sister departed to fetch it. I heard her footsteps retreat toward the pantry. I observed Mr. Pumblechook poising his knife. I perceived the rekindling of appetite in Mr. Wopsle’s Roman nostrils. I heard Mr. Hubble observe that “a morsel of savory pork pie would crown any fare and do no harm,” and I heard Joe assure, “You shall have some, Pip.” I could not ascertain with certainty whether I uttered a shrill cry of terror, merely in spirit, or aloud amidst the company. I felt that I could endure no more, and that flight was imperative. I relinquished my grasp upon the table leg and fled for my life.

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 23 ---
Joe divested himself of coat and waistcoat, cravat loosened, and donned his leather apron before entering the forge. One of the soldiers threw open the wooden casements, another kindled the fire, a third turned to the bellows, while the remainder stood about the blaze, which soon rose to a fierce roar. Then Joe commenced his hammering—clink, clink, clink—as we all looked on.

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 24 ---
“Churchyard!” repeated my sister. “Had it not been for me, you would long since have been laid in the churchyard and remained there. Who, pray, brought you up by hand?”

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 25 ---
My sister was not comely, and I formed the impression that she must have compelled Joe Gargery to wed her by sheer force of hand. Joe was a fair man, adorned with curls of flaxen hue upon each side of his smooth countenance, and possessed eyes of such an indecisive blue that they seemed to have mingled with their own whites. He bore a mild, good‑natured, sweet‑tempered, easy‑going, foolish, dear disposition—a veritable Hercules in both strength and frailty.

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 26 ---
I beheld all these occurrences, yet at the moment I knew not that I perceived them, for I was gripped by an agony of dread. However, as I began to discern that the handcuffs were not destined for me, and that the military had, for the time being, gained the upper hand over the pie, relegating it to the background, I gathered together a fragment of my scattered senses.

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 27 ---
The resolve required to accomplish this purpose struck me as utterly formidable; it felt akin to resolving to leap from the summit of a lofty edifice, or to plunge into a profound abyss. Moreover, the insensible Joe rendered the task yet more arduous. In the fellowship we had already noted—our mutual suffering—and in his genial companionship, it had become our evening custom to compare, in silent homage, the manner in which we each dispatched our slices of bread, holding them aloft for mutual appraisal, thereby spurring each other to fresh endeavour. This evening, Joe repeatedly offered, by exhibiting his rapidly dwindling slice, to renew our accustomed friendly rivalry; yet each time he found me with my yellow mug of tea poised upon one knee and my untouched bread and butter resting upon the other. At length, compelled by desperation, I concluded that the contemplated act must be undertaken, and that it ought to be effected in the least implausible manner befitting the circumstances. I seized the moment when Joe had just turned his gaze toward me, and slipped my bread and butter down my leg.

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 28 ---
I remarked that I had descended to listen to the carols. “Ah, well!” observed Mrs. Joe. “You might have fared worse.” I entertained no doubt of this.

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 29 ---
My sister lunged at me, seized me by the hair, and, without further utterance, pronounced the dreadful words: “Come along and be dosed.”

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 30 ---
“Who, then, is discharging their weapons?” I inquired.

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 31 ---
He gripped me by the collar and fixed his stare upon me with such intensity that I began to suspect his original notion of cutting my throat had been rekindled.

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 32 ---
“I anticipate no benefit therefrom,” declared the convict with a greedy chuckle, “nor do I desire it to avail me more than it already does. I apprehended him; he is aware of the fact; that suffices me.”

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 33 ---
“I say, you know!” muttered Joe, shaking his head at me in grave admonition. “Pip, old fellow, you shall bring harm upon yourself; it will lodge somewhere; you cannot have chewed it, Pip.”

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 34 ---
Mrs. Joe, ever inclined to assume the mantle of explicator, snapped, “Escaped. Escaped.” She delivered the pronouncement as though administering a dose of tar‑water.

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 35 ---
“It is but half past two,” she noted.

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 36 ---
“Once more,” the man intoned, fixing his gaze upon me, “utter it aloud!”

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 37 ---
“Ha!” he muttered, pondering, “With whom dost thou dwell, assuming thou art permitted to live—a matter I have not yet resolved upon?”

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 38 ---
Chapter IV.

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 39 ---
Simultaneously, he clasped his trembling frame within both arms, embracing himself as though to hold his very being together, and limp‑footed toward the low church wall. As I watched him depart, threading his path amid nettles and brambles that encircled the verdant mounds, he lifted his gaze to my youthful eyes, as if seeking to elude the grasp of the dead, whose skeletal hands reached timidly from their graves to seize his ankle and draw him down.

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 40 ---
He was already thrusting mincemeat down his gullet in a most singular fashion—resembling less a man partaking of sustenance than one hurriedly stowing it away in some concealed receptacle—yet he paused to swallow a draught of liquor. His frame shuddered with such violence that he could scarcely retain the bottle’s neck between his teeth without risking a bite.

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 41 ---
“Indeed, sir.”

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 42 ---
“You must partake,” my sister addressed the guests with her finest grace, “you must taste, to conclude, such a delightful and delicious offering from Uncle Pumblechook!”

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 43 ---
Joe ladled additional gravy upon my plate.

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 44 ---
“Were I not the blacksmith’s wife,” Mrs. Joe mused, “and, to speak plainly, a bondservant whose apron is never removed, I might have ventured to hear the carols.” She added, “I am myself quite fond of carols, and that very affection furnishes the finest excuse for my never having listened to any.”

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 45 ---
I declared that I would procure the file for him, and that I would gather whatever fragments of victuals I could muster, and that I would repair to the Battery at daybreak to meet him there.

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 47 ---
“There was a convict at large last night,” Joe proclaimed aloud, “after the sunset gun. They discharged a warning shot at him. And now it seems they are firing a warning at yet another.”

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 48 ---
Water splashed and mud flew, oaths rang out and blows were exchanged, when additional men descended into the ditch to aid the sergeant, and hauled forth, separately, my convict and his companion. Both were bleeding, panting, cursing, and straining; yet I recognised them instantly.

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 49 ---
“Madam,” replied the gallant sergeant, “speaking for myself, I would say that it has been an honour and a pleasure to make the acquaintance of his fine wife; speaking on behalf of the king, I answer that a modest task has been accomplished.”

--- great_expectations | Ambition and Self-Improvement | Paragraph 50 ---
It availed me nothing to pose that question unto myself at that juncture. There I was, perched upon Joe's back, while Joe himself, beneath me, surged toward the ditches with the zeal of a hunter, urging Mr. Wopsle lest he tumble upon his Roman visage and beseeching him to keep pace with us. The soldiers marched ahead, forming a broad line with intervals between each man. We pursued the route I had initially embarked upon, from which I had strayed amidst the mist. Whether the mist had yet to lift again, or the wind had scattered it, I could not say. Beneath the low crimson glare of the setting sun, the beacon, the gibbet, the Battery mound, and the far shore of the river stood distinct, though each bore a watery leaden hue.

--- great_expectations | Social Class | Paragraph 1 ---
The landscape of our marshland domain, where the sluggish river carved its serpentine path toward the distant sea, was a realm of stark desolation and brooding mystery. On that fateful evening, when the very air seemed to shudder with the approach of dusk, my young mind first grasped the haunting truth of my existence. The bleak expanse, choked with thorny brambles and skeletal reeds, revealed itself as the final resting place of the departed Pirrip clan—a graveyard where the whispers of the dead mingled with the mournful sighs of the wind. Here, in that eerie expanse, the infantile fragments of my identity—tiny Pip, a trembling mite of terror—were etched into the raw fabric of my consciousness, as I beheld the grim procession of headstones and the leaden hush of the marshes stretching beyond.

--- great_expectations | Social Class | Paragraph 2 ---
The figure before me, a wraith-like silhouette against the dying light, clutched his shuddering frame in a desperate embrace, as though the very act of holding himself together might stave the encroaching chill. His gait, a laborious stagger, carried him toward the low stone wall that bordered the churchyard, each step a testament to his tortured limbs. To my wide-eyed terror, he moved like a man pursued by phantoms, his gaunt figure bending and weaving as if the grasping claws of the interred dead sought to drag him back into their earthen prisons. The brambles clawed at his tattered garments, yet he pressed onward, a shadow fleeing the clutches of the grave.

--- great_expectations | Social Class | Paragraph 3 ---
Upon reaching the weathered wall, he scaled it with the labored hesitance of one whose bones had been steeped in frost. His back, curved like a question mark, turned toward me as I stood frozen in the encroaching darkness. Yet, in that fleeting moment, I spied his silhouette melting into the gathering gloom, his hunched form retreating toward the river’s murky embrace. With limbs akimbo and heart pounding, he navigated the treacherous terrain of jagged stones—those grim stepping-stones left by autumn’s wrath—his every movement a dance with the abyss. I, a fugitive in my own right, spurred home with all the urgency of a soul pursued by his own nameless dread.

--- great_expectations | Social Class | Paragraph 4 ---
“Churchyard, indeed!” Mrs. Joe’s voice rang out like a whip crack, her shrill tone cutting through the evening’s unease. “You’ll drive *me* to the churchyard betwixt you, one of these days, and *O*, a precious pair you’d be without me!” Her ire was a tempest, and though I had uttered naught but the truth, her accusation fell like a thunderclap, reducing the room to a silence broken only by the ticking of the clock and the echo of her indignation. The very air seemed to tremble at her wrath, a monument to her fierce, unyielding spirit.

--- great_expectations | Social Class | Paragraph 5 ---
In the watches of that sleepless night, I was haunted by visions of drifting down the river’s treacherous currents, a spectral vessel bound for the Hulks. A ghastly voice, bellowing through a trumpet of infernal brass, warned me to abandon my course and face the hangman’s noose at the gibbet’s stake. Each creak of the bedframe, each whisper of the wind, was a summons to the gallows, and I dared not close my eyes, lest the darkness swallow me whole. The pantry robbery, that dire necessity, could not be undertaken in the black of night, for the very act of striking flint would summon forth the devil himself, rattling his chains in mockery of my plight.

--- great_expectations | Social Class | Paragraph 6 ---
Yet lo, this figure—though clad in coarse homespun and bearing the brand of a felon—was a creature of contrasts. His iron-shackled leg and stooped frame bore the marks of his servitude, yet his eyes, though wild and desperate, held a cunning that spoke of survival. His attempt to strike me was a feeble flourish, a blow that missed its mark and sent him sprawling into the mist, his stumbling gait a farce of menace. In that fleeting glimpse, I beheld the duality of his nature: a man shaped by suffering, yet defiant in his defiance of the world’s judgment.

--- great_expectations | Social Class | Paragraph 7 ---
The dining table, a stage for these peculiar gatherings, bore the weight of mismatched company. We feasted in the kitchen, a realm of soot and clatter, before adjourning to the parlor—a chamber of faded grandeur where the scent of oranges and apples mingled with the ghosts of better days. My sister, ever the tyrant of her domain, was buoyed by the presence of Mrs. Hubble, a sprite-like creature whose marriage to the ancient Mr. Hubble had elevated her to a position of mock importance. He, a titan of sawdust and starch, loomed over the table with legs splayed like twin towers, his every gesture a monument to the absurdity of his station.

--- great_expectations | Social Class | Paragraph 8 ---
The meal began with Mr. Wopsle’s theatrical benediction, a soliloquy that might have been lifted from the pages of some melodramatic tragedy. His voice, a deep baritone of self-importance, dripped with the pomposity of a man who fancied himself a latter-day Hamlet. The conclusion of his prayer—a fervent supplication for gratitude—drawn forth a withering glance from my sister, whose whispered retort, “Do you hear that? Be grateful,” hung in the air like a challenge to the very heavens. The tension between piety and hypocrisy crackled with the force of a lightning strike.

--- great_expectations | Social Class | Paragraph 9 ---
Joe’s presence in the company was a study in quiet resignation. His station, though diminished by the presence of guests, never faltered in its kindness. At the dinner table, he assumed the role of humble servant, his hands steadied by an unspoken duty. When he ladled gravy into my plate, his gesture was a small act of rebellion against the tyranny of the household—a silent defiance of the social order that sought to crush his gentle spirit.

--- great_expectations | Social Class | Paragraph 10 ---
“Trouble?” my sister echoed, her voice a whip crack of inquiry. “Trouble?” Her words, laced with mock sympathy, unspooled into a diatribe of imagined ailments and nocturnal wanderings. She recounted my childhood indiscretions with the fervor of a prosecutor, each accusation a dagger aimed at my fragile psyche. The list of my sins—real and imagined—grew longer with each breath, as she wove a tapestry of my supposed transgressions, concluding with her bitter lament: “I have wished you in your grave, and you have refused to go there.”

--- great_expectations | Crime and Justice | Paragraph 1 ---
As Mrs. Joe sat, her head bent industriously over her needlework, I shaped my young lips to the form of a question, putting it to Joe, “What is a convic’?” Joe, in turn, shaped his own mouth into such a highly elaborate and roundabout response that I could decipher nothing from its labyrinth of words but the single, solitary word, “Pip.”

--- great_expectations | Crime and Justice | Paragraph 2 ---
“Now, that’s the way with this boy!” exclaimed my sister, pointing me out with her needle and thread, and shaking her head at me as if I were a personal affront. “Ask him but one question, and he’ll trouble you with a dozen directly. Hulks are prison-ships, right across the meshes.” The meshes were, and ever shall be in our country, the name for those desolate marshes.

--- great_expectations | Crime and Justice | Paragraph 3 ---
“I wonder,” said I, in a general way, and with a quiet desperation that belied my young years, “who’s put into prison-ships, and why they’re put there?”

--- great_expectations | Crime and Justice | Paragraph 4 ---
“With you, Hob and nob,” returned the sergeant. “The top of mine to the foot of yours—the foot of yours to the top of mine—Ring once, ring twice—the best tune on the Musical Glasses! Your health. May you live a thousand years, and never be a worse judge of the right sort than you are at the present moment of your life!”

--- great_expectations | Crime and Justice | Paragraph 5 ---
Now that we were out upon the dismal wilderness, where they little suspected I had been within eight or nine hours, and had seen both men hiding, I considered for the first time, with a great dread, if we should come upon them, would my particular convict suppose that it was I who had brought the soldiers there? He had asked me if I was a deceiving imp, and he had said I should be a fierce young hound if I joined the hunt against him. Would he believe that I was both imp and hound in treacherous earnest, and had betrayed him?

--- great_expectations | Crime and Justice | Paragraph 6 ---
The soldiers were moving on in the direction of the old Battery, and we were moving on a little way behind them, when, all of a sudden, we all stopped. For there had reached us, on the wings of the wind and rain, a long shout. It was repeated. It was at a distance towards the east, but it was long and loud. Nay, there seemed to be two or more shouts raised together—if one might judge from a confusion in the sound.

--- great_expectations | Crime and Justice | Paragraph 7 ---
To this effect the sergeant and the nearest men were speaking under their breath, when Joe and I came up. After another moment’s listening, Joe (who was a good judge) agreed, and Mr. Wopsle (who was a bad judge) agreed. The sergeant, a decisive man, ordered that the sound should not be answered, but that the course should be changed, and that his men should make towards it “at the double.” So we slanted to the right (where the East was), and Joe pounded away so wonderfully, that I had to hold on tight to keep my seat.

--- great_expectations | Crime and Justice | Paragraph 8 ---
Water was splashing, and mud was flying, and oaths were being sworn, and blows were being struck, when some more men went down into the ditch to help the sergeant, and dragged out, separately, my convict and the other one. Both were bleeding and panting and execrating and struggling; but of course I knew them both directly.

--- great_expectations | Crime and Justice | Paragraph 9 ---
“Mind!” said my convict, wiping blood from his face with his ragged sleeve, and shaking torn hair from his fingers: “_I_ took him! _I_ give him up to you! Mind that!”

--- great_expectations | Crime and Justice | Paragraph 10 ---
“I don’t expect it to do me any good. I don’t want it to do me more good than it does now,” said my convict, with a greedy laugh. “I took him. He knows it. That’s enough for me.”

--- great_expectations | Crime and Justice | Paragraph 11 ---
The other convict was livid to look at, and, in addition to the old bruised left side of his face, seemed to be bruised and torn all over. He could not so much as get his breath to speak, until they were both separately handcuffed, but leaned upon a soldier to keep himself from falling.

--- great_expectations | Crime and Justice | Paragraph 12 ---
“Tried to murder him?” said my convict, disdainfully. “Try, and not do it? I took him, and giv’ him up; that’s what I done. I not only prevented him getting off the marshes, but I dragged him here—dragged him this far on his way back. He’s a gentleman, if you please, this villain. Now, the Hulks has got its gentleman again, through me. Murder him? Worth my while, too, to murder him, when I could do worse and drag him back!”

--- great_expectations | Crime and Justice | Paragraph 13 ---
“Lookee here!” said my convict to the sergeant. “Single-handed I got clear of the prison-ship; I made a dash and I done it. I could ha’ got clear of these death-cold flats likewise—look at my leg: you won’t find much iron on it—if I hadn’t made the discovery that _he_ was here. Let _him_ go free? Let _him_ profit by the means as I found out? Let _him_ make a tool of me afresh and again? Once more? No, no, no. If I had died at the bottom there,” and he made an emphatic swing at the ditch with his manacled hands, “I’d have held to him with that grip, that you should have been safe to find him in my hold.”

--- great_expectations | Crime and Justice | Paragraph 14 ---
“He lies!” said my convict, with fierce energy. “He’s a liar born, and he’ll die a liar. Look at his face; ain’t it written there? Let him turn those eyes of his on me. I defy him to do it.”

--- great_expectations | Crime and Justice | Paragraph 15 ---
The other one still gasped, “He tried—he tried-to—murder me. Bear—bear witness.”

--- great_expectations | Crime and Justice | Paragraph 16 ---
“Did you speak?”

--- great_expectations | Crime and Justice | Paragraph 17 ---
“He was a world of trouble to you, ma’am,” said Mrs. Hubble, commiserating my sister.

--- great_expectations | Crime and Justice | Paragraph 18 ---
It began the moment we sat down to dinner. Mr. Wopsle said grace with theatrical declamation—as it now appears to me, something like a religious cross of the Ghost in Hamlet with Richard the Third—and ended with the very proper aspiration that we might be truly grateful. Upon which my sister fixed me with her eye, and said, in a low reproachful voice, “Do you hear that? Be grateful.”

--- great_expectations | Crime and Justice | Paragraph 19 ---
“Looked? When?”

--- great_expectations | Crime and Justice | Paragraph 20 ---
I took the advice. My sister, Mrs. Joe, throwing the door wide open, and finding an obstruction behind it, immediately divined the cause, and applied Tickler to its further investigation. --- great_expectations | Coming of Age | Paragraph 1 ---
I bear the name Pirrip, as attested by my father’s gravestone and the testimony of my sister, Mrs. Joe Gargery, who wed the blacksmith. Never having beheld my parents, nor any likeness thereof—since their days antedated the invention of photography—I formed my earliest notions of their appearance from the very stones that marked their resting place. The angular letters upon my father’s slab suggested to my youthful imagination a squat, corpulent, sable‑haired man, his locks curling like serpents. From the inscription ‘Also Georgiana Wife of the Above’ I inferred that my mother was flecked with freckles and of a delicate constitution. Moreover, the five modest stone lozenges, each about eighteen inches in length, aligned neatly beside their grave, commemorating five infant brothers who had relinquished the struggle for life almost at its inception, led me to cherish the pious belief that they had entered the world lying upon their backs, their tiny hands forever lodged within the pockets of their breeches, never to be withdrawn during their brief sojourn.

‘Listen well,’ the man continued, ‘you must bring to me, at the first light of tomorrow, that file and the victuals. Carry the whole lot to the old Battery yonder. Should you obey, and never utter a syllable nor make the slightest gesture betraying that you have beheld me—or any other soul—you shall be spared. But should you falter, or deviate in the slightest particular from my words, I shall have your heart and liver torn forth, roasted, and devoured. Know this: I am not solitary, as you might suppose. A youth lurks beside me, and compared to him I am an angel. That lad hears every word I utter; he possesses a secret, peculiar art whereby he can reach a boy, lay hold of his heart, and seize his liver. No child can conceal himself from him, be the door bolted, the bed warm, the covers drawn over the head, the mind at ease; he will creep silently, insinuate himself, and rend the victim asunder. I, with great exertion, restrain that young man from harming you at this very moment; it is a struggle to keep him from your very entrails. Now, what say you?’

--- great_expectations | Coming of Age | Paragraph 16 ---
The marshes stretched before me as a long, black, horizontal band; the river resembled another such line, less broad and not so sable; the sky was a jumble of long, angry red streaks intertwined with dense black bands. Upon the river’s edge I could just discern the two solitary black objects that stood upright amidst the expanse: one, the beacon whereby sailors steer—an unhooped cask upon a pole, an unsightly sight at close range; the other, a gibbet bearing chains that once had held a pirate. The man limped toward the latter, as if he were that very pirate returned to life, descended, and seeking to re‑hang himself. The thought struck me with horror, and as I watched the cattle lift their heads to follow him, I wondered whether they shared my sentiment. I scanned the horizon for the dreadful youth, but saw no trace of him. Yet my fear renewed, and I fled homeward without pausing.

--- great_expectations | Coming of Age | Paragraph 17 ---

Aware that Mrs. Joe’s household was governed by the sternest economy, and that my furtive searches might yield nothing within the locked store, I determined to conceal my lump of bread and butter within the leg of my breeches.

--- great_expectations | Coming of Age | Paragraph 22 ---
‘You know, old chap,’ Joe remarked, fixing his gaze upon me rather than upon Mrs. Joe, with the remnants of his bite still lodged in his cheek, ‘I myself took to bolting when I was your years—often—and as a lad I fell in with many a bolter; yet I have never witnessed your equal in bolt‑craft, Pip, and ’tis a mercy you have not bolted yourself to death.’

--- great_expectations | Coming of Age | Paragraph 23 ---
Conscience, when it accuses a man or a boy, is a dreadful thing; yet when, in the case of a lad, that secret weight partners with another secret burden concealed within the leg of his breeches, the punishment becomes, as I can attest, most grievous. The guilty knowledge that I intended to purloin from Mrs. Joe—I never imagined myself thieving from Joe, for I never regarded any of the household goods as his—combined with the incessant need to keep one hand upon my bread and butter, whether I sat still or was dispatched upon some trifling kitchen errand, all but unseated my reason. Moreover, when the marsh winds fanned the fire into a fitful blaze, I fancied I heard the voice of the iron‑legged man beyond the door, the very one who had bound me to silence, declaring that he could neither suffer starvation nor defer his sustenance until the morrow, but must be fed forthwith. At other moments I pondered: What if the young man, whose restraint over me had been so arduous, should yield to a sudden constitutional impatience, or misreckon the hour, and presume himself entitled to my heart and liver this very night rather than the next? Should any mortal’s hair ever rise on end from terror, surely mine must have done so then. Yet perhaps no one has ever experienced such a sensation.

--- great_expectations | Coming of Age | Paragraph 24 ---
It was Christmas Eve, and I was tasked with stirring the pudding for the morrow, using a copper rod, from seven to eight according to the Dutch clock. I attempted the task while bearing the weight upon my leg—a circumstance that recalled anew the man burdened with his own iron—and discovered that the motion of exercise tended to dislodge the concealed bread and butter at my ankle, a predicament most unmanageable. Fortunately I slipped away and deposited that fragment of my troubled conscience within the garret chamber.

--- great_expectations | Coming of Age | Paragraph 25 ---
‘Blast that boy,’ my sister interjected, frowning over her needlework, ‘what an incessant questioner he is. Pose no queries, and you shall hear no falsehoods.’

--- great_expectations | Coming of Age | Paragraph 26 ---
‘Good heavens, bless the boy!’ my sister exclaimed, though her tone suggested the opposite, ‘straight from the Hulks!’

--- great_expectations | Coming of Age | Paragraph 27 ---
‘Such is the nature of this lad!’ my sister cried, gesturing toward me with her needle and thread, shaking her head. ‘Answer him a single query, and he will bombard you with a dozen more. Hulks, as we call them, are prison‑ships lying right across the marshes.’ In our district we invariably applied that name to the fens.

--- great_expectations | Coming of Age | Paragraph 28 ---
Since that epoch, now sufficiently distant, I have frequently reflected upon how few comprehend the secrecy that dwells within youth when terror grips them, be the fear ever so unreasonable. I was seized by mortal dread of the youth who coveted my heart and liver; I stood in equal terror of my interlocutor bearing the iron leg; I trembled at the thought of myself, from whom a frightful pledge had been wrested; I held no expectation of rescue from my omnipotent sister, who repulsed me at every turn; and I shudder to contemplate what deeds I might have been compelled to perform, shrouded in the secrecy of my terror.

--- great_expectations | Coming of Age | Paragraph 29 ---
The mist grew denser still as I stepped onto the marshes, so that rather than I charging at objects, the objects seemed to surge toward me—a circumstance most disagreeable to a conscience laden with guilt. The gates, dikes, and banks loomed forth through the fog as if they cried aloud, ‘A boy bearing another’s pork pie! Halt!’ The cattle fell upon me with the same abrupt stare, their eyes fixed, their nostrils exhaling vapour, bellowing, ‘Halloa, young thief!’ A sable ox, adorned with a white cravat, which to my awakened conscience lent a semblance of clerical gravity, locked its gaze upon me obstinately, swinging its blunt head to and fro in an accusatory rhythm matching my own turns, whereupon I blubbered, ‘I could not help it, sir! It was not for myself that I took it!’ Thereupon the beast lowered its head, expelled a cloud of smoke from its nostrils, and vanished with a flurry of hind‑leg kicks and a flourish of its tail.

--- great_expectations | Coming of Age | Paragraph 30 ---
All the while I pressed onward toward the river, yet no matter how swiftly I advanced, my feet remained chilled, the damp cold clinging to them as firmly as the iron clung to the stranger’s limb. I knew the route to the Battery tolerably straight, for I had visited it on a Sunday with Joe, who, perched upon an antiquated cannon, had assured me that, once bound as his apprentice, we would share many a lark at that spot. Nevertheless, the mist’s confusion led me to drift too far to the right, compelling me to retrace my steps along the riverbank, upon a shelf of loose stones situated above the mire and the stakes that demarcated the tide. Hurrying with all dispatch, I had just traversed a ditch I recognised as lying near the Battery, and had just scrambled up the rise beyond it, when I beheld the man seated before me, his back turned, his arms folded across his chest, his head nodding forward beneath the weight of slumber.

--- great_expectations | Coming of Age | Paragraph 31 ---
I imagined he would welcome my approach with his breakfast, offered so unexpectedly, and thus I advanced with soft footsteps, laying my hand upon his shoulder. He sprang upright in an instant, yet it was not the same individual, but a different man!

--- great_expectations | Coming of Age | Paragraph 32 ---
Yet this newcomer, too, was attired in coarse grey, bore a great iron upon his leg, was lame, hoarse, and chilled, and mirrored the former in every respect save for his visage and the flat, broad‑brimmed low‑crowned felt hat that crowned his head. All these details impressed themselves upon me in the fleeting instant I had to observe them: he cursed me with an oath, swung at me—a feeble, round blow that missed its mark and nearly toppled him, for it caused him to stumble—then he fled into the mist, stumbling twice as he went, and I lost sight of him.

--- great_expectations | Coming of Age | Paragraph 33 ---
‘It is the youth!’ I thought, my heart leaping as I recognised him; I dare say I ought to have felt a pang in my liver as well, had I been aware of its whereabouts.

--- great_expectations | Coming of Age | Paragraph 34 ---
Soon thereafter I arrived at the Battery, and there stood the true man—clutching himself, limping to and fro as though he had never ceased his nocturnal embrace and shuffle—awaiting my coming. He was perishingly cold; I half‑expected to see him collapse before my visage, succumbing to lethal chill. His eyes burned with a frightful hunger, so that when I placed the file in his hand and he set it upon the grass, it struck him that he might have endeavoured to devour it had he not espied my bundle. This time he did not invert me to seize my possessions; instead he allowed me to remain upright while I unfastened the bundle and turned out my pockets.

--- great_expectations | Coming of Age | Paragraph 35 ---
‘What lies within the flask, lad?’ he inquired.

--- great_expectations | Coming of Age | Paragraph 36 ---
He was already thrusting mincemeat down his gullet in a most peculiar fashion—resembling less a man partaking of a meal and more one hurriedly stowing victuals away—yet he paused to sip the liquor. He shuddered incessantly, so violently that maintaining the bottle’s neck between his teeth without biting it off was nearly the utmost he could manage.

--- great_expectations | Coming of Age | Paragraph 37 ---
‘I am largely of your mind, boy,’ he remarked.

--- great_expectations | Coming of Age | Paragraph 38 ---
‘Thank you, my lad,’ he said, ‘I do.’

--- great_expectations | Coming of Age | Paragraph 39 ---
I had frequently observed our large dog at his meals, and now I discerned a marked resemblance between his manner of eating and that of the man. The fellow seized his food with strong, sharp, sudden bites, akin to the canine’s snap. He gulped, or rather seized, each mouthful far too hastily; whilst eating he cast wary glances to left and right, as though danger lurked in every quarter, threatening to snatch the pie from him. His mind was so unsettled over the repast that he could not savour it at ease, nor endure a dining companion without threatening to snap his jaws at the visitor. In all these respects he bore a striking likeness to the dog.

--- great_expectations | Coming of Age | Paragraph 40 ---
‘The youth,’ he said, ‘the one of whom you spoke, the one concealed with you.’

--- great_expectations | Coming of Age | Paragraph 41 ---
He ceased his feeding and fixed upon me a gaze of the sharpest scrutiny mingled with utter astonishment.

--- great_expectations | Coming of Age | Paragraph 42 ---
‘Well, observe!’ he exclaimed. ‘When a man stands alone upon these flats, his head light, his stomach empty, perishing of cold and want, he hears naught through the night save the roar of guns and the summons of voices. He beholds the soldiers, their scarlet coats illuminated by the torches borne ahead, closing in upon him. He hears his number called, feels himself challenged, listens to the clatter of muskets, catches the orders “Make ready! Present! Cover him steady, men!” and yet is seized—only to find nothing. Why, if I witnessed a single pursuing party last night advancing in orderly ranks—damn them, with their tramp, tramp—I perceived a hundred. And as for the artillery! Why, I saw the mist tremble with the cannon’s roar, even after broad day—but this man,’ he continued, as though he had forgotten my presence, ‘did you discern aught in him?’

--- great_expectations | Coming of Age | Paragraph 43 ---
‘Not here?’ the man cried, striking his left cheek with merciless force using the flat of his palm.

--- great_expectations | Coming of Age | Paragraph 44 ---
‘Where is he?’ he demanded, stuffing the remnants of his fare into the breast of his grey jacket. ‘Point me to the path he took; I shall run him down as a bloodhound. Confound this iron upon my sore limb! Lad, surrender the file to me.’

--- great_expectations | Coming of Age | Paragraph 45 ---
I indicated the direction in which the mist had veiled the other man, and he glanced upward for a moment. Yet there he lay upon the soggy, rank grass, filing at his iron with the frenzy of a madman, heedless of me and of his own limb, which bore an old chafe and was bloody, yet he treated it as insensible as the file itself. My fear of him surged anew, now that he had plunged into this feverish haste, and I equally dreaded prolonging my absence from home. I informed him that I must depart, but he gave no heed; thus I deemed the wisest course to slip away. The final image I retained of him showed his head bowed over his knee, labouring intensely at his fetter, uttering impatient imprecations against it and against his leg. The last sound that reached my ears, as I paused in the mist to listen, was the relentless rasp of the file still at work.

--- great_expectations | Coming of Age | Paragraph 46 ---
Thus our portions were dispensed, as though we were two thousand soldiers on a forced march rather than a solitary man and boy at home; we swallowed gulps of milk and water from a jug upon the dresser, our faces apologetic. Meanwhile, Mrs. Joe hoisted spotless white curtains, affixed a fresh flowered flounce across the broad chimney to supplant the former, and laid bare the modest state parlour beyond the passage—a chamber never unveiled at any other season, which for the remainder of the year lay veiled in a cool haze of silver paper, even draping the four diminutive white crockery poodles upon the mantelpiece, each bearing a sable nose and a basket of blossoms in its jaws, each a mirror of its fellow. Mrs. Joe kept her house with immaculate precision, yet possessed a singular talent for rendering her cleanliness more irksome and intolerable than any grime. As the adage runs, cleanliness stands next to godliness, and certain souls pursue the same virtue through their faith.

--- great_expectations | Coming of Age | Paragraph 47 ---
Joe and I proceeding to church must, no doubt, have presented a touching sight to compassionate observers; yet the anguish I endured outwardly paled beside the turmoil I harboured within. The dread that seized me whenever Mrs. Joe approached the pantry or stepped from the room was matched only by the remorse that gnawed at my conscience for the deeds my hands had wrought. Beneath the burden of my iniquitous secret, I pondered whether the sanctuary of the Church could avail to shield me from the vengeance of the dreadful youth, should I confide therein. I imagined that the moment when the banns were proclaimed and the clergyman intoned, ‘Ye are now to declare it!’ would be the apt occasion for me to rise and seek a private audience in the vestry. I cannot assert with certainty that I would not have astonished our modest congregation by resorting to such an extreme, were it not for the fact that the day was Christmas, not a Sabbath.

--- great_expectations | Coming of Age | Paragraph 48 ---
‘Mrs. Joe,’ declared Uncle Pumblechook—a sizable, labor‑breathing, middle‑aged man of sluggish disposition, whose mouth resembled a fish’s gape, whose eyes stared dully, and whose sandy hair stood erect upon his crown, giving him the aspect of one just rescued from choking and now recovered—‘I have brought you the season’s compliments—I bear you, madam, a bottle of sherry, and likewise a bottle of port.’

--- great_expectations | Coming of Age | Paragraph 49 ---
Upon such occasions we took our repast in the kitchen, thereafter withdrawing to the parlour for nuts, oranges, and apples—a shift akin to Joe’s transmutation from his labour‑garb to his Sunday attire. My sister exhibited uncommon vivacity on this particular day, and indeed she was habitually more affable in the company of Mrs. Hubble than elsewhere. I retain the image of Mrs. Hubble as a petite, curly, sharp‑featured maiden clad in sky‑blue, occupying a conventionally youthful station owing to her marriage to Mr. Hubble—though I know not at what distant epoch—when she was considerably younger than he. Mr. Hubble I recollect as a rugged, broad‑shouldered, stooping elder, redolent of sawdust, whose legs lay extraordinarily asunder, so that in my youthful peregrinations I would often discern miles of open field stretching between us whenever I encountered him ascending the lane.

--- great_expectations | Coming of Age | Paragraph 50 ---

--- oliver_twist | Social Injustice | Paragraph 1 ---
Behold a striking testament to the tyranny of tailor’s work, this young Oliver Twist! Swathed in the solitary blanket that had hitherto constituted his entire wardrobe, he might have passed for the scion of a duke or the offspring of a mendicant; the proudest aristocrat would have stumbled in assigning the infant his proper niche in the great hierarchy of mankind. But once invested in the antiquated calico vestments, yellowed by generations of parish service, he was branded and catalogued, sinking instantly into his ordained groove—a parish foundling, the workhouse orphan, the lowly, half-famished drudge—destined to be knocked about and battered through the world’s thoroughfares, scorned by the multitude, unwept by any.

--- oliver_twist | Social Injustice | Paragraph 2 ---
The guardians of this establishment were profoundly sage, abysmally deep, and eminently philosophical gentlemen; and when they deigned to direct their intellects toward the workhouse, they instantly penetrated a mystery that would have baffled common understandings—the paupers actually *enjoyed* their residence! It served as a perpetual palace of diversion for the indigent; a tavern demanding no reckoning; a ceaseless round of breakfast, dinner, tea, and supper throughout the twelvemonth; a brick-and-mortar Elysium where labour was unknown and leisure absolute. “Ha!” cried the Board, with an air of profound penetration; “we are the very fellows to remedy this abuse; we shall put a stop to it forthwith.” Accordingly, they decreed that the destitute should possess the alternative—for they would compel no man, oh no—of perishing by inches within the walls, or by a swifter method without. With this benevolent aim, they negotiated with the water-works for an unbounded torrent; and with a corn-factor for periodic pittances of oatmeal; and ordained three daily meals of watery gruel, an onion bi-weekly, and half a roll on Sundays. They enacted a multitude of other wise and tender ordinances concerning the female sex, which decorum forbids rehearsing; they charitably undertook to sunder married couples, sparing them the ruinous expense of a suit in Doctors’ Commons; and, instead of enforcing a husband’s duty to maintain his family, as heretofore, they relieved him of his encumbrances and restored him to the blessings of celibacy! One shudders to think what crowds of claimants these two latter clauses might have summoned from every stratum of society, had they not been yoked to the workhouse and the gruel; but the Board were far-sighted men, and had forestalled the difficulty. Relief was made inseparable from the House and the porridge; and that prospect frightened the applicants away.

--- oliver_twist | Social Injustice | Paragraph 3 ---
Let not the adversaries of “the system” imagine that, during the term of his solitary confinement, Oliver was deprived of the salutary benefits of exercise, the solace of society, or the comforts of spiritual consolation. Regarding exercise: the weather being sharp and frosty, he was permitted to perform his morning ablutions beneath the pump in a stone courtyard, under the superintendence of Mr. Bumble, who prevented a chill from settling on his frame by the frequent application of the cane, causing a most salutary tingling to diffuse itself through his extremities. Regarding society: he was conveyed every alternate day into the dining-hall where the boys assembled, and there sociably flagellated as a public spectacle and warning. And far from being denied religious privilege, he was kicked into the same apartment each evening at prayer-time, there to listen to, and derive comfort from, a general supplication of the boys, into which a special clause had been inserted by Board authority, wherein they petitioned to be rendered good, virtuous, contented, and obedient, and to be shielded from the sins and vices of Oliver Twist—whom the prayer explicitly designated as being under the exclusive patronage of the Powers of Darkness, and a direct manufacture of the Devil himself.

--- oliver_twist | Social Injustice | Paragraph 4 ---
Mr. Gamfield cast a sly, knowing glance at the visages encircling the table, and, detecting a grin upon every countenance, gradually expanded his own features into a smile. The compact was sealed. Mr. Bumble received immediate instructions that Oliver Twist and his indentures were to be presented before the magistrate for signature and ratification that very afternoon.

--- oliver_twist | Social Injustice | Paragraph 5 ---
Upon the road to the justice’s parlour, Mr. Bumble impressed upon Oliver that his sole duty was to wear an aspect of radiant felicity, and to reply, when the gentleman inquired if he desired apprenticeship, that he should like it exceedingly well; both which mandates Oliver undertook to observe, the more readily as Mr. Bumble seasoned the advice with a gentle intimation that failure in either particular would precipitate consequences too terrible to contemplate. Arrived at the office, he was closeted in a small apartment by himself, and adjured by Mr. Bumble to remain there until his return.

The next will be Unwin, and the next Vilkins. I have names prepared to the end of the alphabet, and all the way through again when we reach Z.”

--- oliver_twist | Social Injustice | Paragraph 10 ---
It could scarcely be anticipated that this method of husbandry would yield any very remarkable or luxuriant harvest. Oliver Twist’s ninth anniversary found him a pallid, attenuated child, somewhat stunted in growth, and decidedly meagre in girth. Yet nature—or inheritance—had planted a sturdy spirit in Oliver’s breast; it had enjoyed ample room for expansion, thanks to the spare diet of the establishment; and perhaps to this circumstance may be attributed the fact of his having any ninth birthday at all. Be that as it may, however, it *was* his ninth birthday; and he was celebrating it in the coal-cellar with a select party of two other young gentlemen, who, after sharing with him in a sound thrashing, had been incarcerated for the atrocious presumption of being hungry, when Mrs. Mann, the excellent matron of the establishment, was suddenly startled by the apparition of Mr. Bumble, the beadle, endeavouring to unfasten the wicket of the garden gate.

At this tremendous spectacle, Oliver began to weep most piteously: thinking, not unnaturally, that the Board must have determined to kill him for some useful purpose, or they never would have begun to fatten him in that fashion.

--- oliver_twist | Social Injustice | Paragraph 22 ---
For the next eight or ten months, Oliver was the victim of a systematic course of treachery and imposition. He was brought up by hand. The famished and destitute condition of the infant orphan was duly reported by the workhouse authorities to the parish authorities. The parish authorities inquired with dignity of the workhouse authorities whether there was no female then resident in “the house” capable of imparting to Oliver Twist the consolation and nourishment of which he stood in need. The workhouse authorities replied with humility that there was not. Upon this, the parish authorities magnanimously and humanely resolved that Oliver should be “farmed”—or, in other words, dispatched to a satellite workhouse some three miles distant, where twenty or thirty other juvenile offenders against the poor-laws rolled about the floor all day, without the inconvenience of too much food or too much clothing, under the parental superintendence of an elderly female, who received the culprits at and for the consideration of sevenpence-halfpenny per small head per week. Sevenpence-halfpenny’s worth per week is a good round diet for a child; a great deal may be procured for sevenpence-halfpenny, quite enough to overload its stomach and make it uncomfortable. The elderly female was a woman of wisdom and experience; she knew what was good for children; and she had a very accurate perception of what was good for herself. So she appropriated the lion’s share of the weekly stipend to her own use, and consigned the rising parochial generation to even a shorter allowance than was originally provided. Thereby discovering in the lowest depth a deeper still; and proving herself a very great experimental philosopher.

Among other public edifices in a certain town, which for many reasons it will be prudent to refrain from mentioning, and to which I shall assign no fictitious name, there is one anciently common to most towns, great or small: to wit, a workhouse; and in this workhouse was born—on a day and date which I need not trouble myself to repeat, inasmuch as it can be of no possible consequence to the reader, at this stage of the business at all events—the item of mortality whose name is prefixed to the head of this chapter.

Occasionally, when there was some more than usually interesting inquest upon a parish child who had been overlooked in turning up a bedstead, or inadvertently scalded to death when a washing happened to be taking place—though the latter accident was very scarce, anything approaching a washing being of rare occurrence in the farm—the jury would take it into their heads to ask troublesome questions, or the parishioners would rebelliously affix their signatures to a remonstrance. But these impertinences were speedily checked by the evidence of the surgeon and the testimony of the beadle; the former of whom had always opened the body and found nothing inside (which was very probable indeed), and the latter of whom invariably swore whatever the parish wanted; which was very self-devotional. Besides, the Board made periodical pilgrimages to the farm, and always sent the beadle the day before to say they were going. The children were neat and clean to behold when *they* went; and what more would the people have!
