--- oliver_twist | Social Injustice | Paragraph 38 ---
“You needn’t mind sending up to me if the child cries, nurse,” said the surgeon, donning his gloves with great deliberation. “It’s very likely it *will* be troublesome. Give it a little gruel if it is.” He put on his hat, and, pausing by the bedside on his way to the door, added, “She was a good-looking girl too; where did she come from?”

--- oliver_twist | Social Injustice | Paragraph 39 ---
“Now don’t you be offended at what I’m a-going to say,” observed Mrs. Mann with captivating sweetness. “You’ve had a long walk, you know, or I wouldn’t mention it. Now, will you take a little drop of somethink, Mr. Bumble?”

--- oliver_twist | Social Injustice | Paragraph 40 ---
“Will you go along with me, Oliver?” said Mr. Bumble in a majestic voice.

--- oliver_twist | Social Injustice | Paragraph 41 ---
As I purpose to show in the sequel whether the white-waistcoated gentleman was right or not, I should perhaps mar the interest of this narrative (supposing it to possess any at all) if I ventured to hint just yet whether the life of Oliver Twist had this violent termination or no.

--- oliver_twist | Social Injustice | Paragraph 42 ---
“This here boy, sir, wot the parish wants to ’prentis,” said Mr. Gamfield.

--- oliver_twist | Social Injustice | Paragraph 43 ---
“Why, you’re quite a literary character, sir!” said Mrs. Mann.

--- oliver_twist | Social Injustice | Paragraph 44 ---
The donkey was in a state of profound abstraction: wondering, probably, whether he was destined to be regaled with a cabbage-stalk or two when he had disposed of the two sacks of soot with which the little cart was laden; so, without noticing the word of command, he jogged onward.

--- oliver_twist | Social Injustice | Paragraph 45 ---
“It’s a nasty trade,” said Mr. Limbkins, when Gamfield had again stated his wish.

--- oliver_twist | Social Injustice | Paragraph 46 ---
“Well! You have come here to be educated and taught a useful trade,” said the red-faced gentleman in the high chair.

--- oliver_twist | Social Injustice | Paragraph 47 ---
The beadle drew himself up with great pride and said, “I inwented it.”

--- oliver_twist | Social Injustice | Paragraph 48 ---
For the combination of both these blessings in the one simple process of picking oakum, Oliver bowed low by direction of the beadle, and was then hurried away to a large ward; where, on a rough, hard bed, he sobbed himself to sleep. What a novel illustration of the tender laws of England! They let the paupers go to sleep!

--- oliver_twist | Social Injustice | Paragraph 49 ---
“I’ll fetch him directly,” said Mrs. Mann, leaving the room for that purpose. Oliver, having had by this time as much of the outer coat of dirt which encrusted his face and hands removed as could be scrubbed off in one washing, was led into the room by his benevolent protectress.

--- oliver_twist | Social Injustice | Paragraph 50 ---
“I’m sure, Mr. Bumble, that I was only a-telling one or two of the dear children as is so fond of you that it was you a-coming,” replied Mrs. Mann with great humility.

--- oliver_twist | Innocence | Paragraph 1 ---
In the shadowed nook of temporal existence, where sorrow and misfortune loom like specters, this infant, so recently ushered into the world by the parish surgeon’s trembling hands, endured for an epoch a suspense so profound that even the prospect of bearing a name ere one’s expiry seemed a whimsical dream. Had such a fate befallen the child, these lines—alas, the whole elaborate chronicle—might never have graced the written world, or, should they by miracle attain the page, would condense into a parable so terse it would rival the brevity of any biographical sketch in all annals.

--- oliver_twist | Innocence | Paragraph 2 ---
As the infant’s lungs, in their first crude symphony of life, burst forth with a cry that rent the stillness, the patchwork covering cast over the bed’s cold iron frame rustled like a whispered warning. A wraithlike silhouette emerged from its shadow, a figure both spectral and vital, as a voice—frail as a leaf in a storm—rustled, “*Let me see the child, and die.*”

--- oliver_twist | Innocence | Paragraph 3 ---
This expected solace, a beacon of maternal hope, instead soaked through the veil of melancholy that enveloped the mother’s countenance. Her hollowed features, set in the act of reaching, bore no warmth but a plea etched in desperation, her hand outstretched as if to grasp the very essence of life from Oliver’s frail form.

--- oliver_twist | Innocence | Paragraph 4 ---
“*Poor dear!*” cried the nurse, her voice a tremulous chorus as she cradled the child with trembling dexterity. In her hand, the cork of a vial—glistening with an unnameable green elixir—slipped from its place, a relic of care or neglect, both possible. “Poor dear!” she murmured again, as though the word itself might temper the boy’s fate.

--- oliver_twist | Innocence | Paragraph 5 ---
The surgeon, with a deliberateness bordering on theatricality, donned gloves as though handling a sacred relic. “*Fear not,*” he intoned, his tone a blend of boundary and belated comfort. “*A potion may be warranted,*” he added, gesturing to a bowl of gruel. Clad in his hat, he lingered at the bedside, his gaze lingering on the nurse. “Ah! A visage fair, though now obscured by straits. Pray, from what low abode did she hail?”

--- oliver_twist | Innocence | Paragraph 6 ---
He lifted Oliver’s hand, as if to touch the source of that fistful of primal energy surging within. “*The tale of old*”—he declared, his brow furrowed in mock solemnity—“*the tale of bitter truth*.” With a nod to the roof, he bid adieu, his parting words a dirge for dreams yet unborn.

--- oliver_twist | Innocence | Paragraph 7 ---
Clad in the yellowed remnants of a blanket once deemed sufficient for a king or a pauper alike, Oliver Twist became a riddle in stark raiment. Were he an heir or a beggar? A scholar or a vagabond? Yet, when ensheathed in the yellow calico that had once been its master’s wife’s calico, he was catalogued—yellow ticket, yellow count, yellow destiny—a parish foundling, an orphan of the workhouse, a shadow deprived of all mirth, consumed by fists and sneers, utterly ignored by wrathful hearts and shunned by pitying eyes.

--- oliver_twist | Innocence | Paragraph 8 ---
Oliver’s lamentations were not mere tears but a tempest of anguish. Had he known, in that very moment, that he was a orphan cast adrift by the wolves of ecclesiastical and civil custodians, might not his cries have torn the moon into fragments?

--- oliver_twist | Innocence | Paragraph 9 ---
For eight long months, Oliver was subjected to the artful machinations of cruelty disguised as pastoral care. Led by the woman who presided over the farm—a woman whose wisdom was as sharp as her avarice—he was fed not for his good, but for her own. She siphoned the meager stipend meant for his sustenance, reducing his allowance to a pittance that barely satiated his hunger. Her system, a farce of faith in human resilience, saw the child’s constitution buckle at the seams. Too often, he succumbed to cold, fire, or accident—a victim of her own miscalculations, each death a lesson in futility, yet the cycle repeated with grim inevitability.

--- oliver_twist | Innocence | Paragraph 10 ---
Mark this as yet another instance of learned folly, akin to the tale of a man who taught a horse to subsist on straw alone, only to meet his demise before testing the theory. Alas, the woman’s “experience” mirrored this folly: she believed she could nourish the child with bread thinner than air, and eight times out of ten, she found the boy languishing on the threshold of death—either from hunger, neglect, or some sudden mischance. In the end, the offspring of this experiment rarely survived to speak of their trials.

--- oliver_twist | Innocence | Paragraph 11 ---
The parishioners, long accustomed to the squalor, nonetheless grumbled when suspicious inquiries arose during inquests orunsigned remonstrances gathered like vines. Yet the surgeon, with his scalpel’s cold certainty, and the beadle, whose loyalty was as unyielding as his spectacles, swiftly squelched dissent. Their witness was a masterclass in evasion: the former declared the body empty as proof of innocence, while the latter swore to whatever the board demanded, for faith in the cause was his sole currency.

--- oliver_twist | Innocence | Paragraph 12 ---
On Oliver’s ninth birthday, he appeared as a specter of a child—pale, slight, and marked by deprivation. Yet within his hollow frame burned a fire of resilience, kindled by scarcity and strengthened by the meager fare of the farm. His life, though, was a gauntlet of privation, yet he endured.

--- oliver_twist | Innocence | Paragraph 13 ---
Mr. Bumble, the beadle, accompanied by his leather-bound ledger, grumbled through the ritual of age-announcing. “*Oliver Twist, the half-baptized, nine years old today,*” he intoned, his voice a mix of formality and malice.

--- oliver_twist | Innocence | Paragraph 14 ---
Oliver’s tears were not for his exile but for the friends he left behind—the only warmth in a world of ice. His hunger was a visible pallor, his voice a tremor of need as he clutched the bread Mrs. Mann had finally given him.

--- oliver_twist | Innocence | Paragraph 15 ---
A gentleman, seated in a vanity-laden chair, addressed Oliver with a air of condescension. “What is thy name, lad?” he inquired, his tone prickling with disdain.

--- oliver_twist | Innocence | Paragraph 16 ---
To which Oliver, at a loss for the language of self, replied only with his name—a sound as foreign as the concept of kinship.

--- oliver_twist | Innocence | Paragraph 17 ---
The gentleman in white, now growling, dismissed the boy as a fool. “Thou art a paragon of insensibility,” he spat, his tone a blade.

--- oliver_twist | Innocence | Paragraph 18 ---
“*Wasst du weinend?*” he demanded, his bewilderment palpable. “What ails thee?”

--- oliver_twist | Innocence | Paragraph 19 ---
Oliver, unversed in the verbs of faith, confessed nothing but his despair. Had he known of charity, he might have entreated for the bread he craved. But he had been taught only the arithmetic of hunger.

--- oliver_twist | Innocence | Paragraph 20 ---
The bowl of gruel, though scant, never lacked sustenance. The boys polished it with zeal, their fingers coated in cereal, their eyes fixed on the copper as if it held the secret to their survival. Their bellies, though, sang a dirge of want.

--- oliver_twist | Innocence | Paragraph 21 ---
A boy, tall and viciously hungry, threatened to devour his companion unless more gruel were served. The others listened, their eyes Hungry as wolves. Oliver, summoned to plead, rose with a desperation that bordered on madness.

--- oliver_twist | Innocence | Paragraph 22 ---
A gentleman in white, observing this farce, muttered to himself, “*This boy will hang.*” His words, though unspoken, hung heavy in the air.

--- oliver_twist | Innocence | Paragraph 23 ---
The next morning, he swore this conviction was no idle fancy. “I swear,” he declared, his voiceNow a mantra of doom, “that boy will meet the gallows.”

--- oliver_twist | Innocence | Paragraph 24 ---
Let it be known that Oliver was not wholly deprived. Granted, he was flogged as a public spectacle, his existence a parade of toil. Yet he had fleeting company at meal times and the hollow rituals of prayer, where the other boys collectively petitioned the heavens to guard them from Oliver’s imagined malice—a supplication laced with irony.

--- oliver_twist | Innocence | Paragraph 25 ---
The gentleman in white, having delivered a sermon to the board, now turned his gaze to Mr. Gamfield, a man whose very name seemed to echo with promise. Gamfield, a man of peculiar miserliness and ambition, saw value in Oliver as a specimen for his chimney-sweeping business.

--- oliver_twist | Innocence | Paragraph 26 ---
"*This_or_that boy*,” Gamfield declared, his voice a rumble, “is the parish’s offer.”

--- oliver_twist | Innocence | Paragraph 27 ---
"*A prentice*,” he proposed, lowering his voice with a flourish. “*In a chimney-sweeping firm, where the boy may learn the arts of soot and steam.*”

--- oliver_twist | Innocence | Paragraph 28 ---
Gamfield explained his reasoning with a grandness fit for a madman. “*The chimneys must burn*,” he insisted. “*A boy who seeks to flee the flame will learn to stay. A boy who learns to sweat shall grip the soot like a thief.*”

--- oliver_twist | Innocence | Paragraph 29 ---
"*Four pounds*,” Gamfield concluded, his hand slapping the ledger. “*Not a penny more.*”

--- oliver_twist | Innocence | Paragraph 30 ---
The gentleman in white scoffed. “*Nonsense!*” he roared. “*He’ll starve you to death for nothing!*” Yet his laughter rang with a strange glee as he sealed the contract.

--- oliver_twist | Innocence | Paragraph 31 ---
Oliver, trembling under the weight of his new fate, whispered “*A prentice?*”

--- oliver_twist | Innocence | Paragraph 32 ---
Mr. Bumble, ever the moralizer, intoned, “*A born of the parish, Oliver! A servant of the people!*”

--- oliver_twist | Innocence | Paragraph 33 ---
Oliver’s sobs were a storm, his heart a drum of betrayal.

--- oliver_twist | Innocence | Paragraph 34 ---
The master, a man of ordinary health, turned ashen. The boys, frozen in horror, clustered around the copper as though it might shield them.

--- oliver_twist | Innocence | Paragraph 35 ---
“*This is the boy,*” Mr. Bumble proclaimed, his voice a thunderclap.

--- oliver_twist | Innocence | Paragraph 36 ---
The old gentleman, emerging from his newspaper, queried, “*Ah? Is this the dream child?*”

--- oliver_twist | Innocence | Paragraph 37 ---
Mr. Bumble, in a tone meant to awe, intoned, “*Oliver, be proud. Thou art a man’s ward now!*”

--- oliver_twist | Innocence | Paragraph 38 ---
The chamber fell silent, save for the clatter of the ladle as the master’s rage quelled.

--- oliver_twist | Innocence | Paragraph 39 ---
Gamfield, with a sharpness of purpose, asked, “*No siree, boy. Yer an orphan, aye?*”

--- oliver_twist | Innocence | Paragraph 40 ---
And so began the third chapter, where Oliver’s next trial lay in the realm of chance—a place not of comfort, but of peril.

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 1 ---
“Ah, poor soul, it is indeed!” exclaimed the nurse, bending to clutch the child, as she retrieved the cork of the green bottle that had rolled upon the pillow. “Poor soul!”

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 2 ---
“She was conveyed hither last night,” replied the ancient woman, “by the overseer’s edict. She had lain in the street, her feet worn to rags after a long trudge, yet whence she came or whither she was bound no mortal could divine.”

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 3 ---
An admirable illustration of the sway wielded by raiment, young Oliver Twist was! Enveloped in the blanket that had hitherto shielded him, he might have been the scion of a noble line or a destitute wretch; it would have been no easy task for the most haughty stranger to assign him his true station. Yet now, swathed in the faded calico of the workhouse, he bore the badge of his condition—an orphan of the parish, a gaunt, half‑starved drudge—doomed to be cuffed and buffeted through life, scorned by all and pitied by none.

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 4 ---
For eight or ten months Oliver endured a relentless course of imposture and oppression. He was nurtured by hand, the infant’s pitiful condition reported by the workhouse to the parish, which, with dignified inquiry, asked whether any woman of the house could furnish the nourishment he required. The workhouse answered in humility that none existed. Thereupon the parish, with magnanimous resolve, “farmed” the child, sending him three miles off to a branch‑workhouse where twenty or thirty juvenile offenders rolled about the floor, feasting on the scant allowance of a workhouse matron who received but a half‑penny per head each week. A half‑penny’s pittance formed a most generous diet for a child—enough to overfill his stomach and render him uncomfortable. The matron, versed in the ways of prudence, appropriated most of the stipend to her own use, consigning the rising generation to an even slimmer ration. Thus she descended to a still lower depth, revealing herself as a most adept experimental philosopher.

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 5 ---
It can scarce be expected that such a system of farming should yield any extraordinary or luxuriant harvest. Oliver’s ninth birthday revealed him a pallid, gaunt child, diminutive of stature and slender of girth. Yet nature had implanted a sturdy spirit within his breast, and the spare diet of the establishment afforded him ample room to grow, perhaps explaining why he survived to see his ninth birthday at all. Be that as it may, on that very day he was celebrating it in the coal‑cellar, accompanied by two other youngsters who, after a sound thrashing, had been locked up for presuming to be hungry when Mrs. Mann, the matron, was startlingly startled by the appearance of Mr. Bumble, the beadle, attempting to open the garden‑gate.

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 6 ---
“Will she accompany me?” the wretched Oliver inquired.

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 7 ---
That offered little solace to the child, though his youth endowed him with enough wit to feign deep regret at his departure. It was no great feat for the boy to summon tears; hunger and recent ill‑usage were puissant promoters of weeping, and Oliver wept most naturally. Mrs. Mann enfolded him in a thousand embraces, and, what he craved above all, a morsel of bread and butter, lest his hunger appear too great upon his arrival at the workhouse. With a slice of bread clutched in his hand and the parish cap of brown cloth perched upon his head, Oliver was led away by Mr. Bumble from the wretched home where no kind word or glance had ever brightened the gloom of his infant years. Yet he burst into an agony of childish grief as the cottage‑gate shut behind him. Though the companions of misery he left behind were wretched enough, they were the only friends he had ever known; and a sudden ache of loneliness pierced his heart for the first time.

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 8 ---
“What is that, sir?” whispered the poor child.

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 9 ---
Poor Oliver! He little imagined, as he lay in a blissful ignorance of all that surrounded him, that the board had, that very day, resolved upon a decision which would determine the very course of his future fortunes. And so it was.

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 10 ---
The members of this board were sage, deep‑minded, philosophical men; and when they turned their attention to the workhouse they uncovered at once what ordinary minds could not grasp—the poor delighted in it! It was a veritable place of public amusement for the lower classes; a tavern where no charge was demanded; a public establishment offering breakfast, dinner, tea and supper throughout the year—a brick‑and‑mortar Elysium wherein all was play and no labour. “Ah!” said the board, assuming a knowing air; “we are the very persons to set this to rights; we shall put an end to it forthwith.” Accordingly they enacted a rule that every poor soul should have the alternative—Surely not to compel any—of being starved by a gradual process within the house, or by a swift one without it. To that end they contracted with the water‑works for an unlimited supply, with a corn‑factory for a periodic dribble of oatmeal, and issued three thin gruel meals a day, an onion twice a week, and half a roll on Sundays. They promulgated other wise and humane regulations concerning the ladies, which need not be recounted; they undertook to divorce poor married persons, in consequence of the expense of a suit in Doctors’ Commons, and, instead of obliging a man to support his family, they removed his family from him and made him a bachelor! It is hard to say how many applicants for relief, under these two heads, might have risen in all ranks of society, had they not been coupled with the workhouse; but the board, being far‑seeing, provided against this. Relief was inseparable from the workhouse and the gruel, and that very circumstance frightened the populace.

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 11 ---
The hall in which the boys took their meals was a vast stone chamber, at one end of which stood a copper kettle. From this the master, clothed in an apron, and assisted by one or two women, ladled out the gruel at mealtimes. Each boy received a single porringer, and no more—except on great public occasions when he might obtain two ounces and a quarter of bread besides. The bowls never required washing; the boys polished them with their spoons till they shone, an operation which, owing to the size of the spoons, never consumed much time. Meanwhile they stared at the copper with eager eyes, as though they could have devoured the very bricks of which it was made, sucking their fingers most assiduously in order to snatch any stray splashes of gruel that might have spattered thereon. Boys generally possess hearty appetites; Oliver Twist and his companions suffered the tortures of slow starvation for three months. At last they grew so voracious that one tall boy, unused to such fare (his father having kept a small cook‑shop), hinted darkly that unless he received another basin of gruel each day he feared he might some night eat the boy who slept next to him, a weakly youth of tender age. He bore a wild, hungry eye, and the others believed him. A council was called; lots were cast to determine who should approach the master after supper and request more; the lot fell to Oliver Twist.

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 12 ---
It chanced one morning, while Oliver’s affairs were in this fortunate and comfortable state, that Mr. Gamfield, chimney‑sweep, traversed the High Street, pondering his means of discharging certain rent arrears that his landlord was pressing for. Mr. Gamfield’s most optimistic estimate fell short by full five pounds of the required sum; in a species of arithmetical desperation he alternately pummelled his brains and his donkey, when passing the workhouse his eye fell upon the bill upon the gate.

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 13 ---
“What shall we give, gentlemen? Come! Spare not a poor man!” cried the sweep.

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 14 ---
As Mr. Bumble paused to draw breath after delivering his address in an awful tone, the tears rolled down the child’s cheek and he sobbed bitterly.

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 15 ---
The elderly gentleman who was reading the newspaper raised his head for a moment, and tugged at the sleeve of the other elder, thereby causing the latter to awaken.

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 16 ---
Unacquainted with the precise nature of a “live board,” Oliver was somewhat astonished by this revelation and could not decide whether to laugh or weep. Before he could ponder further, Mr. Bumble tapped him upon the head with his cane to rouse him, then gave him another pat on the back to set him moving, and, bidding him follow, led him into a large, white‑washed room where eight or ten corpulent gentlemen sat round a table. At the head of the table, seated in a high‑backed arm‑chair, was a particularly rotund gentleman of a most flushed complexion.

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 17 ---
“Not a farthing more,” replied Mr. Limbkins firmly.

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 18 ---
“Why, you are quite a literary character, sir!” exclaimed Mrs. Mann.

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 19 ---
“If the parish vould like him to learn a right pleasant trade, in a good ’spectable chimbley‑sweepin’ business,” said Mr. Gamfield, “I seek an apprentice, and I am ready to take him.”

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 20 ---
“Well! You have come here to be instructed, and taught a useful trade,” said the red‑faced gentleman in the high‑chair.

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 21 ---
Though this invitation was accompanied by a curtsey that might have softened the heart of a churchwarden, it by no means softened the beadle’s resolve.

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 22 ---
Oliver had not been within the workhouse walls a quarter of an hour, and had scarcely finished devouring a second slice of bread, when Mr. Bumble, who had delivered him to the care of an elderly woman, returned; and, informing him that it was a “board night,” announced that the board desired his immediate appearance before it.

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 23 ---
As I intend to demonstrate in the sequel whether the gentleman in the white waistcoat was justified, I should perhaps detract from the interest of this narrative—assuming it possesses any interest at all—if I were to hint at this juncture whether the life of Oliver Twist met a violent end or not.

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 24 ---
“Wooo‑ooo!” cried Mr. Gamfield to the donkey.

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 25 ---
“Mr. Limbkins, I beg your pardon, sir! Oliver Twist has asked for more!”

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 26 ---
At length the whispers ceased; the board members, having resumed their seats and solemnity, heard Mr. Limbkins speak:

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 27 ---
The elderly gentleman with spectacles gradually dozed off over the scrap of parchment, and a brief silence followed after Oliver had been placed by Mr. Bumble before the desk.

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 28 ---
“Why, it is what I keep a little of in the house to put into the blessed infants’ Daffy when they are not well, Mr. Bumble,” replied Mrs. Mann, as she opened a corner cupboard and withdrew a bottle and glass. “It is gin. I shall not deceive you, Mr. B. It is gin.”

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 29 ---
Mr. Gamfield’s countenance brightened, and, with a swift step, he returned to the table and said…

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 30 ---
CHAPTER III.  
RELATES HOW OLIVER TWIST WAS VERY NEAR GETTING A PLACE WHICH WOULD NOT HAVE BEEN A SINECURE

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 31 ---
“Pooh! pooh! nonsense!” declared the gentleman in the white waistcoat. “He would be cheap at nothing at all, as a premium. Take him, you silly fellow! He is just the boy for you. He wants the stick now and then; it will do him good; and his board need not be costly, for he has never been overfed since his birth. Ha! ha! ha!”

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 32 ---
Oliver was on the point of assenting to go with anyone who would take him willingly, when, looking upward, he caught sight of Mrs. Mann, who had positioned herself behind the beadle’s chair and was brandishing her fist at him with a ferocious countenance. He instantly took the hint, for that fist had often been impressed upon his body and was therefore deeply imprinted upon his memory.

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 33 ---
Mrs. Mann ushered the beadle into a small parlour with a brick floor; she set a seat for him; and, with officious propriety, placed his cocked hat and cane upon the table before him. Mr. Bumble wiped the perspiration from his brow, cast a complacent glance at the cocked hat, and smiled—yes, he smiled. Beadles are but men, and Mr. Bumble smiled.

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 34 ---
“Hush!” said the gentleman who had spoken first. “You know you have no father or mother, and that you were brought up by the parish, do you not?”

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 35 ---
“Not a drop. Nor a drop,” said Mr. Bumble, waving his right hand in a dignified yet placid manner.

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 36 ---
“Young boys have been smothered in chimneys before now,” remarked another gentleman.

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 37 ---
“I should say, three pounds ten was a generous sum,” observed Mr. Limbkins.

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 38 ---
“This here boy, sir, wot the parish wants to ‘prentis,” said Mr. Gamfield.

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 39 ---
“No,” replied Mr. Limbkins; “at least, as it is a vile trade, we think you ought to receive something less than the premium we offered.”

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 40 ---
Mr. Bumble coughed.

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 41 ---
CHAPTER II.  
TREATS OF OLIVER TWIST’S GROWTH, EDUCATION, AND BOARD

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 42 ---
“I am sure, Mr. Bumble, that I was only telling one or two of the dear children who are so fond of you, that it was you a‑coming,” replied Mrs. Mann with great humility.

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 43 ---
“And now about business,” said the beadle, producing a leather pocket‑book. “The child, half‑baptized Oliver Twist, is nine years old today.”

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 44 ---
“No, she cannot,” replied Mr. Bumble. “But she will come and see you sometimes.”

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 45 ---
The gentleman in the white waistcoat looked considerably amused by this explanation; but his mirth was quickly quenched by a look from Mr. Limbkins. The board then fell to muttering among themselves for a few minutes, in such a low tone that only words such as “saving of expenditure,” “looked well in the accounts,” “have a printed report published” were audible. These phrases, repeated with great emphasis, happened to be heard.

--- oliver_twist | Poverty and the Lower Class | Paragraph 46 ---
“You need not trouble yourself with sending up to me, nurse, if the child cries,” said the surgeon, putting on his gloves with deliberate care. “It is very likely it will be troublesome. --- oliver_twist | Institutional Cruelty | Paragraph 1 ---
In a certain unspecified town, which it would be both wise and discreet to abstain from naming, and to which this narrative shall assign no false or imaginary title, there lies a venerable establishment common even to the humblest of hamlets: a workhouse. And within this hallowed den of destitution, on a date and hour of no particular import to the sagas of mankind, was born that most inauspicious of mortal specimens, whose name graces the opening of this chronicle.

--- oliver_twist | Institutional Cruelty | Paragraph 2 ---
For a prolonged period subsequent to his introduction into a world replete with sorrow and privation, administered by the parish surgeon, his prospects of survival hung in the balance, as though fate itself debated whether his brief entrance into the theatre of life deserved the honour of a name. Had he perished at birth, these annals would never have been penned; or, should they exist, they would merit naught but the distinction of being the shortest and most faithful biography ever committed to parchment in any age or land.

--- oliver_twist | Institutional Cruelty | Paragraph 3 ---
While I confess that to be born in a workhouse is rarely the crown of earthly fortune, in this singular instance, it proved the most beneficial stroke of fate for our protagonist. The fact remains that he struggled mightily to adopt the art of breathing—a feat rendered necessary, as custom demands, even unto mortality; yet for an extended interval, he lay suspended betwixt life and oblivion upon a paltry flock-stuff cushion. Had there been vigilant grandmothers, anxious aunts, or sage physicians to oversee his demise, he would have followed the path of all the preceding infants of his station. But none were present save an inebriated pauper woman and a surgeon motivated solely by contract. Thus, Oliver Twist and Nature clashed in earnest. At last, he exhaled, sneezed, and cried with a volume that eclipsed the wail of any infant thus newly come into the world.

--- oliver_twist | Institutional Cruelty | Paragraph 4 ---
What a striking illustration of the transformative power of attire is young Oliver Twist! Wrapped in his sole covering—a humble blanket—he might have been mistaken for the offspring of royalty or highwayman indecipherable to the keenest observer. But clad in the official garb of the workhouse, those abject yellow calico robes that had long since betrayed their owners to despair, he became instantly recognisable: a cursed parish child, an orphaned drudge, a wretch bowed beneath the yoke of servitude, to be whipped and scorned through the labyrinths of existence, pitied by none and feared by all.

--- oliver_twist | Institutional Cruelty | Paragraph 5 ---
CHAPTER II: THE TRAJECTORY OF OLIVER TWIST’S MATURATION, INSTRUCTION, AND NUTRITION ALLOCATION

--- oliver_twist | Institutional Cruelty | Paragraph 6 ---
For an octennium of ciceronian folly, Oliver was subjected to a regimen of perfidy and guile. Reared by the rod, his youthful resentment was duly reported by the administrators of the workhouse to the overseeing magistrates. These latter, addressing their superiors with austere inquiry, demanded whether a female of any standing resided within the house to nourish and solace the orphan. The reply was as evasive as the winds: no such woman existed. Whereupon, the authorities, with magnanimous benevolence, decreed that Oliver be “farmed”—that is, dispatched to an extramural institution wherein a cadre of juvenile offenders, numbering some twenty or thirty, were left to gnaw their fillings all day without the encumbrance of sustenance or raiment, under the tutelage of an elderly dame whose compensation per capita was a paltry sevenpence and a halfpenny weekly. Such a pittance, one might remark, could amply sate a child’s gut and more, yet this virago, ever a pragmatist of effrontery, pocketed the lion’s share, leaving her charges with even scantier rations than before.

--- oliver_twist | Institutional Cruelty | Paragraph 7 ---
Though infrequently, when probes into the untoward fate of some forsaken parishioner—perhaps slain by a spilled pail of scalding water, or crushed beneath a bedstead’s wrath—gave rise to the prurient curiosity of juries, or the defiant scrawls of dissenters, these impertinences were swiftly quelled. The surgeon’s autopsy invariably revealed an interior emptier than a pauper’s purse, while the beadle’s testimony, as ever, conformed to the parish’s desires. Pilgrimages by the board to inspect their charges were likewise staged, the children polished as though for inspection, while Mr. Bumble, having forewarned them of their approach, saw to it that order reigned.

--- oliver_twist | Institutional Cruelty | Paragraph 8 ---
“Permissionable, Madam?” inquired Mr. Bumble, cane aloft, addressing the dame. “Does this comport with decorum—that you leave the parish officers waiting at your dock when they attend to matters of state?”

--- oliver_twist | Institutional Cruelty | Paragraph 9 ---
“Nay,” observed Mrs. Mann, perching her goblet. “You possess the virtues of Mother Teresa.”

--- oliver_twist | Institutional Cruelty | Paragraph 10 ---
“And despite the offer of twenty guineas,” continued the beadle, “we’ve made no progress in discerning the parentage of this accursed waif. Not a shadow of his sire nor his mother’s provenance do we possess.”

--- oliver_twist | Institutional Cruelty | Paragraph 11 ---
“Indeed,” replied Mrs. Mann, “it may be you yourself.” With a final slurp of his aqua vitae, Mr. Bumble declared, “Oliver is now mature, madam, and must return to the mother house. I am come personally to escort him thither.”

--- oliver_twist | Institutional Cruelty | Paragraph 12 ---
This proved no consolation to the lad. Though young, he suspected far more than met the eye. With the guile of a seasoned actor, he wept piteously, Mrs. Mann hurrying to arm him with a loaf of bread ere the procession began. But as the iron gates closed behind him, a profound isolation gripped his breast.

--- oliver_twist | Institutional Cruelty | Paragraph 13 ---
No sooner had he traversed the threshold of the workhouse than Mr. Bumble returned, bearing word that a board had convened. “Hark! A session of judgment is underfoot,” he intoned, seizing Oliver by the collar and marching him into a whitewashed chamber where a ring of corpulent gentlemen lounged, their faces all barts and “honorary.”

--- oliver_twist | Institutional Cruelty | Paragraph 14 ---
“Stand and observe,” commanded the beadle. Oliver, bewildered, bowed to the table in their stead.

--- oliver_twist | Institutional Cruelty | Paragraph 15 ---
“Behold this disgraceful spectacle,” the rotund magistrate declared. “He is nameless, parentless, a creature of the parish’s charity. Assemble your noises and prostrations, wretched insect.”

--- oliver_twist | Institutional Cruelty | Paragraph 16 ---
Here the board, in their sagacity, had resolved that the paupers cherished their lot too soundly. For what utility was a forsake if not to endure mild suffering? Thus, they instituted a dual tyranny: starve within the workhouse, or starve forth—oh, the glories of their ingenuity! They contracted with plumbers and grocers to furnish oatmeal and water, and doled out thin pottage three times daily, with stale onions and communion rolls on Sundays. They also instituted divorce proceedings without recourse, and severed familial bonds with surgical precision. Yet their edict was clear: all relief demanded submission to the workhouse, and the thought thereof curdled the blood of all poorfolk.

--- oliver_twist | Institutional Cruelty | Paragraph 17 ---
For half an annum, the system thrived. Yet the coffers groaned beneath the weight of coffins—beneath the sway of undertakers—and the paupers dwindled in number. The board sighed, their hearts light.

--- oliver_twist | Institutional Cruelty | Paragraph 18 ---
The hall where the boys were fattened resembled a cathedral of misery. At its apex stood the master—apron-clad, ladle in hand—administering their meagre rations. The bowls gleamed under the meagre light, polished by lips sodden with hunger. The sight flickered in their eyes; they gnawed fingers for crumbs and eyed the copper as if it held the seeds of salvation. Oliver and his kin marketh three months of this purgatory—forsooth, they devoured the pottage with such hungfulness that one boy, ever bold, whispered of the perils of starvation, assuring them he would sooner cannibalise his neighbour than endure another day.

--- oliver_twist | Institutional Cruelty | Paragraph 19 ---
At last, the boy who would petition the master most boldly among them elected himself emissary of the plea. With basin and spoon quivering in hand, he advanced and bespoke the master with a demand: “Mightier fare”—

--- oliver_twist | Institutional Cruelty | Paragraph 20 ---
The master, a dolorous gargantua, pallid as a corpse, recoiled. He brandished his ladle like a scepter, bellowed for the beadle, and Oliver was seized and thrust into a dungeon of his own making.

--- oliver_twist | Institutional Cruelty | Paragraph 21 ---
Thus confined, Oliver endured the trial of isolation, save for daily rituals of water from the pump, under Mr. Bumble’s watchful cane. Yet no priest nor companion was granted. Even prayers were perverted to expel his “vices,” as though the boy hosted the Devil’s own accoutrement.

--- oliver_twist | Institutional Cruelty | Paragraph 22 ---
Then came Mr. Gamfield, a chimney sweep, whose finances were as perilous as his tempers. Spied upon the bill for five pounds—a reprieve—and interest sparkled in his malevolent eye. Toting donkey-in-hand, he bargained for the lad, assured the workhouse of his expertise in “chimneys,” and carried off his prize like a farmer his newest colt.

--- oliver_twist | Institutional Cruelty | Paragraph 23 ---
The board, after pondering Gamfield’s “previous inclinations” (a euphemism for murder), resolved that five pounds was a bargain, and noted the steward’s relief at shedding a burden.

--- oliver_twist | Institutional Cruelty | Paragraph 24 ---
“Merely a trifle,” scoffed one magistrate. “He’ll do errand work. No expense!”

--- oliver_twist | Institutional Cruelty | Paragraph 25 ---
And so, Oliver was restored his limb to Gamfield, who whipped him as a tutorial in obedience while his foster master grumbled about “wasted opportunities.”

--- oliver_twist | Institutional Cruelty | Paragraph 26 ---
That very night, the beadle came for a new waif—a Foundling, as it were, in the gutter. Some nameless woman had trod her last in the streets, and the beadle noted the shoes worn to dust.

--- oliver_twist | Institutional Cruelty | Paragraph 27 ---
Gamble of the gamest sort: the board gave their verdict of “economical,” and so Mr. Bumble marched anew, bearing fresh victims to the clutches of “charity.”

--- tale_of_two_cities | Fate and History | Paragraph 1 ---
It was an epoch of supreme blessing and dire woe, an age wherein wisdom sat cheek‑by‑jowl with folly, belief wrestled with incredulity, light contended with darkness, hope sprang eternal whilst despair lay wintry and cold; we possessed all that could be imagined, yet we owned nothing at all; we were all hastening toward heaven’s gates, yet we were equally bent upon the opposite road—so that, in brief, the age bore such a striking resemblance to our own that the loudest pundits of the day declared it could only be judged in the superlative, for good or for ill.

--- tale_of_two_cities | Fate and History | Paragraph 2 ---
France, less favoured in spiritual matters than her sister‑island bearing shield and trident, glided downhill with unnatural smoothness, minting paper coin and squandering it with abandon. Under the tutelage of her Christian clergy she amused herself, moreover, with such humane feats as condemning a youth to have his hands severed, his tongue torn out with pincers, and his body consigned to the flames, merely because he had failed to kneel in the rain before a filthy procession of monks that passed within fifty or sixty yards of his gaze. It is probable that, deep in the forests of France and Norway, saplings were already taking root when that wretch met his end, trees earmarked by the Woodman—Fate herself—to be felled and sawn into planks for a certain movable framework bearing a sack and a knife, a grim relic of history. Likewise, in the rough outbuildings of the hard‑working tillers whose fields skirt Paris, one might have found, that very day, rude carts caked with rustic mire, sniffed at by swine and roosted in by fowl, already set aside by the Farmer—Death himself—to become the tumbrils of the coming Revolution. Yet both the Woodman and the Farmer, though they labour without ceasing, do so in silence; no ear detects their muffled tread, for to suspect them awake would be to brand oneself an atheist and a traitor.

--- tale_of_two_cities | Fate and History | Paragraph 3 ---
It was the Dover road that lay, on a bleak Friday night late in November, before the first of those souls with whom this narrative is concerned. The Dover road stretched, as far as he could tell, beyond the lumbering Dover mail as it laboured up Shooter’s Hill. He trudged upward through the mire at the mail’s flank, as did the other passengers; not because any of them relished walking exercise under such circumstances, but because the hill, the harness, the mud, and the mail were altogether so ponderous that the horses had halted thrice already, and once even drawn the coach across the road with a mutinous design to haul it back to Blackheath. Nevertheless, reins, whip, coachman, and guard, acting in concert, had read that article of war which forbade a purpose otherwise strongly favouring the notion that some brute beasts are endowed with reason; the team, therefore, yielded and returned to its duty.

--- tale_of_two_cities | Fate and History | Paragraph 4 ---
The passenger entrusted to this history stood upon the coach‑step, half‑in, half‑out, preparing to climb aboard; the two other travellers lingered close behind him, poised to follow. He remained upon the step, suspended between carriage and road, while they stayed in the lane below. All three turned their eyes from coachman to guard and back again, listening intently. The coachman glanced rearward, the guard did likewise, and even the emphatic leader pricked up his ears and turned his gaze backward, without uttering a word of contradiction.

--- tale_of_two_cities | Fate and History | Paragraph 5 ---
A solemn truth to ponder is that every human creature is constituted to be an impenetrable secret and mystery to every other. A grave reflection, when I enter a great city by night, is that each of those darkly clustered houses shelters its own hidden life; each room within each house encloses its own private thought; each beating heart among the hundreds of thousands there harbours, in some of its imaginings, a secret that lies concealed from the heart nearest to it! Something of the awfulness, even of Death itself, is traceable to this condition. I can no longer turn the leaves of this beloved book, vainly hoping to read it all in time; I can no longer gaze into the depths of this unfathomable water, wherein, as fleeting lights dance upon its surface, I have caught glints of buried treasure and other sunken things. It was ordained that the book should snap shut with a spring, forever and ever, after I had read but a single page. It was decreed that the water should be locked in an eternal frost, while the light played upon its surface and I stood ignorant upon the shore. My friend is dead, my neighbour is dead, my love—the darling of my soul—is dead; it is the inexorable consolidation and perpetuation of the secret that ever dwelt within that individuality, a secret I shall bear within myself to my life’s end. In any of the burial‑places of this city through which I wander, is there a sleeper more inscrutable than its bustling inhabitants are, in their innermost selves, to me—or than I am to them?

--- tale_of_two_cities | Fate and History | Paragraph 6 ---
What time the mail‑coach lumbered, jolted, rattled, and bumped upon its wearisome way, bearing its three fellow‑inscrutables within. To these likewise, the shadows of the night revealed themselves, taking shape in the forms suggested by their dozing eyes and wandering thoughts.

--- tale_of_two_cities | Fate and History | Paragraph 7 ---
Tellson’s Bank experienced a run upon it in the mail. As the bank passenger—his arm thrust through the leathern strap that strove to keep him from pounding against his neighbour and thrusting him into his corner whenever the coach suffered a special jolt—nodded in his seat with half‑closed eyes, the little coach‑windows, and the coach‑lamp dimly gleaming through them, together with the bulky bundle of the opposite passenger, became the bank and transacted a great stroke of business. The harness’s rattle sounded like the chink of coin, and more drafts were honoured in five minutes than even Tellson’s, with all its foreign and domestic connections, ever managed in thrice the span. Then the strong‑rooms deep underground at Tellson’s, containing such of their valuable stores and secrets as were known to the passenger (and it was not a little that he knew of them), flung wide before him; he entered among them with the great keys and the feebly‑burning candle, and found them safe, strong, sound, and still, exactly as he had last beheld them.

The mildewed interior of the coach, with its damp and dirty straw, its unpleasant odour, and its gloom, resembled nothing so much as a larger dog‑kennel. Mr. Lorry, the passenger, shaking himself free of it amid straw‑chains, a tangle of shaggy wrapper, a flapping hat, and muddy legs, appeared rather as a larger sort of dog.

Mr. Lorry had been idle a considerable time, and had just poured out his last glassful of wine with as complete an air of satisfaction as ever graces an elderly gentleman of fresh complexion who has reached the bottom of a bottle, when a rattling of wheels rose up the narrow street and rumbled into the inn‑yard.

--- tale_of_two_cities | Fate and History | Paragraph 12 ---
The obscurity was so thick that Mr. Lorry, picking his way over the well‑worn Turkey carpet, supposed Miss Manette to be, for the moment, in some adjoining room; until, having passed the two tall candles, he beheld standing to receive him at the table between them and the fire a young lady of not more than seventeen, cloaked in a riding‑coat, still clutching her straw travelling‑hat by its ribbon. As his eyes settled upon her short, slight, pretty form, a wealth of golden hair, a pair of blue eyes that met his own with an inquiring glance, and a forehead possessing a singular capacity (remembering how young and smooth it was) to ripple and knit itself into an expression that was neither pure perplexity nor wonder nor alarm nor merely a bright fixed attention, though it embraced all four, a sudden vivid likeness flashed before him—of a child he had once cradled in his arms on the passage across that very Channel, on a bitter cold night when hail drove heavily and the sea ran high. The likeness fled, like a breath across the surface of the gaunt pier‑glass behind her, on whose frame a hospital procession of negro cupids—several headless, all crippled—offered black baskets of Dead Sea fruit to black divinities of the feminine gender; and he made his formal bow to Miss Manette.

I had the honour of knowing him there. Our dealings were business, yet confidential. I was then resident in our French House, and had remained so—oh!—for twenty years.”

No; you have been the ward of Tellson’s House ever since, and I have been occupied with the other business of Tellson’s House since. Feelings! I have no leisure for them, no opportunity for them. “As I was saying; if Monsieur Manette had not died; if he had instead vanished suddenly and silently; if he had been spirited away; if it had not been difficult to conjecture to what dreadful place, though no art could trace him; if he had an enemy among his compatriots who could exercise a privilege that I, in my own time, have known even the boldest souls afraid to whisper of across the water—for instance, the privilege of filling blank forms for the consignment of any soul to the oblivion of a prison for any length of time; if his wife had implored king, queen, court, clergy, for any tidings of him, and all in vain;—then the chronicle of your father would have become the chronicle of this unfortunate gentleman, the Doctor of Beauvais.”

--- tale_of_two_cities | Fate and History | Paragraph 18 ---
And now that the cloud had settled upon Saint Antoine, which a fleeting gleam had once driven from his sacred visage, the darkness lay heavy—cold, filth, sickness, ignorance, and want stood in attendance upon the saintly presence, nobles of great power all; but, most especially the last. One beheld specimens of a people who had endured a terrible grinding and regrinding in the mill, and certainly not in the fabulous mill that grinds old men young; they shuddered at every corner, passed in and out at each doorway, peered from every window, fluttered in every rag that the wind shook. The mill that had subdued them was the mill that grinds youth into age; the children bore ancient faces and grave voices; and upon them, and upon the grown visages, and ploughed into every furrow of age, only to rise afresh, lay the sigh—Hunger. It prevailed everywhere. Hunger was expelled from the tall houses, hanging in the wretched clothing upon poles and lines; Hunger was patched into them with straw, rag, wood, and paper; Hunger was repeated in every fragment of the meagre firewood that the man sawed off; Hunger stared down from the smokeless chimneys, and rose up from the filthy street that possessed no offal among its refuse, of anything edible. Hunger was inscribed upon the baker’s shelves, in every small loaf of his scanty stock of bad bread; at the sausage shop, in every dead‑dog preparation offered for sale. Hunger rattled its dry bones among the roasting chestnuts in the turning cylinder; Hunger was shred into atomics in every farthing porringer of husky potato chips, fried with a few reluctant drops of oil.

Yet the time had not yet arrived; and every wind that swept over France merely shook the rags of the scarecrows in vain, for the birds, fine of song and feather, took no heed.

Madame Defarge, his wife, sat in the shop behind the counter as he entered. Madame Defarge was a stout woman of about his own age, possessed of a watchful eye that seldom seemed to rest upon anything, a large hand heavily ringed, a steady visage, strong features, and great composure of manner. There was a character about Madame Defarge from which one might have predicted that she rarely erred against herself in any of the reckonings over which she presided. Madame Defarge, being sensitive to cold, was wrapped in fur, and had a quantity of bright shawl twined about her head, though not so as to conceal her large earrings. Her knitting lay before her, but she had laid it down to pick her teeth with a toothpick. Thus engaged, with her right elbow supported by her left hand, Madame Defarge uttered not a word when her lord entered, but coughed a single grain of cough. This, combined with the lifting of her darkly defined eyebrows over the toothpick by the breadth of a line, suggested to her husband that he would do well to look round the shop among the customers, for any new patron who had dropped in while he crossed the way.

Such a staircase, with its appurtenances, in the older and more crowded quarters of Paris, would be bad enough in the present day; but at that time it was vile indeed to unaccustomed and unhardened senses. Every little dwelling within the great foul nest of one lofty building—that is to say, the room or rooms within each door that opened upon the general staircase—left its own heap of refuse upon its own landing, besides hurling other refuse from its own windows. The uncontrollable and hopeless mass of decay thus engendered would have polluted the air, even if poverty and deprivation had not already laden it with their intangible impurities; the two foul sources combined rendered the atmosphere all but unbearable. Through such an atmosphere, by a steep, dark shaft of dirt and poison, the way lay. Yielding to his own disturbance of mind, and to his young companion’s agitation—which grew with each instant—Mr. Jarvis Lorry twice paused to rest. Each pause was made at a doleful grating, whereby any lingering good air that remained uncorrupted seemed to escape, and all spoilt and sickly vapours seemed to crawl in. Through the rusted bars, tastes rather than glimpses were seized of the jumbled neighbourhood; and nothing within range, nearer or lower than the summits of the two great towers of Notre‑Dawn, held any promise of healthy life or wholesome aspiration.

The half‑door, once opened, was opened a fraction further and secured at that angle for the nonce. A broad shaft of light poured into the garret, revealing the workman with an unfinished shoe upon his lap, pausing in his labour. His few common tools and various scraps of leather lay at his feet and upon his bench. He bore a white beard, raggedly cut, yet not very long; a hollow face; and exceedingly bright eyes. The hollowness and thinness of his countenance would have caused those eyes to appear large beneath his yet‑dark eyebrows and his confused white hair, though they were in truth otherwise; but they were naturally large, and seemed unnaturally so. His yellowed rags of shirt gaped at the throat, exposing a body withered and worn. He, his old canvas frock, his loose stockings, and all his poor tatters of attire had, in a long seclusion from direct light and air, faded to such a dull uniformity of parchment‑yellow that it would have been hard to discern which was which.

--- tale_of_two_cities | Fate and History | Paragraph 28 ---
All within reach had suspended their labour, or their idleness, to hasten to the spot and drink the wine. The rough, irregular stones of the street, pointing every which way, and which one might have thought expressly designed to lame every living creature that approached them, had dammed the wine into little pools; each pool was encircled by its own jostling throng, according to its size. Some men knelt, cupped their two hands together, and sipped, or endeavoured to aid women who bent over their shoulders to sip, before the wine had all run out between their fingers. Others, men and women alike, dipped into the puddles with little mugs of broken earthenware, or even with handkerchiefs torn from women’s heads, which were squeezed dry into infants’ mouths; others raised small mud‑embankments to stem the wine as it ran; others, guided by lookers‑on stationed at high windows, darted hither and thither to cut off little streams of wine that fled in new directions; others devoted themselves to the sodden, lee‑dyed fragments of the cask, licking and even champing the moister wine‑rotted pieces with eager relish. There was no drainage to carry off the wine, and not only was it all taken up, but so much mud was gathered with it that there might have been a scavenger in the street, had anyone acquainted with it believed in such a miraculous presence.

To keep out the cold, one half of this door was fastened shut, while the other was opened but a crack. Such a scant admission of light rendered it difficult, upon first entering, to discern anything; and only long habit could have slowly formed in anyone the ability to perform work requiring nicety in such obscurity. “Long ago.”

--- tale_of_two_cities | Resurrection and Sacrifice | Paragraph 1 ---
In the year of our Lord one thousand seven hundred and seventy‑five, a season of spiritual illumination was granted to England, as it was to this realm. Mrs. Southcott, lately attaining the venerable age of twenty‑five blessed years, had been foretold by a prophetic private of the Life Guards that the very city of London and Westminster was destined to be engulfed. Even the phantom of Cock‑lane, a short dozen years after its rapping, seemed to have exhausted its prophetic messages, for the spirits of this same year, deficient in originality, issued their own feeble cries. communications, strange to relate, from a gathering of British subjects in America had recently reached the Crown and the people of England; and, oddly enough, these tidings proved of greater consequence to mankind than any utterances ever conveyed through the fowls of the Cock‑lane brood.

--- tale_of_two_cities | Resurrection and Sacrifice | Paragraph 2 ---
In England there was scarcely a sufficiency of order or protection to permit any national boasting. Daring burglaries by armed men and nocturnal highway robberies occurred in the capital with alarming regularity; families were publicly admonished not to venture beyond the town’s bounds unless they first removed their furniture to the warehouses of upholsterers for safekeeping. The highwayman, cloaked in darkness, was often a city tradesman by daylight, and, when recognised and challenged by a fellow tradesman whom he had stopped in the guise of “the Captain,” he would gallantly shoot his victim through the head and ride away. The mail was ambushed by a band of seven robbers, the guard discharged three of them, only to be slain himself by the remaining four, “in consequence of the failure of his ammunition,” after which the mail was plundered at leisure. The illustrious Lord Mayor of London was compelled to surrender on Turnham Green, in the presence of his retinue, to a single highwayman who despoiled him before all eyes. Prisoners within Newgate’s walls engaged in fierce battles with their keepers, while the majesty of the law discharged blunderbusses loaded with shot and ball among them. Thieves stole diamond crosses from the necks of noble lords at courtly assemblies; musketeers entered St. Giles’s in search of contraband, and the mob fired upon the musketeers, who in turn returned fire, none regarding these events as extraordinary. Amidst this chaos, the ever‑busy and ever‑ineffectual hangman was constantly summoned: now stringing together heterogeneous criminals, now hanging a housebreaker seized on Tuesday on Saturday, now burning a dozen persons at Newgate, now incinerating pamphlets at Westminster Hall’s door; today he took the life of a monstrous murderer, tomorrow that of a pitiful thief who had pilfered a farmer’s boy of sixpence.

A marvelous fact to ponder is that every human being is a profound secret and mystery to every other. A solemn reflection arises when I enter a great city by night: each darkly clustered house encloses its own secret; each room within those houses guards its own secret; each beating heart among the hundreds of thousands harbours a secret within its own imaginings, a secret to the heart nearest it! A portion of the awe, even that which Death itself inspires, is attributable to this. No longer can I turn the pages of this beloved book, hoping in vain to read it through to the end. No longer can I gaze into the unfathomable depths of this water, wherein fleeting glimmers have revealed buried treasure and other submerged marvels. It was ordained that the book should shut with a spring, forever and ever, when I had read but a single page. It was ordained that the water should be sealed in an eternal frost, while the light played upon its surface and I stood ignorant upon the shore. My friend is dead, my neighbour is dead, my beloved, the darling of my soul, is dead; it is the inexorable consolidation and perpetuation of the secret that ever resided within that individuality, a secret I shall carry with me to the very end of my days. In any of the burial‑places of this city through which I pass, is there a sleeper more inscrutable than the busy inhabitants are, in their innermost personality, to me, or than I am to them?

The wine was red, and it had stained the narrow street of the Saint Antoine suburb in Paris where it was spilled. It had stained many hands, many faces, many naked feet, many wooden shoes. The hands of the man who sawed the wood left red marks upon the billets; the forehead of the woman who nursed her baby was stained with the old rag she wound about her head again. --- tale_of_two_cities | Resurrection and Sacrifice | Paragraph 14 ---
Its abiding place was in all things fitted to it. A narrow, winding street, replete with offence and stench, branched into other narrow, winding streets, all peopled by rags and nightcaps, and all smelling of rags and nightcaps, while every visible thing wore a brooding look that boded ill. In the hunted air of the people there lingered a wild‑beast thought of the possibility of turning at bay. Though depressed and slinking, they possessed eyes of fire; compressed lips white with what they suppressed; foreheads knit in the likeness of the gallows‑rope they contemplated, either to endure or to inflict. The trade signs—almost as numerous as the shops—were grim illustrations of Want. The butcher and porkman were depicted with only the leanest scraps of meat; the baker with the coarsest, meagre loaves. The people, rudely pictured as drinking in the wine‑shops, croaked over their scanty measures of thin wine and beer, and glowered confidentially together. Nothing was shown in a flourishing condition, save tools and weapons; yet the cutler’s knives and axes were sharp and bright, the smith’s hammers heavy, and the gunmaker’s stock murderous. The crippling stones of the pavement, with their many little reservoirs of mud and water, offered no footways but broke abruptly at the doors. The kennel, to make amends, ran down the centre of the street—when it ran at all, which was only after heavy rains, and then it ran in many eccentric fits into the houses. Across the streets, at wide intervals, a clumsy lamp was suspended by rope and pulley; at night, when the lamplighter had let these down, lit them, and hoisted them again, a feeble grove of dim wicks swung overhead in a sickly manner, as if at sea. Indeed they were at sea, and the ship and crew were in peril of tempest.

Such a staircase, with its accessories, in the older and more crowded parts of Paris, would be bad enough now; but at that time it was vile indeed to unaccustomed and unhardened senses. Every little habitation within the great foul nest of one high building—namely, the room or rooms within every door that opened onto the general staircase—left its own heap of refuse upon its own landing, besides flinging other refuse from its own windows. The uncontrollable and hopeless mass of decomposition engendered would have polluted the air even if poverty and deprivation had not loaded it with their intangible impurities; the two bad sources combined made it almost insupportable. Through such an atmosphere, by a steep, dark shaft of dirt and poison, the way lay. Yielding to his own disturbance of mind, and to his young companion’s mounting agitation, which grew more intense with each instant, Mr. Jarvis Lorry twice halted to rest. Each of these stoppages was made at a doleful grating, by which any lingering good air that might have escaped seemed to be corrupted, and all sickly vapours crawled in. Through the rusted bars, tastes rather than glimpses were caught of the jumbled neighbourhood; and nothing within range, nearer or lower than the summits of the two great towers of Notre‑Dame, promised any healthy life or wholesome aspirations.

The faintness of the voice was pitiable and dreadful. It had entirely lost the life and resonance of the human voice, affecting the senses like a once‑beautiful colour faded into a poor weak stain. So sunken and suppressed was it that it was like a voice from underground. So expressive was it of a hopeless, lost creature that a famished traveller, weary from solitary wandering in a wilderness, would have recalled home and friends in such a tone before lying down to die.

The Dover road lay before him on a Friday night late in November, before the first of those with whom this history has business. The Dover road, for him, extended beyond the Dover mail as it lumbered up Shooter’s Hill. He walked up the hill in the mire beside the mail, as the other passengers did; not because they relished the exercise, but because the hill, the harness, the mud, and the mail were all so heavy that the horses had already ceased three times, and once drew the coach across the road with a mutinous intent to return to Blackheath. Reins, whip, coachman, and guard, however, collectively read the article of war which forbade any other purpose, and the team capitulated, returning to its duty.

--- tale_of_two_cities | Resurrection and Sacrifice | Paragraph 47 ---
And now that the cloud settled upon Saint Antoine, which a momentary gleam had driven from his sacred countenance, the darkness grew heavy—cold, dirt, sickness, ignorance, and want were the lords in waiting upon the saintly presence—nobles of great power, above all the last. Samples of a people that had endured a terrible grinding and regrinding in the mill, and certainly not in the fanciful mill that ground old people young, shivered at every corner, passed in and out of every doorway, looked from every window, fluttered in every vestige of garment that the wind shook. The mill that had worked them down was the mill that grinds young people old; the children bore ancient faces and grave voices; and upon them, and upon the grown faces, and ploughed into every furrow of age and coming up afresh, was the sigh, Hunger. It was prevalent everywhere. Hunger was thrust from the tall houses in the wretched clothing that hung upon poles and lines; Hunger was patched into them with straw, rag, wood, and paper; Hunger was repeated in every fragment of the small modicum of firewood that the man sawed off; Hunger stared down from the smokeless chimneys and rose from the filthy street that offered no offal, among its refuse, of anything to eat. Hunger was the inscription upon the baker’s shelves, written upon every small loaf of his scanty stock of bad bread; at the sausage‑shop, it appeared in every dead‑dog preparation offered for sale. Hunger rattled its dry bones among the roasting chestnuts in the turned cylinder; Hunger was shred into atomics in every farthing porringer of husky chips of potato, fried with some reluctant drops of oil.

“Almost eighteen years.”

--- tale_of_two_cities | Mob Mentality | Paragraph 1 ---
It was the epoch of our Lord one thousand seven hundred seventy-five, an age of revelations as profound as those whispered in the annals of Holy Writ. In England, the heavens seemed to lean closer, bestowing upon the nation tokens of divine insight. Mrs. Southcott, in her five-and-twentieth year of sanctified existence, had become a vessel of prophetic truth, her visage a beacon of celestial promise. A soldier of the Life Guards, clad in the gilded trappings of authority, had proclaimed to the gathered multitude that the swallows of doom were poised to consume the citadel of London and the crown of Westminster. Even the specter of Cock-lane, long excised from the living memory of men, had, in the chaos of these times, been rekindled in the spectral tongues of the year past—though its lamentations, though fervent, lacked the fervor of true originality. Meanwhile, the correspondence of British subjects in America, though distant as the stars, bore tidings more vital than the whims of any Cock-lane apparition, shaping the fates of crown and commoner alike.

--- tale_of_two_cities | Mob Mentality | Paragraph 2 ---
In the realm of England, the scales of order and security tilted precariously, their balance swayed by the relentless tides of lawlessness. The capital itself, that proud jewel of commerce and crown, was plagued by the predations of midnight marauders whose blades danced through the night like specters. Families, fearing for their hearths, were advised to entrust their possessions to the safeguards of upholsterers, lest the night’s avarice spill into daylight. The highwayman, though cloaked in darkness, was but a tradesman in daylight, a cunning wretch whose marksmanship turned the wounded into the dust. The mail coach, a relic of regal trust, became a battlefield as conspirators ambushed its passage, leaving the guard to meet his end beneath a storm of leaden fury. The jails, choked with the breath of rebellion, echoed with the clash of prisoners and turnkeys, their strife punctuated by the thunder of blunderbusses. The hangman, a specter of futility, stretched his brutal hands to hang the convicted, burn the rebellious, and purge the streets with the fire of his trade, ever a warden to the cyclical tide of crime.

The year 1775, cloaked in its own tempest, saw the great hearts and the humble souls alike tread their paths, the woodman and the farmer, unseen, shaping the tide of history.

--- tale_of_two_cities | Class Struggle and Revolution | Paragraph 1 ---
In the annus mirabilis of our Lord, one thousand seven hundred and seventy-five, when celestial whispers seemed to pierce the English realm as they had not done since time’s inception, extraordinary portents befell. Mrs. Southcott, celebrated in her twenty-fifth year of spiritual rebirth, was heralded by a man of arms who prophesied cataclysmic upheavals—a vision of London and Westminster engulfed in a fiery union. Even the spectral remnant of Cock-lane, long since silenced, roared anew, though its cries lacked the fervor of its forebears. Conversely, tidings from America’s British delegates arrived, bearing revelations so profound they eclipsed even the ghostly warnings. These messages, delivered by humble yet steadfast delegates, held sway over the realm, their impact surpassing any preceding oracular murmurs from the spectral hearth of Cock-lane.

--- tale_of_two_cities | Class Struggle and Revolution | Paragraph 2 ---
France, our neighboring land of contrasts, seemed to glide through existence with unerring ease, churning currency from inked ledgers and parting it with reckless abandon. Under the benevolent oversight of fervent clergy, its populace endured grim dogmas: a youth sentenced to have his digits severed and tongue torn, a public pyre reserved for dissenters who refused to kneel before a mendicant procession. Yet, in the hidden groves of northern woods, trees marked by Fate’s inscrutable hand sprouted, destined to be felled into planks for a grand, ominous construct—one that would house a sentinel and a blade, a harbinger of dread. Meanwhile, in the outskirts of Paris, humble laborers sheltered in makeshift huts, their dwellings infested with swine and poultry, had been earmarked by Death’s doleman to become deathbeds of the Revolution. Though these unseen architects of doom toiled in silence, their labor was relentless, their progress as invisible as their devotion to an inevitable fate. To suspect their sentience was to court heresy, deemed both godless and treasonous.

--- tale_of_two_cities | Class Struggle and Revolution | Paragraph 3 ---
Within England’s borders, no semblance of order could boast of grandeur. Each night brought armed marauders prowling the capital, their blades clashing against the weary, while highwaymen, masquerading as tradesmen, assassinated their brethren in a spectacle of duplicity. Families were advised to sequester their valuables in upholsterers’ depots, their trepidation palpable. A daylight highwayman, upon recognition, felled a fellow citizen with unflinching precision before vanishing into the shadows. The Lord Mayor of London, a man of immense repute, was accosted at Turnham Green by a bold robber who stripped him of his finery in full view of his guests. Gaols erupted in internecine strife, with inmates fighting their stern jailers, and the law’s avenger fired volleys among them, though with disastrous imprecision. Thieves plucked diamond regalia from noble necks in courtly drawing-rooms, while musketeers, dispatched to inspect St. Giles’s for illicit goods, found themselves besieged by a vengeful mob. The hangman, a figure of relentless toil and impotent justice, was perpetually summoned: to suspend rioters, to execute thieves caught years prior, to immolate dissidents or pamphlets at Westminster. In this cacophony, even the most heinous crimes were meted out with courtly solemnity, from the slicing of a family’s heirlooms to the public burning of a pauper to death.
