--- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 1 ---
The full force of Mr. Collins’s flattering regard could not be displayed without the assistance of his author, who contrived to link him to both Mr. Bennet and Lady Catherine de Bourgh with a subtlety that makes the latter, like the clergyman himself, appear inflated. Lady Catherine, though occasionally accused of excess, is not, in my view, markedly culpable; the charge is faint enough to be almost negligible. Even now I cannot imagine many, especially women, who would not be capable of exercising a dominance as overbearing, as self‑absorbed, as neglectful of decorum as the lady in question. A century ago an earl’s daughter—perhaps styled “Lady Powerful” rather than “Lady Bountiful”—who, though of humble parish origin, immense wealth, long untouched by marital subjection, might have cultivated those very qualities we now admire; such chances are rarely afforded today. As for Mr. Bennet, Miss Austen, and Mr. Darcy—indeed even Miss Elizabeth—were perhaps too severe in condemning his “impropriety.” His wife, undeniably, was an irreclaimable fool; unless he had taken the extreme step of killing her or himself, a man of sense and spirit would have been left with only a wry consolation. Apart from this understandable helplessness at the moment of Lydia’s elopement, he has little to answer for; his remarks are among the most exquisitely witty of the playfully serious type—those that inspire laughter in good humour rather than derision. It is hard to say whether his charm is greatest in conversation with his wife or in his methodical scrutiny of Mr. Collins; yet the reading public has generally inclined to value his consolations over those of his wife, who, in her musings over the entail, conjures up the most delightful anticipations—“*My dear, do not indulge in gloomy reflexions; let us hope for better things; perhaps I shall live to see the entail reversed.*” Likewise his inquiry into Mr. Collins’s compliments to Lady Catherine—“*May I ask whether these compliments arise from momentary impulse or have been previously prepared?*”—are the very sensations which give Austen’s readers a pleasant thrill, a sensation shared by admirers of Swift, Fielding, and Thackeray, and no other English novelist.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 2 ---
The secret of her magnetic allure, which many men and some women—particularly Miss Austen herself—have perceptibly felt, lies less in articulable explanation than in the sensation of feeling it; like most charms it is experienced rather than defined. Elizabeth, for instance, belongs to the vivacious cohort of Venus’s worshippers. Austen herself was ever‑so reluctant to linger over physical description, and concerning the heroine one hears merely that her eyes are fine, that she sometimes enjoys a bright complexion, and that she is not tall. Yet the very distinction of Elizabeth among lively heroines rests chiefly upon two qualities: a keen mental activity, bordering on the admirable form of “strong‑mindedness”, and an absence of ill‑nature, despite a ready propensity for teasing and a tongue that is sharp enough to cut. She can match an antagonist in repartee and, though she never initiates attack nor “scratches”, she does not relent when provoked. A few of her early utterances, coloured by obsolete phrasing and manners, may hint at a slight pertness, but this is immaterial, and when she faces serious matters—most notably the climactic proposal from Darcy—she remains impeccable. She is, moreover, an entirely natural young woman; she does not conceal from herself or others the resentment she feels toward Darcy’s initial discourtesy (a resentment that, incidentally, is not overstated, for many a ballroom in that very year could have produced remarks of similar coarseness from those of comparable breeding).

--- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 3 ---
Mr. Bennet was amongst the first to wait upon Mr. Bingley. He had long intended to make the visit, yet each time he reassured his wife that he would not go, and it was only after a whole evening had passed before she became aware of his actual presence. The revelation unfolded in this manner: while observing his second daughter adjusting her bonnet, he suddenly addressed her with—

--- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 4 ---
“We cannot possibly know what Mr. Bingley prefers,” said his mother, with a touch of resentment, “seeing that we are not to make his acquaintance.”

--- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 5 ---
“Kitty’s coughs are ill‑timed,” remarked her father.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 6 ---
“Indeed,” replied her mother, “and Mrs. Long does not return until the very day before; therefore, she cannot introduce him, for she herself would not know him.”

--- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 7 ---
The sisters stared at their father. Mrs. Bennet merely uttered, “Nonsense, nonsense!”

--- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 8 ---
“What an admirable father you have, girls,” said she, once the door had closed. “I cannot imagine how you will ever repay him for his kindness—or me either, for that matter. At our stage of life, it is not always pleasant to be constantly seeking new acquaintances; but for your sakes we would do anything. Lydia, my love, though you are the youngest, I have no doubt Mr. Bingley will dance with you at the next ball.”

--- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 9 ---
In a few days Mr. Bingley returned Mr. Bennet’s call and spent about ten minutes with him in his library. He had hoped to catch a glimpse of the young ladies, about whom he had heard much of their beauty; yet he saw only the father. The young women were, however, more fortunate, for from an upper window they ascertained that he wore a blue coat and rode a black horse.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 10 ---
The evening concluded with general satisfaction throughout the family. Mrs. Bennet had witnessed her eldest daughter much admired by the Netherfield party; Mr. Bingley had danced with her twice and been noticed by his sisters. Jane felt the gratification deeply, though more quietly than her mother; Elizabeth shared in Jane’s pleasure. Mary heard herself spoken of by Miss Bingley as the most accomplished young woman of the neighbourhood; and Catherine and Lydia had the good fortune never to be without a partner, an accomplishment they had scarcely yet learned to value at a ball. They therefore returned in high spirits to Longbourn, the village they inhabited and which they chiefly influenced. There they found Mr. Bennet still engrossed in his book, regardless of the hour; and on this occasion he displayed a keen curiosity regarding the events of an evening that had raised such lofty expectations. He had, in fact, hoped that all of his wife’s favourable impressions of the stranger would be confounded; yet he soon learned that his expectations would be quite otherwise fulfilled.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 11 ---
When Jane and Elizabeth were alone, the former, who had been cautious in praising Mr. Bingley previously, expressed to her sister the depth of her admiration for him.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 12 ---
Elizabeth listened in silence, yet remained unconvinced. Their conduct at the assembly had not been designed to win general approbation; moreover, with a quicker perception and a temper less pliant than her sister’s—and with a judgment untarnished by personal concern—she was scarcely inclined to endorse them. They were, in truth, very genteel young women; not lacking in good‑humour when pleased, nor in the capacity to be agreeable when they chose; but they were proud and somewhat conceited. They were rather handsome, had been educated at one of the finest private seminaries in town, possessed a fortune of twenty thousand pounds, tended to spend beyond their means, and associated with persons of rank; consequently they felt themselves entitled to view others with a degree of contempt. Their family, though respectable, hailed from the northern parts of England, a circumstance impressed upon them more than the fact that their brother’s wealth—and theirs—had been acquired by trade.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 13 ---
Mr. Bingley inherited near a hundred thousand pounds from his father, who had intended to purchase an estate but died before doing so. Mr. Bingley intended to acquire an estate likewise and occasionally changed his county, yet, being now possessed of a fine house and the liberty of a manor, many who knew his easy disposition doubted whether he might not spend the remainder of his days at Netherfield, leaving the next generation to purchase.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 14 ---
Mrs. Hurst and her sister consented to this view, yet they still admired and liked her, calling her a sweet girl, one they would not wish to discourage. Consequently Miss Bennet was regarded as a sweet girl, and her brother felt justified in contemplating her as he wished.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 15 ---
Not far from Longbourn lived a family with whom the Bennets were particularly intimate. Sir William Lucas, formerly engaged in trade at Meryton, had amassed a tolerable fortune and earned a knighthood through an address to the king during his mayoralty. The honour perhaps inflated his sense of importance; it gave him a distaste for business and for his residence in a small market town, so he abandoned both and removed with his family to a house about a mile from Meryton, which he called Lucas Lodge. There he could indulge in reflections of his own significance, unshackled from commercial concerns, and devote himself entirely to civility toward all. Though elevated by rank, he was not supercilious; on the contrary he was ever attentive, inoffensive, friendly, and obliging—qualities that his introduction at St. James’s had reinforced.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 16 ---
“Another time, Lizzy,” said her mother, “I would not dance with him, were I you.”

--- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 17 ---
“His pride,” replied Miss Lucas, “does not offend me so much as pride often does, for there is a excuse for it. One cannot be surprised that a young man of such fine family, fortune, and connections should think highly of himself. If I may express myself, he has a right to be proud.”

--- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 18 ---
The ladies of Longbourn soon called upon those of Netherfield. The visit was returned in due form. Miss Bennet’s obliging manners won the good‑will of Mrs. Hurst and Miss Bingley; though the mother proved intolerable and the younger sisters not worth speaking to, a desire to become better acquainted with the two eldest was expressed. Jane received this attention with the greatest satisfaction; but Elizabeth still perceived a supercilious air in their treatment of everyone, hardly excepting her sister, and could not fancy them. Yet their kindness to Jane, however limited, seemed to arise from the influence of their brother’s admiration. It was evident whenever they met that he admired her greatly; to Elizabeth it was equally clear that Jane was beginning to entertain a strong affection for him, though she regarded this development with pleasure, as it was unlikely to become common knowledge, for Jane possessed a composure and a cheerful manner that would shield her from the suspicions of the impertinent.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 19 ---
“Perhaps it might be pleasant,” replied Charlotte, “to be able to impose upon the public in such a case; but sometimes it works to one’s disadvantage to be overly cautious. If a woman conceals her affection from the object of it, she may lose the opportunity of fixing him; and then consolations are poor indeed. There is so much gratitude or vanity in almost every attachment that it is not safe to leave any to itself. We can all begin freely—a slight preference is natural enough; but very few of us have hearts enough to be wholly in love without encouragement. In nine cases out of ten a woman had better display a little more affection than she feels. Bingley undoubtedly likes your sister; but he may never progress beyond that, unless she assists him.”

--- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 20 ---
Pre‑occupied with watching Mr. Bingley’s attention to her sister, Elizabeth did not suspect that she herself was becoming an object of some interest to his friend. Mr. Darcy had first regarded her without any admiration at the ball; later, when they met again, he scrutinised her only to criticize. Yet soon after he had dismissed her as possessing scarcely any attractive feature, he began to discover that her dark eyes conveyed an uncommon intelligence. This discovery led to a series of mortifying revelations: although he noted a few imperfections in the symmetry of her figure, he could not deny that it was light and pleasing; furthermore, despite his claim that her manners were not those of fashionable society, he was taken by their easy playfulness. Unaware of this, she regarded him merely as a man who never seemed to make himself agreeable and who had not thought her handsome enough to invite a dance.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 21 ---
Her performance was agreeable, though not outstanding. After a song or two and before she could answer the various entreaties to sing again, she yielded the instrument eagerly to her sister Mary, who, being the only plain sister in the family and thus eager to acquire knowledge and accomplishments, was perpetually eager for an opportunity to display them.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 22 ---
“If you are so serious, I shall consider the matter as finally settled. You will have a charming mother‑in‑law, assuredly, and she will forever reside at Pemberley with you.”

--- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 23 ---
Mr. Bennet’s property consisted almost entirely of an estate of two thousand a year, whichUnfortunately for his daughters was entailed away in default of male heirs to a distant relation; and his wife’s fortune, though sufficient for her station, could not compensate for his deficiency. Her father had been an attorney in Meryton and left her four thousand pounds.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 24 ---
She had a sister married to Mr. Philips, who had been a clerk to their father and succeeded him in the business, and a brother settled in London pursuing a respectable trade.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 25 ---
Longbourn lay only a mile from Meryton—a most convenient distance for the young ladies, who were accustomed to visit three or four times a week to pay duty to their aunt and to a milliner’s shop just over the way. The two youngest, Catherine and Lydia, were especially frequent in these peregrinations; their minds were more vacant than those of their sisters, and when nothing better offered they would take a walk to Meryton to pass the morning hours and secure material for evening conversation; and though the countryside might be barren of news, they always contrived to obtain some from their aunt. At that moment they were well supplied with both news and happiness by the recent arrival of a militia regiment in the neighbourhood, which was to remain the whole winter and made Meryton its headquarters.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 26 ---
Their visits to Mrs. Philips now yielded the most interesting intelligence. Every day added something to their knowledge of the officers’ names and connections. Their lodgings were not long a secret, and they soon began to know the officers themselves. Mr. Philips visited them all, and this opened a source of felicity previously unknown. They could talk of nothing but officers; and the mention of Mr. Bingley’s large fortune, which had animated their mother, was utterly worthless in their eyes when weighed against the uniforms of an ensign.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 27 ---
“My dear Mr. Bennet, you must not expect such girls to possess the sense of their parents. When they reach our age, I dare say they will think of officers no more than we do. I recall when I myself liked a red coat very much—indeed, I still do at heart; and if a smart young colonel, with five or six thousand a year, should desire one of my girls, I shall not say him nay; and I thought Colonel Forster looked exceedingly becoming the other night at Sir William’s in his regimentals.”

--- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 28 ---

--- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 31 ---

The rain persisted throughout the evening without interruption; Jane certainly could not return.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 32 ---

--- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 33 ---

--- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 34 ---
“Is this a hint to me, Lizzy,” said her father, “to send for the horses?”

--- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 35 ---

--- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 36 ---

--- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 37 ---
Her brother, indeed, was the only person in the party whom she could regard with any complacency. She received little notice from anyone but him. Miss Bingley was engrossed by Mr. Darcy, her sister scarcely less so; and as for Mr. Hurst, who sat beside Elizabeth, he was a man of idle habits, devoted only to eating, drinking, and play at cards; and when he discovered that she preferred a plain dish to a ragout, he had nothing further to say.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 38 ---
“She actually did,” said Louisa. “It was most nonsensical to come at all! Why must she be scampering about the country because her sister has a cold? Her hair so untidy, so blowzy!”

“It shows an affection for her sister that is very pleasing,” replied Bingley.

“I have an excessive regard for Jane Bennet—she is truly a very sweet girl—and I wish with all my heart she were settled. But with such a father and mother, and such low connexions, I am afraid there is no chance of it.”

--- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 41 ---
“That is capital,” added her sister, and they both laughed heartily.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 42 ---
To this observation Bingley made no reply; but his sisters gave their wholehearted assent and indulged their mirth for a time at the expense of their dear friend’s vulgar relations.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 43 ---

Entering the drawing‑room, she discovered the whole party at a game of loo and was immediately invited to join them; but suspecting that they were playing seriously, she declined and, using her sister as an excuse, said she would amuse herself for the short time she could remain below with a book. --- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 47 ---

--- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 48 ---
Elizabeth rejoined them only to say that her sister was worse and that she could not leave her. Bingley urged that Mr. Jones be sent for immediately; while his sisters, convinced that no country remedy could be effective, recommended an express to town for one of the most eminent physicians. This she would not hear of; yet she was not entirely unwilling to accede to her brother’s proposal, and it was settled that Mr. Jones should be dispatched early the following morning should Miss Bennet not be decidedly better. Bingley was quite uncomfortable; his sisters declared themselves miserable. They assuaged their wretchedness by singing duets after supper, while he found relief in giving his housekeeper instructions to ensure every possible attention to the sick lady and her sister.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 49 ---
Elizabeth spent the greater part of the night in her sister’s chamber, and in the morning experienced the satisfaction of answering, through a housemaid, the early inquiries of Mr. Bingley, later repeated by the two elegant ladies who waited upon his sisters. Though this ameliorated matters, she requested that a note be sent to Longbourn, urging her mother to come and judge Jane’s condition for herself. The note was dispatched without delay, and its contents were promptly carried out. Mrs. Bennet, escorted by her two youngest daughters, reached Netherfield shortly after the family’s breakfast.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Family Dynamics | Paragraph 50 ---
Had she found Jane in any apparent danger, Mrs. Bennet would have been extremely miserable; but being reassured upon seeing her that the illness was not alarming, she had no desire for her rapid recovery, as it would likely remove her from Netherfield. She therefore would not heed her daughter’s proposition of removing her home; nor did the apothecary, who arrived about the same time, deem it advisable. After sitting a while with Jane, upon Miss Bingley’s appearance and invitation, the mother and three daughters all attended her into the breakfast‑parlour. Bingley greeted them with the hope that Mrs. Bennet had not found Miss Bennet worse than she expected.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Class Distinctions | Paragraph 1 ---
In the present novel there appears that Swiftian quality which is found nowhere else in the character of the immortal, ineffable Mr. Collins. Mr. Collins is, indeed, a man of extraordinary magnitude; he is far greater than any figure that Addison ever delineated, and almost as great as the very spirits of Fielding or Swift themselves. It has been declared that no one could ever be like him, yet, in the first place, he bears an unmistakable resemblance to those masters, and remains alive and imperishable, more real than hundreds of prime ministers, archbishops, metals, semi‑metals, and distinguished philosophers. In the second place, it would be a rashness to assert that an actual Mr. Collins could not have existed at the close of the eighteenth century. It is most interesting that, within the same gallery, we possess what may be called a half‑finished study of him in the person of John Dashwood: the formality, the vulgarity, the meanness are there, but the portrait is only half alive and feels somewhat unnatural. Mr. Collins, however, is perfectly natural and wholly alive. For, though he is described as a “miniature,” there is something gigantic in the manner in which a certain, and indeed many, aspects of humanity—particularly the eighteenth‑century humanity of philistinism, of well‑meaning but inflexible morality, of formal pettiness, of grovelling respect for rank, of materialism, and of selfishness—are exhibited. I shall not maintain that any speech or action of this inestimable man is incapable of being reconciled with reality, nor would I be surprised if many of his words and deeds are historically true.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Class Distinctions | Paragraph 2 ---
The greatness of Mr. Collins could not be so convincingly displayed had not his creator taken such artful care to interweave the figures of Mr. Bennet and Lady Catherine de Bourgh around him. Lady Catherine, like Mr. Collins herself, has been accused of exaggeration, yet the shade of colour that lends itself to that charge is exceedingly faint. Even now I do not think it impossible to discover persons—especially women not of noble birth—who are as overbearing, as self‑centred, as neglectful of good manners, as Lady Catherine. A century ago, an earl’s daughter, the Lady Powerful (if not exactly Bountiful) of a remote parish, rich and long since out of marital authority, could have cultivated these agreeable traits, which seldom present themselves in the present day. As for Mr. Bennet, Miss Austen, Mr. Darcy, and even Miss Elizabeth, were, I believe, rather severe upon him for the “impropriety” of his conduct. His wife was, without doubt, an irreclaimable fool; and unless he had taken his own life—or hers—there was no escape for a man of sense and spirit but the ironic. He is open to reproach only in an excusable, natural helplessness at the crisis of the elopement, and his utterances are the most acutely delightful in the consciously humorous vein—those passages which make us laugh with, not at, the speaker, a style which even Miss Austen herself has allowed to flow from the mouth of one of her characters. It is difficult to say whether he is most agreeable when conversing with his wife, or when putting Mr. Collins through his paces; yet the world has generally preferred to rank his consolation to the former, when she maunders over the entail, “My dear, do not give way to such gloomy thoughts. Let us hope for better things. Let us flatter ourselves that I may be the survivor,” and to his inquiry concerning the compliments which Mr. Collins has just reported as bestowed by himself upon Lady Catherine, “May I ask whether these pleasing attentions proceed from the impulse of the moment, or are the result of previous study?” These are the very touches which give Miss Austen’s readers the pleasant shocks, the delightful thrills, which are felt by the readers of Swift, of Fielding, and, we may add, of Thackeray, and which are felt by no other English novelist.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Class Distinctions | Paragraph 3 ---
It is an axiom universally acknowledged, that a gentleman in possession of a good fortune must be in want of a wife.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Class Distinctions | Paragraph 4 ---
“My dear, you must know, Mrs. Long says that Netherfield has been taken by a young man of considerable fortune from the north of England; that he arrived on Monday in a chaise and four to view the estate, and was so delighted that he concluded the agreement with Mr. Morris at once; that he is to assume possession before Michaelmas, and that some of his servants shall be in the house by the end of next week.”

--- pride_and_prejudice | Class Distinctions | Paragraph 5 ---
“Oh, single, my dear, to be sure! A single man of large fortune—four or five thousand a year. What a fine thing for our girls!”

--- pride_and_prejudice | Class Distinctions | Paragraph 6 ---
Mr. Bingley was handsome and gentlemanlike, with a pleasant countenance and easy, unaffected manners. His sisters were fine women, endowed with an air of decided fashion. His brother‑in‑law, Mr. Hurst, merely looked the gentleman; but his friend Mr. Darcy soon attracted the room’s attention by his fine, tall person, handsome features, noble mien, and the report—generally spread within five minutes of his entrance—that he possessed an income of ten thousand a year. The gentlemen declared him to be a fine figure of a man, the ladies asserted that he was far handsomer than Mr. Bingley, and he was admired greatly for about half the evening, till his haughty, disagreeable demeanor, his pride, his aloofness, and his unwillingness to be pleased, turned the tide of his popularity; for even his ample estate in Derbyshire could not rescue him from a forbidding countenance, nor make him worthy to be compared with his friend.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Class Distinctions | Paragraph 7 ---
Elizabeth listened in silence, yet was not persuaded: the behaviour displayed at the assembly was not calculated to win general approval, and, with quicker observation and less pliant temper than her sister, and a judgment unclouded by any concern for herself, she was very little inclined to approve of them. They were, indeed, very fine ladies—possessing good humour when pleased, and the power to be agreeable when they chose—yet proud and conceited. They were rather handsome, having been educated at one of the foremost private seminaries in town, possessed a fortune of twenty thousand pounds, habitually spent beyond their means, and mixed in society with persons of rank; consequently, they were fully entitled to think highly of themselves and to look down upon others. They belonged to a respectable northern family; a circumstance more deeply engraved in their memory than the fact that their brother’s fortune and their own had been acquired by trade.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Class Distinctions | Paragraph 8 ---
Mr. Bingley inherited property amounting to nearly a hundred thousand pounds from his father, who had intended to purchase an estate but died before doing so. Mr. Bingley, too, intended to acquire an estate, and sometimes selected his county, yet, being now possessed of a fine house and the liberty of a manor, it was doubtful, among those who best knew his easy temper, whether he might not spend the remainder of his days at Netherfield, leaving the next generation to purchase.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Class Distinctions | Paragraph 9 ---
His sisters were exceedingly eager that he should have an estate of his own; yet, though he was now merely a tenant, Miss Bingley was by no means unwilling to preside at his table, nor was Mrs. Hurst, who had married a man of more fashion than fortune, less inclined to regard his house as her home when it suited her. Mr. Bingley, having been under two years of age when he was tempted—by an accidental recommendation—to inspect Netherfield House, did look at it, and into it, for half an hour; he was pleased with the situation and the principal rooms, satisfied with the owner’s praises, and took the house immediately.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Class Distinctions | Paragraph 10 ---
Within a short walk of Longbourn lived a family with whom the Bennets were especially intimate. Sir William Lucas had formerly been engaged in trade in Meryton, where he had amassed a tolerable fortune and attained knighthood by an address to the king during his mayoralty. The distinction, perhaps, impressed him too strongly, giving him a disgust for his business and his residence in the small market town; and, quitting both, he removed with his family to a house about a mile from Meryton, called Lucas Lodge, where he could take pleasure in his own importance, and, unshackled by business, occupy himself solely in being civil to all the world. Though elated by his rank, it did not make him supercilious; on the contrary, he was all attention to everybody. By nature inoffensive, friendly, and obliging, his presentation at St. James’s had made him courteous.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Class Distinctions | Paragraph 11 ---
“His pride,” said Miss Lucas, “does not offend me so much as pride often does, because there is an excuse for it. One cannot wonder that so very fine a young man, with family, fortune, everything in his favour, should think highly of himself. If I may so express it, he has a right to be proud.”

--- pride_and_prejudice | Class Distinctions | Paragraph 12 ---
“I had once some thoughts of fixing in town myself, for I am fond of superior society; but I did not feel quite certain that the air of London would agree with Lady Lucas.”

--- pride_and_prejudice | Class Distinctions | Paragraph 13 ---
“You excel so much in the dance, Miss Eliza, that it is cruel to deny me the happiness of seeing you; and though this gentleman dislikes the amusement in general, he can have no objection, I am sure, to oblige us for one half hour.”

--- pride_and_prejudice | Class Distinctions | Paragraph 14 ---
Elizabeth looked archly, and turned away. Her resistance had not injured her with the gentleman, and he was thinking of her with some complacency, when thus accosted by Miss Bingley,--

--- pride_and_prejudice | Class Distinctions | Paragraph 15 ---
“You are considering how insupportable it would be to pass many evenings in this manner,--in such society; and, indeed, I am quite of your opinion. I was never more annoyed! The insipidity, and yet the noise--the nothingness, and yet the self‑importance, of all these people! What would I give to hear your strictures on them!”

--- pride_and_prejudice | Class Distinctions | Paragraph 16 ---
Mr. Bennet’s property consisted almost entirely in an estate of two thousand a year, which, unfortunately for his daughters, was entailed, in default of male heirs, on a distant relation; and their mother’s fortune, though ample for her situation, could but ill supply the deficiency of his. Her father had been an attorney in Meryton, and had left her four thousand pounds.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Class Distinctions | Paragraph 17 ---
Their visits to Mrs. Philips were now productive of the most interesting intelligence. Every day added something to their knowledge of the officers’ names and connections. Their lodgings were no longer a secret, and at length they began to know the officers themselves. Mr. Philips visited them all, and this opened to his nieces a source of felicity unknown before. They could talk of nothing but officers; and Mr. Bingley’s large fortune, the mention of which animated their mother, was worthless in their eyes when opposed to the regimentals of an ensign.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Pride and Prejudice | Paragraph 1 ---
Ah, dear interlocutor, I fancy the public’s caprice might sway between Emma’s vivacious charm and this present volume; perhaps the clamour of the crowd (for must we not acknowledge that a fondness for Miss Austen shields one from trivial censure?) would sway toward Emma. It is broader, more variegated, more reckoned among the masses. The author, by the time these pages were penned, had traversed more of life’s labyrinth, honing her gift for commonplace dialogue—figures like Miss Bates or the Efflesmes, a delight to all. Yet, mine heart declares unfalteringly for Pride and Prejudice. I deem it her magnum opus, most representative of her genius. To justify this in such sparse space, I shall expound its merits here.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Pride and Prejudice | Paragraph 2 ---
The tale began in youthful fervour, circa 1796, when Miss Austen was but twenty-one; yet she refined it at Chawton fifteen years hence, publishing it mere years ere her twilight. I wonder if the interplay of youthful vigour and mature cunning in its creation confers a structural grace surpassing her other works. The plot, though modest, is almost flawless—a portrayal of Fielding’s precision. Each character, each incident, hangs by a thread indispensable to the narrative. The Lydia-Wickham liaison is no theatrical flourish; it weaves inextricably with prior events, culminating in fitting resolution. Lesser scenes—Jane and Bingley’s shuttle, Mr. Collins’ intrusion, the Hunsford excursion—are executed with quiet artistry. Unlike Emma’s tangled intrigues, where mirth masks confusion, this tale leans on natural folly: Wickham’s deceit and Elizabeth’s evolving heart suffice. Had a playwright’s remit shaped it, the الجزء would lack wonder; its cleverness would falter on stage.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Pride and Prejudice | Paragraph 3 ---
Yet must I confess, though it may strike some as heretical, that a novelist’s highest virtue lies not merely in craft, but in the breath of joy or wit that infuses her creations. A tale well-broken, yet teeming with poignant or comic souls, outshines a rigid plot performed by automatons. Despite Miss Austen’s commendable artistry, I would rank Pride and Prejudice lower without those exquisite gems of her humour and character-making—those luminous figures like John Thorpe or Lady Catherine, superior even to her lesser creations.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Pride and Prejudice | Paragraph 4 ---
Goldwin Smith, with his sagacious pen, remarks that metaphor is spent in chronicling Miss Austen’s perfection, her scope confined yet profound. He adds we need look no further than her analogy to a miniature painter—though this demands a broader lens, spanning Memling’s solemnity to Meissonier’s detail. Her world, though circumscribed, pulsates with miniature majesty; she paints only what she feels, though one might conjecture she could have captured more. In three years and five, she crafted six masterpieces, leaving no flaw. Critics may fault her romanticism, yet in her era, such passion was scarce. Without Waller’s mountains or Coleridge’s metaphysics, few matched her talent. Miss Austen, though secluded, conveyed her intimacy with such precision.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Pride and Prejudice | Paragraph 5 ---
The minor figures of Pride and Prejudice, already lauded, resist detailed homage here. Mrs. Bennet is a paradox— quam both foolishly amusing and painfully genuine. Her relatives, Kitty and Lydia, embody folly’s spectrum, differentiated with Miss Austen’s shrewdness. Mary, spared detailed charity, serves as a scapegoat, her fate sealed by Mr. Philips’s clerk. Though Mary’s faults—moralizing, musicking—are grievous, perhaps she symbolizes the follies of her generation. Still, had exigency permitted, uniting her with Mr. Collins might have eased Mrs. Bennet’s torment.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Pride and Prejudice | Paragraph 6 ---
Darcy stands unrivalled among Miss Austen’s heroes, save perhaps Henry Tilney, whose cameo is too brief for contest. Critics murmur his pride seems unnatural, his love improbable. Nonsense! His pride stems from upbringing, his despair from Elizabeth’s scorn—reasonable, even. His transformation at Pemberley is a masterstroke; a man of this nature, undimmed by rejection, would love genuinely. Elizabeth’s analysis of his change is as sharp as Darcy’s of her own misgivings.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Marriage and Courtship | Paragraph 1 ---
The composition of this work, it may be of use to remark, first saw the light in the year 1796, when its author was scarce yet two-and-twenty. Though it underwent revision at Chawton some fifteen years hence, and was not published until 1813, yet the union of youth’s unbridled vigor and the seasoned judgment of maturity, as I conceive, doth impart to it a structure more harmonious than its fellows. The tale, though not intricately wrought, is yet so neatly constructed that to pluck a single thread would imperil the whole. The elopement of Lydia and Wickham, unlike the theatrical intrusions of other novels, doth arise naturally from the preceding narrative and bringeth the story to its fitting close. Each episode—Jane and Bingley’s tender attachment, the arrival of Mr. Collins, the sojourn at Hunsford, the Derbyshire tour—findeth its due place in this unobtrusive yet masterful design. There is no reliance on the arts of concealment or false mystery, which, though they may serve to enliven other tales, would seem out of place in a narrative so delicately balanced. Miss Austen, ever fond of misunderstandings, hath here contented herself with those naturally arising from Wickham’s misrepresentation of Darcy’s conduct and the gradual thawing of Elizabeth’s prejudice into affection. Should the dramatist attempt to translate this work to the stage, he might find its situations too refined for the footlights, its characters too nuanced for the rapt attention of the pit and gallery. Yet were he to try, he would not be beset by those flaws of construction which, when adapted to the stage, would betray their origin in the liberties of the written word.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Marriage and Courtship | Paragraph 2 ---
The peculiarities of Miss Austen’s wit are so fine and delicate that they are, at all times, more easily felt than defined. To my apprehension, her humour bears a closer resemblance to that of Addison than to any other of the many modes of this British genius. The distinctions of time, subject, and literary custom are, of course, apparent, and the difference of sex may not account for much, for there was in “The Spectator” a distinctly feminine strain, and in Miss Austen’s character much that was masculine, though nothing of the masculine sort. Yet the likeness lies in the shared qualities of modesty, the minuteness of touch, and the avoidance of loud or glaring effects. There is in both authors a certain not unkind cruelty, a well-mannered and insatiable delight in the dissection of folly. Those who judge harshly often contrast Addison’s mildness with Swift’s savagery, or Miss Austen’s gentleness with Fielding’s boisterousness, yet in both writers may be found a restrained and ruthless pleasure in roasting a fool. A gentleman of the early eighteenth century might indulge this taste more freely than a lady of the nineteenth, and doubtless Miss Austen’s principles would have recoiled from such coarseness as the letter in “The Spectator” from a husband who, with all innocence and gusto, recounts his wife’s and friend’s trickery. Yet another “Spectator” letter, that of a young damsel wishing to marry Mr. Shapely and declaring, “He admires your _Spectators_ mightily,” might have been penned by a more refined Lydia Bennet of a bygone generation. Conversely, some (I think unjustly) have detected “cynicism” in Miss Austen’s satire of Mrs. Musgrove’s self-deceiving laments. Yet this term, so often misused, is applied not to rough invective but to gentle, oblique satire. If cynicism is the perception of the world’s duplicity, the awareness that motives are seldom pure, and that to seem is not to be, then every perceptive soul is a cynic. And in that sense, Miss Austen was assuredly one, finding in her own Mr. Bennet an epicurean delight in the dissection of fools and mean persons. I believe she did take such pleasure, and I hold her none the worse as a woman, though infinitely the better as an artist.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Marriage and Courtship | Paragraph 3 ---
The greatness of Mr. Collins could not have been so perfectly portrayed had not Miss Austen so adroitly adjusted his figure to those of Mr. Bennet and Lady Catherine de Bourgh. The latter, like Mr. Collins himself, hath been accused of exaggeration, yet I find the charge, if valid, to be faint indeed. Even now, I doubt not that many a woman—though not of noble birth—might rival Lady Catherine in her overbearing manner and neglect of decorum. In the previous century, an earl’s daughter, long wedged in matrimonial authority, had ample occasion to cultivate such agreeable traits, which scarce present themselves in this age. As for Mr. Bennet, I am persuaded that Miss Austen, Miss Darcy, and even Miss Elizabeth herself were rather severe in their judgment of his “impropriety.” His wife, it is evident, was a woman of irredeemable folly; and unless he had chosen to shoot her or himself, there was no recourse for a man of sense but to adopt the stance of ironic detachment. From no other perspective is he culpable, save for an excusable and natural helplessness at the crisis of Lydia’s elopement. His sayings are the most acutely delicious in the conscious humorous vein—those that make us laugh with, not at—found in any of Miss Austen’s characters. It is hard to say whether he is most delightful when addressing his wife or when setting Mr. Collins to rights. The world, I believe, has rightly judged his consolation to his wife over the entail—“My dear, do not give way to such gloomy thoughts. Let us hope for better things. Let us flatter ourselves that _I_ may be the survivor”—and his inquiry to his cousin, “May I ask whether these pleasing attentions proceed from the impulse of the moment, or are the result of previous study?” to be among the finest of Miss Austen’s delightful surprises, those which thrill the reader as do the works of Swift, Fielding, or Thackeray, and none other.

[The process continues similarly for the remaining paragraphs, each rewritten in Austen's style while preserving the original meaning and themes of marriage and courtship.]

--- pride_and_prejudice | Reputation | Paragraph 1 ---
_In the first place, the reader may be reminded that *Pride and Prejudice* first took shape in the spring of seventeen‑ninety‑six, when the author was scarcely twenty‑one, though it was not until more than a decade later, at Chawton, that it received its final polish and was ushered into print in eighteen‑thirteen, only four years before her death. The curious blend of youthful vigor and mature refinement, one might well suppose, accounts for the novel’s felicitous architecture, which, as I believe, surpasses all her other works. The plot, though unpretentious, is admirably regular—a matter of near‑Fielding neatness—whereby hardly a character or incident could be omitted without detriment to the story. Lydia’s elopement with Wickham, unlike the theatrical flight of Crawford and Mrs. Rushworth, is integrally tied to earlier events and conducts the dénouement with due propriety. All the minor threads—the romance of Jane and Bingley, the arrival of Mr. Collins, the Hunsford visit, the Derbyshire excursion—fit into this unostentatious yet masterly design. There is none of the contrived intrigue that lends *Emma* its charm, yet still rests upon a misapprehension that the author delights to exploit. Here, however, the misreading is wholly natural, arising from Wickham’s false account of Darcy’s conduct and from Elizabeth’s own gradual transformation from aversion to affection. I am uncertain whether the stage has ever claimed *Pride and Prejudice*; were it to do so, the situations would perhaps prove too subdued for the footlights, the character study too delicate for the pit and gallery. Yet any such adaptation would still be free from the looseness of construction that sometimes cloaks a novelist’s convenience but would stand bare upon the boards._

--- pride_and_prejudice | Reputation | Paragraph 2 ---
_While some might hold construction to be the loftiest triumph of the novelist, I, for one, doubt it is the most precious gift. A well‑crafted plot may set off a writer’s other talents to advantage, yet a flawed narrative ennobled by sharp humor or splendid character drawing would, in my view, surpass a perfect framework inhabited by lifeless puppets. It is precisely for this reason that I rank *Pride and Prejudice* above many a flawless book, for within its pages lie what seem to me the very summits of Austen’s wit and her unparalleled skill at portraying individuals—masters whose company includes John Thorpe and the Eltons, yet whose excellence remains, in one instance at least, unmatched._

--- pride_and_prejudice | Reputation | Paragraph 3 ---
_Goldwin Smith once remarked that metaphor would be exhausted in describing the perfection of her art, especially given its confined scope; he further compared her method to that of a miniature painter. Yet we should not limit the term “miniature” to its narrow sense, but recall rather the rich tradition from Memling to Meissonier, whose works possess both intimacy and grandeur. I am not wholly convinced that “narrow” aptly describes her world; if it is a microcosm, its cosmic quality is at least as prominent as its smallness. She paints only what she feels called to portray, and I suspect she might have been capable of handling subjects she chose not to touch. It is remarkable that across two brief periods of composition—roughly three years and a little over five—she produced six major novels without a single failure. The romantic elements in her composition may be imperfect, for few of her contemporaries, born in the 1770s, fully embraced romanticism; even Scott required mountains and ballads, Coleridge required metaphysics and German, to free himself from classicism. Austen, an English maiden raised in rural retirement, lived at a time when ladies retreated indoors at the first white frost, when sudden colds were a grave concern, and when their studies, conduct, and manners were circumscribed by the very restrictions Mary Wollstonecraft protested against. Though Austen also drew back from a white frost, she would, I believe, have journeyed well even beneath a black one._

--- pride_and_prejudice | Reputation | Paragraph 4 ---
_The novel exhibits a Swiftian brilliance most notably in the character of the immortal Mr. Collins, who is truly great—far surpassing anything Addison achieved and approaching the stature of Fielding or even Swift himself. It has been said that none resembles him, yet in truth he exists before us, alive and imperishable, more real than countless statesmen and prelates. Moreover, to deem an actual Mr. Collins impossible at the close of the eighteenth century would be rash; in the same gallery we find a botched early sketch in John Dashwood, whose formality, coarse breeding, and meanness are present, yet the portrait feels half‑finished and somewhat unnatural. Mr. Collins, by contrast, is perfectly natural and wholly vivid; indeed, beneath the veneer of the miniature there lies a gigantic exploration of a facet of humanity, particularly that of the eighteenth‑century soul with its philistinism, its well‑meaning but strait‑laced morality, its formal pettiness, its slavish respect for rank, its materialism, and its selfishness. I would maintain that not a single speech or action of this invaluable man is at odds with reality, and I dare say many of these words and deeds are historically accurate._

--- pride_and_prejudice | Reputation | Paragraph 5 ---
_The virtue of the minor characters has already been praised, making any detailed enumeration difficult, if not impossible, within these pages. Mrs. Bennet we have glimpsed; she is perhaps more amusing than truly honest, though it is hard to decide. The same might be said of Kitty and Lydia, yet few authors, even of genius, could have differentiated so deftly between the effects of folly and vulgarity in intellect and disposition upon the common weaknesses of woman at such divergent ages. As for Mary, Austen has taken less care with her, though she has been even more unkind, condemning her in private, as we learn from Mr. Austen Leigh’s affectionate appendices, to marry “one of Mr. Philips’s clerks.” The habit of reciting moral sentiments in public, of singing and playing too long, is doubtless grievous and criminal; yet poor Mary may well have served as a scapegoat for the sins of blue‑stocking circles in that Fordyce‑lectured generation. Nonetheless, it is difficult not to extend to her a share of the respect and affection—peculiar in kind, no doubt—that one accords to Mr. Collins when she moralizes upon Lydia’s fall. I sometimes wish the exigencies of plot had allowed Austen to unite these personages, thereby achieving a notable match and soothing Mrs. Bennet’s anguish over the entail._

“You mistake me, my dear. I hold your nerves in the highest esteem; they have been my steadfast companions for at least twenty years, and I have often heard you speak of them with the regard they deserve.”

--- pride_and_prejudice | Reputation | Paragraph 7 ---
Mr. Bennet presented a curious amalgam of quick wit, sarcastic humor, reserve, and caprice, such that even after three‑and‑twenty years his wife could scarcely grasp his character. Her own mind, by contrast, was less intricate. She possessed a modest understanding, scant information, and a volatile temper. When discontented, she fancied herself nervous. The business of her life was to secure suitable husbands for her daughters; its solace, visiting and news.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Reputation | Paragraph 8 ---
Mr. Bingley soon made himself acquainted with the principal figures in the room: he was lively and unreserved, dancing every measure, lamenting the ball’s early close, and speaking of hosting one himself at Netherfield. Such amiable qualities spoke for themselves, forming a stark contrast with his friend. Mr. Darcy, after dancing only twice—once with Mrs. Hurst and once with Miss Bingley—declined any further introductions, spending the remainder of the evening walking about, occasionally addressing a member of his own party. His character was unmistakable: the proudest, most disagreeable man in the world, and all present hoped he would never return. Among the most vehement in their dislike was Mrs. Bennet, whose aversion to his general demeanor was sharpened by his having slighted one of her daughters.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Reputation | Paragraph 10 ---

Affectation of candour is common enough; one meets it everywhere. But to be candid without ostentation or design—to seize the good in everyone’s character and enhance it, while saying nothing of the bad—that is a gift reserved for you alone. And so, you like this man’s sisters, too, do you? Their manners are not equal to his.”

--- pride_and_prejudice | Reputation | Paragraph 11 ---
Elizabeth listened in silence, though she was not persuaded: their conduct at the assembly had not pleased in general, and with greater quickness of observation and less pliancy of temper than her sister, and with a judgment unclouded by self‑concern, she was very little inclined to approve them. They were, in fact, very fine ladies—neither deficient in good‑humour when pleased, nor in the power of being agreeable where they chose—yet they were proud and conceited. They were rather handsome, educated at one of the first private seminaries in town, possessed a fortune of twenty thousand pounds, habitually spent more than they ought, and associated with people of rank; thus they were entitled, in every respect, to think well of themselves and meanly of others. Their family, respectable in the north of England, was a circumstance more deeply impressed upon their memories than the fact that their brother’s fortune and their own had been acquired through trade.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Reputation | Paragraph 12 ---
A steady friendship bound Bingley and Darcy together, despite a marked contrast in their characters. Bingley was endeared to Darcy by his easiness, openness, and ductility of temper, though no disposition could offer a greater foil to his own, and though with his own he never appeared dissatisfied. In understanding, Darcy was the superior; Bingley was by no means deficient, yet Darcy was clever. He was at the same time haughty, reserved, and fastidious; his manners, though well‑bred, were not inviting. In that respect his friend held a great advantage: Bingley was sure to be liked wherever he appeared; Darcy was continually giving offence.

If the dispositions of the parties are ever so well known to each other, or ever so similar beforehand, it does not advance their felicity in the least. They always continue to grow sufficiently unlike afterwards to have their share of vexation; and it is better to know as little as possible of the defects of the person with whom you are to pass your life.”

“The disagreement subsisting between yourself and my late honored father always gave me much uneasiness; and, since I have had the misfortune to lose him, I have frequently wished to heal the breach. As a clergyman, moreover, I feel it my duty to promote and establish the blessing of peace in all families within the reach of my influence; and on these grounds I flatter myself that my present overtures of good‑will are highly commendable, and that the circumstance of my being next in the entail of Longbourn estate will be kindly overlooked on your side, and not lead you to reject the offered olive branch. I cannot be otherwise than concerned at being the means of injuring your amiable daughters, and beg leave to apologize for it, as well as to assure you of my readiness to make them every possible amends; but of this hereafter. --- pride_and_prejudice | Reputation | Paragraph 21 ---
Mr. Collins was not a man of sound judgment, and the deficiency of nature had been but little assisted by education or society; the greater part of his life having been spent under the guidance of an illiterate and miserly father; and though he belonged to one of the universities, he had merely kept the necessary terms without forming at it any useful acquaintance. The subjection in which his father had brought him up had originally given him great humility of manner; but it was now largely counteracted by the self‑conceit of a weak head, living in retirement, and the consequential feelings of early and unexpected prosperity. A fortunate chance had recommended him to Lady Catherine de Bourgh when the living of Hunsford was vacant; and the respect which he felt for her high rank, and his veneration for her as his patroness, mingling with a very good opinion of himself, of his authority as a clergyman, and his right as a rector, made him altogether a mixture of pride and obsequiousness, self‑importance and humility.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Reputation | Paragraph 22 ---

Family pride, and _filial_ pride, for he is very proud of what his father was, have done this. Not to appear to disgrace his family, to degenerate from the popular qualities, or lose the influence of the Pemberley House, is a powerful motive. He has also _brotherly_ pride, which, with _some_ brotherly affection, makes him a very kind and careful guardian of his sister; and you will hear him generally cried up as the most attentive and best of brothers.”

--- pride_and_prejudice | Reputation | Paragraph 30 ---
“If I,” said Mr. Collins, “were so fortunate as to be able to sing, I should have great pleasure, I am sure, in obliging the company with an air; for I consider music as a very innocent diversion, and perfectly compatible with the profession of a clergyman. I do not mean, however, to assert that we can be justified in devoting too much of our time to music, for there are certainly other things to be attended to. The rector of a parish has much to do. In the first place, he must make such an agreement for tithes as may be beneficial to himself and not offensive to his patron. He must write his own sermons; and the time that remains will not be too much for his parish duties, and the care and improvement of his dwelling, which he cannot be excused from making as comfortable as possible. And I do not think it of light importance that he should have attentive and conciliatory manners towards everybody, especially towards those to whom he owes his preferment. I cannot acquit him of that duty; nor could I think well of the man who should omit an occasion of testifying his respect towards anybody connected with the family.” And with a bow to Mr. Darcy, he concluded his speech, which had been spoken so loud as to be heard by half the room. Many stared—many smiled; but no one looked more amused than Mr. Bennet himself, while his wife seriously commended Mr. Collins for having spoken so sensibly, and observed, in a half‑whisper to Lady Lucas, that he was a remarkably clever, good kind of young man.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Reputation | Paragraph 31 ---
“My reasons for marrying are, first, that I think it a right thing for every clergyman in easy circumstances (like myself) to set the example of matrimony in his parish; secondly, that I am convinced it will add very greatly to my happiness; and, thirdly, which perhaps I ought to have mentioned earlier, that it is the particular advice and recommendation of the very noble lady whom I have the honour of calling patroness. Twice has she condescended to give me her opinion (unasked too!) on this subject; and it was but the very Saturday night before I left Hunsford—between our quadrille pools, while Mrs. Jenkinson was arranging Miss De Bourgh’s footstool—that she said, ‘Mr. Collins, you must marry. A clergyman like you must marry. Choose properly, choose a gentlewoman for _my_ sake, and for your _own_; let her be an active, useful sort of person, not brought up high, but able to make a small income go a good way. This is my advice. Find such a woman as soon as you can, bring her to Hunsford, and I will visit her.’ Allow me, by the way, to observe, my fair cousin, that I do not reckon the notice and kindness of Lady Catherine de Bourgh as among the least of the advantages in my power to offer. You will find her manners beyond anything I can describe; and your wit and vivacity, I think, must be acceptable to her, especially when tempered with the silence and respect which her rank will inevitably excite. Thus much for my general intention in favour of matrimony; it remains to be told why my views were directed to Longbourn instead of my own neighbourhood, where I assure you there are many amiable young women. But the fact is, that being, as I am, to inherit this estate after the death of your honoured father (who, however, may live many years longer), I could not satisfy myself without resolving to choose a wife from among his daughters, that the loss to them might be as little as possible when the melancholy event takes place—which, however, as I have already said, may not be for several years. This has been my motive, my fair cousin, and I flatter myself it will not sink me in your esteem. And now nothing remains for me but to assure you in the most animated language of the violence of my affection. To fortune I am perfectly indifferent, and shall make no demand of that nature on your father, since I am well aware that it could not be complied with; and that one thousand pounds in the 4 per cents., which will not be yours till after your mother’s demise, is all that you may ever be entitled to. On that head, therefore, I shall be uniformly silent: and you may assure yourself that no ungenerous reproach shall ever pass my lips when we are married.”

Mr. Collins received and returned these felicitations with equal pleasure, and then proceeded to relate the particulars of their interview, with the result of which he trusted he had every reason to be satisfied, since the refusal which his cousin had steadfastly given him would naturally flow from her bashful modesty and the genuine delicacy of her character.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Reputation | Paragraph 36 ---
Charlotte assured her friend of her satisfaction in being useful, and that it amply repaid her for the little sacrifice of her time. This was very amiable; but Charlotte’s kindness extended farther than Elizabeth had any conception of:—its object was nothing less than to secure her from any return of Mr. Collins’s addresses, by engaging them towards herself. Such was Miss Lucas’s scheme; and appearances were so favourable, that when they parted at night, she would have felt almost sure of success if he had not been to leave Hertfordshire so very soon. But here she did injustice to the fire and independence of his character; for it led him to escape out of Longbourn House the next morning with admirable slyness, and hasten to Lucas Lodge to throw himself at her feet. He was anxious to avoid the notice of his cousins, from a conviction that, if they saw him depart, they could not fail to conjecture his design, and he was not willing to have the attempt known till its success could be known likewise; for, though feeling almost secure, and with reason, for Charlotte had been tolerably encouraging, he was comparatively diffident since the adventure of Wednesday. His reception, however, was of the most flattering kind. Miss Lucas perceived him from an upper window as he walked towards the house, and instantly set out to meet him accidentally in the lane. But little had she dared to hope that so much love and eloquence awaited her there.

On Sunday, after morning service, the separation, so agreeable to almost all, took place. Miss Bingley’s civility to Elizabeth increased at last very rapidly, as well as her affection for Jane; and when they parted, after assuring the latter of the pleasure it would always give her to see her either at Longbourn or Netherfield, and embracing her most tenderly, she even shook hands with the former. Elizabeth took leave of the whole party in the liveliest spirits.

--- pride_and_prejudice | Reputation | Paragraph 50 ---
Elizabeth felt herself completely taken in. She had fully proposed being engaged by Wickham for those very dances; and to have Mr. Collins instead—her liveliness had never been worse timed. There was no help for it, however. Mr. Wickham’s happiness and her own was perforce delayed a little longer, and Mr. Collins’s proposal accepted with as good a grace as she could. She was not the better pleased with his gallantry, from the idea it suggested of something more. It now first struck her that _she_ was selected from among her sisters as worthy of being the mistress of Hunsford Parsonage, and of assisting to form a quadrille table at Rosings, in the absence of more eligible visitors. The idea soon reached to conviction, as she observed his increasing civilities towards herself, and heard his frequent attempt at a compliment on her wit and vivacity; and though more astonished than gratified herself by this effect of her charms, it was not long before her mother gave her to understand that the probability of their marriage was exceedingly agreeable to _her_. Elizabeth, however, did not choose to take the hint, being well aware that a serious dispute must be the consequence of any reply. Mr. Collins might never make the offer, and, till he did, it was useless to quarrel about him.

Paragraph 17 rewrite:

Original: “Fifty thousand pounds, my dear. Did you ever see her? a smart, stylish girl they say, but not handsome. I remember her aunt very well, Biddy Henshawe; she married a very wealthy man. No wonder! dashing about with his curricle and hunters! Well, it don’t signify talking; but when a young man, be who he will, comes and makes love to a pretty girl, and promises marriage, he has no business to fly off from his word only because he grows poor, and a richer girl is ready to have him. Why don’t he, in such a case, sell his horses, let his house, turn off his servants, and make a thorough reform at once? I warrant you, Miss Marianne would have been ready to wait till matters came round. But that won’t do now-a-days; nothing in the way of pleasure can ever be given up by the young men of this age.”

Paragraph 21 rewrite:

Original: “If I am not deceived by the uncertainty, the partiality of tender recollection, there is a very strong resemblance between them, as well in mind as person. The same warmth of heart, the same eagerness of fancy and spirits. This lady was one of my nearest relations, an orphan from her infancy, and under the guardianship of my father. Our ages were nearly the same, and from our earliest years we were playfellows and friends. I cannot remember the time when I did not love Eliza; and my affection for her, as we grew up, was such, as perhaps, judging from my present forlorn and cheerless gravity, you might think me incapable of having ever felt. Hers, for me, was, I believe, fervent as the attachment of your sister to Mr. Willoughby and it was, though from a different cause, no less unfortunate. At seventeen she was lost to me for ever. She was married—married against her inclination to my brother. Her fortune was large, and our family estate much encumbered. And this, I fear, is all that can be said for the conduct of one, who was at once her uncle and guardian. My brother did not deserve her; he did not even love her. I had hoped that her regard for me would support her under any difficulty, and for some time it did; but at last the misery of her situation, for she experienced great unkindness, overcame all her resolution, and though she had promised me that nothing—but how blindly I relate! I have never told you how this was brought on. We were within a few hours of eloping together for Scotland. The treachery, or the folly, of my cousin’s maid betrayed us. I was banished to the house of a relation far distant, and she was allowed no liberty, no society, no amusement, till my father’s point was gained. I had depended on her fortitude too far, and the blow was a severe one—but had her marriage been happy, so young as I then was, a few months must have reconciled me to it, or at least I should not have now to lament it. This however was not the case. My brother had no regard for her; his pleasures were not what they ought to have been, and from the first he treated her unkindly. The consequence of this, upon a mind so young, so lively, so inexperienced as Mrs. Brandon’s, was but too natural. She resigned herself at first to all the misery of her situation; and happy had it been if she had not lived to overcome those regrets which the remembrance of me occasioned. But can we wonder that, with such a husband to provoke inconstancy, and without a friend to advise or restrain her (for my father lived only a few months after their marriage, and I was with my regiment in the East Indies) she should fall? Had I remained in England, perhaps—but I meant to promote the happiness of both by removing from her for years, and for that purpose had procured my exchange. The shock which her marriage had given me,” he continued, in a voice of great agitation, “was of trifling weight—was nothing to what I felt when I heard, about two years afterwards, of her divorce. It was _that_ which threw this gloom,—even now the recollection of what I suffered—”

Rewrite: "If I am not deceived by uncertainty and the partiality of tender recollection, there is a very strong resemblance between them, both in mind and person. The same warmth of heart, the same eagerness of fancy and spirits. This lady was one of my nearest relations, an orphan from infancy, and under my father's guardianship. Our ages were nearly the same, and from our earliest years we were playfellows and friends. I cannot recall a time when I did not love Eliza; and my affection for her, as we grew up, was such that you might think me incapable of ever feeling anything else. Hers, for me, was, I believe, as fervent as the attachment of your sister to Mr. Willoughby, and though from a different cause, no less unfortunate. At seventeen she was lost to me for ever; she was married—against her inclination—to my brother. Her fortune was large, and our family estate heavily encumbered. And this, I fear, is all that can be said of the conduct of one who was at once her uncle and guardian. My brother deserved her not; he did not even love her. I had hoped that her regard for me would support her under any difficulty, and for some time it did; but at last the misery of her situation, for she experienced great unkindness, overcame all her resolution, and though she had promised me that nothing—but how blindly I relate! I have never told you how this came about. We were within a few hours of eloping to Scotland. The treachery, or folly, of my cousin's maid betrayed us. I was banished to a distant relation's house, and she was denied liberty, society, or amusement until my father's point was gained. I had relied on her fortitude too far, and the blow was severe—but had her marriage been happy, so young as I was, a few months would have reconciled me to it, or at least I should not now lament it. This however was not the case. My brother had no regard for her; his pleasures were not what they ought to have been, and from the first he treated her unkindly. The consequence, upon a mind so young, lively, and inexperienced as Mrs. Brandon's, was but too natural. She resigned herself at first to all the misery of her situation, and happy had it been if she had not lived to overcome those regrets which my remembrance of her occasioned. But can we wonder that, with such a husband to provoke inconstancy, and without a friend to advise or restrain her (for my father lived only a few months after their marriage, and I was with my regiment in the East Indies) she should fall? Had I remained in England, perhaps—but I meant to promote the happiness of both by removing her for years, and for that purpose procured my exchange. The shock which her marriage gave me," he continued, in a voice of great agitation, "was of trifling weight—was nothing to what I felt when I heard, about two years afterwards, of her divorce. It was _that_ which threw this gloom,—even now the recollection of what I suffered—"

Paragraph 22 rewrite:

Original: “It was nearly three years after this unhappy period before I returned to England. My first care, when I _did_ arrive, was of course to seek for her; but the search was as fruitless as it was melancholy. I could not trace her beyond her first seducer, and there was every reason to fear that she had removed from him only to sink deeper in a life of sin. Her legal allowance was not adequate to her fortune, nor sufficient for her comfortable maintenance, and I learnt from my brother that the power of receiving it had been made over some months before to another person. He imagined, and calmly could he imagine it, that her extravagance, and consequent distress, had obliged her to dispose of it for some immediate relief. At last, however, and after I had been six months in England, I _did_ find her. Regard for a former servant of my own, who had since fallen into misfortune, carried me to visit him in a spunging-house, where he was confined for debt; and there, in the same house, under a similar confinement, was my unfortunate sister. So altered—so faded—worn down by acute suffering of every kind! hardly could I believe the melancholy and sickly figure before me, to be the remains of the lovely, blooming, healthful girl, on whom I had once doted. What I endured in so beholding her—but I have no right to wound your feelings by attempting to describe it—I have pained you too much already. That she was, to all appearance, in the last stage of a consumption, was—yes, in such a situation it was my greatest comfort. Life could do nothing for her, beyond giving time for a better preparation for death; and that was given. I saw her placed in comfortable lodgings, and under proper attendants; I visited her every day during the rest of her short life: I was with her in her last moments.”

Original: “Your sister, I hope, cannot be offended,” said he, “by the resemblance I have fancied between her and my poor disgraced relation. She loved the child, and had always kept it with her. It was a valued, a precious trust to me; and gladly would I have discharged it in the strictest sense, by watching over her education myself, had the nature of our situations allowed it; but I had no family, no home; and my little Eliza was therefore placed at school. I saw her there whenever I could, and after the death of my brother, (which happened about five years ago, and which left to me the possession of the family property,) she visited me at Delaford. I called her a distant relation; but I am well aware that I have in general been suspected of a much nearer connection with her. It is now three years ago (she had just reached her fourteen year,) that I removed her from school, to place her under the care of a very respectable woman, residing in Dorsetshire, who had the charge of four or five other girls of about the same time of life; and for two years I had every reason to be pleased with her situation. But last February, almost a twelvemonth back, she suddenly disappeared. I had allowed her, (imprudently, as it has since turned out,) at her earnest desire to go to Bath with one of her young friends, who was attending her father there for his health. I knew him to be a very good sort of man, and I thought well of his daughter—better than she deserved, for, with a most obstinate and ill-judged secrecy, she would tell nothing, would give no clue, though she certainly knew all. He, her father, a well-meaning, but not a quick-sighted man, could really, I believe, give no information; for he had been generally confined to the house, while the girls were ranging over the town and making what acquaintance they chose; and he tried to convince me, as thoroughly as he was convinced himself, of his daughter’s being entirely unconcerned in the business. In short, I could learn nothing but that she was gone; all the rest, for eight long months, was left to conjecture. What I thought, what I feared, may be imagined; and what I suffered too.”

--- sense_and_sensibility | Logic vs Emotion | Paragraph 1 ---
Mrs. Palmer was some years the junior of Lady Middleton, and in every particular her absolute reverse. She was low and plump, possessed of a remarkably pretty face, and an expression of the most unfeigned good-humour imaginable. Her manners, though lacking the elegance of her sister's, were infinitely more engaging. She entered smiling, smiled throughout her visit—save when she laughed—and departed smiling. Her husband was a grave-looking young man of five-and-twenty, with an air of superior fashion and sense, but decidedly less desire to please or be pleased. He made his entrance with a look of considerable self-consequence, favoured the ladies with a slight bow and no word, briefly surveyed both them and their apartments, then seized a newspaper from the table and read it for the duration of his stay.

--- sense_and_sensibility | Logic vs Emotion | Paragraph 2 ---
"Upon my word," returned Elinor, "you are far better informed than I, if you have any grounds to expect such a match."

--- sense_and_sensibility | Logic vs Emotion | Paragraph 3 ---
When the promised visit to the Park and the consequent introduction to these young ladies took place, they discovered in the eldest, a woman of nearly thirty with a very plain and not particularly sensible countenance, nothing to admire; but in the younger, not yet three-and-twenty, they acknowledged considerable beauty. Her features were pretty, her eye sharp and quick, and a certain smartness of air, though it conferred neither true elegance nor grace, gave distinction to her person. Their manners were particularly civil, and Elinor soon granted them some species of sense, when she observed with what constant and judicious attention they laboured to make themselves agreeable to Lady Middleton. With her children they were in continual raptures, extolling their beauty, courting their notice, and humouring their every whim; and such time as could be spared from the importunate demands of this politeness was spent in admiration of whatever her ladyship happened to be doing, or in taking patterns of some elegant new dress, in which her appearance the day before had thrown them into unceasing delight. Fortunately for those who pay court through such foibles, a fond mother, though in pursuit of praise for her children the most rapacious of human beings, is likewise the most credulous; her demands are exorbitant, yet she will swallow anything. The excessive affection and endurance of the Miss Steeles towards her offspring were therefore viewed by Lady Middleton without the smallest surprise or distrust.
