Mrs. Palmer, several years the junior of Lady Middleton, differed from her in every point of character. She was of modest stature and plump figure, her countenance both pretty and marked by an exuberant good humour. Though her manners lacked the refinement of her sister's, they were far more engaging. She entered with a smile, maintained it throughout her visit, and departed smiling still. Her husband, a young man of some five and twenty, bore an air of fashion and sense, though a certain self‑sufficiency rendered him less eager to please. He bowed slightly to the ladies without a word, then settled upon a newspaper and read it unmoved for the duration of his stay.

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 3 ---
“How delightful this room is!” exclaimed Mrs. Palmer, turning to Mrs. Dashwood. “I never beheld anything so charming! Think, Mama, how it has been improved since I last saw it. You have made it truly enchanting. Look, sister, how everything speaks of taste! I do protest I should love to possess such a house—were it not for yourself, Mr. Palmer, would you not agree?”

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 4 ---
“We were indeed delighted to welcome them,” Mrs. Jennings murmured, leaning toward Elinor as if sharing a secret, though the room was otherwise silent. “Yet I cannot but wish they had not travelled so swiftly, nor made such a long journey, for they came round by London on account of some affair—something unbecoming of her situation. I urged her to stay and rest this morning, but she was eager to join us; her longing to see you all was irresistible.”

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 5 ---
“Here comes Marianne,” Sir John announced. “Palmer, prepare to be charmed by a monstrously pretty girl.”

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 6 ---
Sir John pressed the Dashwoods to spend the ensuing day in the park. Mrs. Dashwood, preferring her cottage to their frequent invitations, absolutely declined for herself, though she permitted her daughters to decide as they saw fit. The Palmers, however, showed no curiosity about their dining habits nor any inclination to engage with them. When they too attempted to excuse themselves, citing uncertain weather, Sir John’s insistence prevailed—the carriage was sent, and they were compelled to come. Lady Middleton, though she spared their mother, nevertheless urged the daughters. Mrs. Jennings and Mrs. Palmer joined the entreaties, all seemingly eager to avoid a family gathering; the young ladies were left with no choice but to yield.

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 7 ---
“Why should they ask us?” Marianne mused, as soon as the carriages had departed. “The cottage’s rent is said to be low, yet we are bound by terms that compel us to dine at the park whenever anyone stays—be it with them or with us.”

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 8 ---
“Their invitations are merely a mask of civility,” Elinor observed, “no less sincere now than they were weeks past. If their parties have grown tedious, the fault lies not with them but elsewhere. We must look for the change in other quarters.”

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 9 ---
“I am so glad to see you all!” she exclaimed, taking her seat between Elinor and Marianne. “The weather is so ill‑favoured I feared you might not come, which would have been most distressing, for we depart tomorrow. We must go, as the Westons will join us next week. --- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 10 ---

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 11 ---
Marianne offered no reply, her visage grave and unyielding.

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 12 ---
She remained utterly silent, though her countenance betrayed a keen interest in the conversation.

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 13 ---
“Mr. Palmer is so droll!” she whispered to Elinor. “He is perpetually out of humour.”

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 14 ---
After a moment’s observation, Elinor found herself disinclined to credit his professed ill‑nature or rudeness. Perhaps his temper had been soured by the realisation—common enough among his sex—that, despite a certain bias toward beauty, he had married a woman of limited understanding. Such blunders, she knew, rarely wound a sensible man to the core. More likely, she surmised, his disdainful treatment of others stemmed from a desire for distinction—a common, if unremarkable, motive. The means he employed might have succeeded in establishing superiority through ill‑breeding, but they could attach no one to him save his spouse.

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 15 ---
“My dear Miss Dashwood,” Mrs. Palmer remarked shortly thereafter, “I have a favour to request of you and your sister. Will you come and spend Christmas at Cleveland? Pray do, and come while the Westons are with us. You cannot imagine how happy I shall be! It will be utterly delightful!—My love, pray tell me, do you not long for the Miss Dashwoods’ presence at Cleveland?”

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 16 ---
Elinor could scarce contain the expression of her discomfort as she acquiesced to the burden of such an obligation.

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 17 ---
She surprised Elinor greatly when, upon their return to the drawing‑room, she asked whether Elinor found Mr. Palmer excessively agreeable.

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 18 ---
“Certainly,” Elinor responded, “he seems very amiable.”

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 19 ---
Elinor was again compelled to decline Mrs. Palmer’s invitation; by shifting the subject, she silenced the entreaties. She speculated that, as they shared a county, Mrs. Palmer might furnish more precise details concerning Willoughby’s character than the Middletons could provide. Her aim was to secure confirmation of his merits that might assuage Marianne’s anxieties. She began by inquiring whether they encountered Mr. Willoughby frequently at Cleveland, and whether they knew him intimately.

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 20 ---
“Oh, indeed,” Mrs. Palmer replied, “I know him exceedingly well—not that I have ever spoken to him, but I have seen him countless times in town. Somehow I never happened to be at Barton while he was at Allenham, nor were we ever in the country together. Mama saw him here once before; I was with my uncle at Weymouth. Nonetheless, I dare say we would have encountered him often in Somersetshire had not circumstances oddly prevented us from sharing that season. He is seldom at Combe, I believe; yet even if he were, Mr. Palmer would not visit him, for he is of the opposition and the distance is considerable. I know why you inquire of him; your sister is to marry him. I am overjoyed at the prospect, for then she will be my neighbour, as it were.”

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 21 ---
“By my faith,” Elinor replied, “you appear to know far more of the matter than I do, if you have any reason to anticipate such a match.”

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 22 ---
“But I assure you it was so, and I shall tell you how it came to pass. --- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 23 ---
“So do I,” she echoed. “He is such a charming man that it is a pity he should be so grave and dull. Mama says he was once in love with your sister, too. I assure you that would be a great compliment, for he rarely falls in love at all.”

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 24 ---
“Is Mr. Willoughby well known in your part of Somersetshire?” Elinor asked.

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 25 ---
“Oh, yes—extremely well, though perhaps not many people know him personally, because Combe Magna lies so far distant. Yet all agree he is exceedingly agreeable, I assure you. No one is more liked wherever he goes, and you may tell your sister she is monstrously fortunate to secure him, by my honour. --- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 26 ---
“I am so gratified we have finally become acquainted,” Charlotte continued. “And now I trust we shall ever be great friends. You cannot imagine how I longed to see you! It is so delightful that you dwell at the cottage! Nothing can equal it, to be sure! And I am so glad your sister is to be so well married. I hope you will visit Combe Magna often. --- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 27 ---
“It has been some while, ever since my sister married,” she reflected. “He was a particular friend of Sir John’s. I believe,” she added in a low voice, “he would have been very glad to have had me, could it have been arranged. Sir John and Lady Middleton wished it greatly. But Mama did not deem the match suitable for me, otherwise Sir John would have mentioned it to the Colonel, and we should have been wedded without delay.”

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 28 ---
“Did not Colonel Brandon know of Sir John’s proposal to your mother before it was made? Had he never professed affection to yourself?”

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 29 ---
The Palmers returned to Cleveland the following day, leaving the Dashwoods once more to entertain each other. This solitude, however, was short‑lived. Elinor had scarcely forgotten their recent visitors, nor wondered at Charlotte’s unexpected happiness, when Sir John and Mrs. Jennings, with their characteristic zeal for society, introduced fresh acquaintances for her to observe.

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 30 ---
The young ladies arrived, their appearance far from ungenteel or unfashionable. Their dress was smart, their manners civil, and they were instantly delighted with the house and its furnishings; they also proved adorably fond of children, securing Lady Middleton’s favour before an hour had elapsed. She declared them to be most agreeable girls indeed—a phrase that for her ladyship signified enthusiastic admiration. Sir John’s confidence in his own judgment grew with this praise, and he forthwith hastened to the cottage to inform the Miss Dashwoods of the Miss Steeles’ arrival, assuring them they were the sweetest girls in the world. Such commendation, however, yielded little insight; Elinor well knew that the sweetest girls could be found in every corner of England, under every variation of form, face, temper, and understanding. Sir John desired the entire family to walk directly to the park to view his guests. The benevolent philanthropist found it painful to keep a third cousin to himself.

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 31 ---
When their promised visit to the park and consequent introduction to these young ladies occurred, they found in the elder, who was near thirty and bore a plain, unsensible face, nothing to admire; but in the younger, not more than two or three and twenty, they acknowledged considerable beauty. Her features were pretty, her eye sharp and quick, and her air smartly distinguished, though it lacked true elegance or grace. Their manners were particularly civil, and Elinor soon credited them with a degree of sense when she observed how judiciously they endeavoured to please Lady Middleton. With her children they were in continual raptures, extolling their beauty, courting their notice, and humouring their whims; the time spared from these importunate demands was spent in admiration of whatever her ladyship was doing, if anything, or in sampling new dresses that had previously enchanted them. Fortunately for those who court through such foibles, a fond mother, though the most rapacious of beings in pursuit of praise for her offspring, is likewise the most credulous. Her demands are exorbitant, yet she will swallow anything; the Miss Steeles’ excessive affection and indulgence toward her children were therefore viewed by Lady Middleton without surprise or distrust. She beheld with maternal complacency all the impertinent encroachments and mischievous tricks to which her cousins submitted. She saw their sashes untied, their hair pulled about their ears, their work‑bags searched, and their knives and scissors stolen away, yet discerned no cause for concern, convinced of a mutual enjoyment. She noted no surprise that Elinor and Marianne could sit so composedly by, making no claim to a share in what was happening.

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 32 ---
Unfortunately, while bestowing these embraces, a pin in her ladyship’s head dress brushed the child’s neck, eliciting from this model of gentleness screams that scarcely could be outdone by any professed noisemaker. The mother’s consternation was excessive, yet it could not surpass the alarm of the Miss Steeles, and everything was done by all three, in that dire emergency, that affection could suggest to assuage the little sufferer’s agony. She was seated upon her mother’s lap, covered with kisses, her wound bathed with lavender‑water by one of the Miss Steeles, who knelt to attend her, and her mouth stuffed with sugar plums by the other. With such recompense for her tears, the child deemed it wise not to cease crying. She continued to scream and sob lustily, kicking her brothers for offering to touch her, and all their united soothing proved ineffectual until Lady Middleton, recollecting that apricot marmalade had succeeded in a similar distress the previous week, eagerly proposed the same remedy for this unfortunate scratch. A brief intermission of screams upon hearing it gave them hope it would not be rejected. She was therefore carried from the room in her mother’s arms in quest of this medicine, and as the two boys chose to follow—though earnestly entreated by their mother to stay behind—the four young ladies were left in a quietness the room had not known for many hours.

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 33 ---
“Yet I scarcely know how,” Marianne cried, “unless it had been under totally different circumstances. But this is the usual way of heightening alarm where there is nothing to be alarmed at in reality.”

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 34 ---
Marianne remained silent; it was impossible for her to utter what she did not feel, however trivial the occasion; thus the entire burden of deception, when politeness required it, fell upon Elinor. She did her best when thus called upon, speaking of Lady Middleton with more warmth than she truly felt, though far less than Miss Lucy did.

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 35 ---
“And Sir John too,” the elder sister declared, “what a charming man he is!”

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 36 ---
“And what a charming little family they have! I never saw such fine children in my life. I declare I quite doat upon them already, and indeed I am ever so fond of children.”

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 37 ---
“I should judge so,” Elinor replied with a smile, “from what I have witnessed this morning.”

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 38 ---
“I confess,” Elinor admitted, “that while I am at Barton Park, I never think of tame and quiet children with any abhorrence.”

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 39 ---
In some surprise at the familiarity of this question, or at least at the manner in which it was uttered, Elinor replied that she did.

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 40 ---
“We have heard Sir John admire it excessively,” Lucy remarked, seeming to think an apology necessary for her sister’s freedom.

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 41 ---
“I think every one must admire it,” Elinor responded, “who ever saw the place; though it is not to be supposed that any one can estimate its beauties as we do.”

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 42 ---
“But why should you think,” Lucy asked, looking ashamed of her sister, “that there are not as many genteel young men in Devonshire as in Sussex?”

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 43 ---
“Upon my word,” Elinor replied, “I cannot tell you, for I do not perfectly comprehend the meaning of the word. But this I can say, that if he ever was a beau before he married, he is one still, for there is not the smallest alteration in him.”

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 44 ---
“Lord! Anne,” her sister cried, “you can talk of nothing but beaux; you will make Miss Dashwood believe you think of nothing else.” And then, to change the subject, she began admiring the house and its furnishings.

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 45 ---
This specimen of the Miss Steeles was sufficient. The vulgar freedom and folly of the elder left her no recommendation, and as Elinor was not blinded by the younger’s beauty or shrewd look to her want of genuine elegance and artlessness, she departed the house without any desire to know them better.

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 46 ---
Not so the Miss Steeles. They arrived from Exeter, well supplied with admiration for the use of Sir John Middleton, his family, and all his relations, and no niggardly proportion was now dealt out to his fair cousins, whom they declared to be the most beautiful, elegant, accomplished, and agreeable girls they had ever beheld, and with whom they were particularly anxious to be better acquainted. And to be better acquainted, Elinor soon found, was their inevitable lot, for as Sir John entirely sided with the Miss Steeles, their party would be too strong for opposition, and that kind of intimacy consisting of sitting an hour or two together in the same room almost daily must be submitted to. Sir John could do no more; but he did not know that any more was required: to be together, in his opinion, was to be intimate, and while his continual schemes for their meeting were effectual, he entertained no doubt of their becoming established friends.

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 47 ---
To do him justice, he did everything in his power to promote their unreserve, by making the Miss Steeles acquainted with whatever he knew or conjectured concerning his cousins’ situations in the most delicate particulars; and Elinor had not seen them more than twice before the elder of them complimented her on her sister’s having been so fortunate as to make a conquest of a very smart beau since her arrival at Barton.

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 48 ---
Elinor could not suppose that Sir John would be more reticent in proclaiming his suspicions of her regard for Edward than he had been with respect to Marianne; indeed it was rather his favourite jest of the two, as being somewhat newer and more conjectural; and since Edward’s visit, they had never dined together without his drinking to her best affections with so much significance and so many nods and winks as to excite general attention. The letter F—had likewise been invariably brought forward, and found productive of countless jokes, that its character as the wittiest letter in the alphabet had long been established with Elinor.

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 49 ---
The Miss Steeles, as she anticipated, now reaped all the benefit of these jokes, and in the elder of them they raised a curiosity to know the name of the gentleman alluded to, which, though often impertinently expressed, was perfectly in keeping with her general inquisitiveness into the concerns of their family. But Sir John did not sport long with the curiosity which he delighted to raise, for he had at least as much pleasure in telling the name as Miss Steele had in hearing it.

--- sense_and_sensibility | Sisterhood | Paragraph 50 ---
“Ferrars!” Miss Steele repeated. “Mr. Ferrars is the happy man, is he? What! your sister‑in‑law’s brother, Miss Dashwood? a very agreeable young man to be sure; I know him very well.”

--- sense_and_sensibility | Love and Heartbreak | Paragraph 1 ---
"Did you sleep, my dear?" inquired his lady, with a smile playing upon her lips.

--- sense_and_sensibility | Love and Heartbreak | Paragraph 2 ---
"Oh, Mr. Palmer!" exclaimed his wife as he crossed the threshold. "You absolutely must assist me in prevailing upon the Miss Dashwoods to honor us with their presence in town this winter."

--- sense_and_sensibility | Love and Heartbreak | Paragraph 3 ---
Her husband offered no reply; merely inclining his head toward the ladies, he forthwith commenced a dissertation upon the disagreeableness of the atmosphere.

--- sense_and_sensibility | Love and Heartbreak | Paragraph 4 ---
"My love, you oppose everybody," observed his spouse, her customary mirth bubbling forth. "Are you aware that you are the very personification of incivility?"

--- sense_and_sensibility | Love and Heartbreak | Paragraph 5 ---
"My dear Miss Dashwood," cried Mrs. Palmer, a short while later, "I have a petition of the utmost importance to lay before you and your sister. Will you not grace Cleveland with your company this Christmas? Do, pray, consent—and arrive whilst the Westons remain under our roof. The felicity it would give me is beyond expression! It would be perfectly delightful!" Turning to her consort, she added, "My love, do you not ardently desire the Miss Dashwoods at Cleveland?"

--- sense_and_sensibility | Love and Heartbreak | Paragraph 6 ---
"So do I. He is a most charming man, which renders his gravity and dullness a thousand pities. Mama declares *he* was once enamored of your sister also. I assure you, it was no small compliment, for he is scarcely ever smitten."

--- sense_and_sensibility | Love and Heartbreak | Paragraph 7 ---
"Was Colonel Brandon privy to Sir John's overture to your mother ere it was tendered? Had he never disclosed his regard to yourself?"

--- sense_and_sensibility | Love and Heartbreak | Paragraph 8 ---
When the long-awaited visit to the Park and the consequent introduction to these young ladies finally occurred, they discovered in the elder—nearly thirty, possessed of a remarkably plain and unintelligent countenance—nothing to commend; but in the younger, scarce three-and-twenty, they were compelled to acknowledge no inconsiderable share of beauty. Her features were agreeable, her eye sharp and lively, and a certain smartness of air, though lacking true elegance or grace, conferred distinction upon her person. Their manners were remarkably civil, and Elinor soon granted them some measure of sense, perceiving with what constant and judicious assiduity they labored to render themselves agreeable to Lady Middleton. Toward her children they were in perpetual ecstasies, extolling their charms, soliciting their notice, and humoring their every caprice; and such time as could be spared from the importunate demands of this politeness was devoted to admiring whatever her ladyship might be doing, or to soliciting patterns of some elegant new gown in which her appearance on the previous day had thrown them into transports. Fortunately for those who pay court through such foibles, a fond mother—though, in the pursuit of praise for her offspring, the most rapacious of human beings—is likewise the most credulous; her demands are exorbitant, yet she will swallow any flattery. The excessive devotion and endurance of the Miss Steeles toward her progeny were therefore regarded by Lady Middleton without the smallest surprise or suspicion. She beheld with maternal complacency all the impertinent liberties and mischievous tricks to which her cousins submitted. She saw their sashes untied, their hair disordered, their work-bags ransacked, and their knives and scissors pilfered, and felt no doubt of a mutual enjoyment. It occasioned no other wonder than that Elinor and Marianne could sit by so composedly, without claiming a share in the fray.

--- sense_and_sensibility | Love and Heartbreak | Paragraph 9 ---
But, unfortunately, in bestowing these embraces, a pin in her ladyship's headdress slightly grazed the child's neck, eliciting from this paragon of gentleness such violent shrieks as could scarcely be exceeded by a professed screamer. The mother's consternation was excessive, yet it could not surpass the alarm of the Miss Steeles; and every expedient which affection could dictate was employed by all three, in so critical an emergency, to assuage the agonies of the little sufferer. She was seated in her mother's lap, smothered in kisses, her wound bathed in lavender-water by one sister on her knees, and her mouth crammed with sugar-plums by the other. With such a reward for her tears, the child was too wise to cease crying. She still screamed and sobbed lustily, kicked her brothers for daring to approach, and all their united soothings proved ineffectual till Lady Middleton, luckily recollecting that apricot marmalade had successfully treated a bruised temple the week before, eagerly proposed the same remedy for this unfortunate scratch. A slight pause in the young lady's shrieks upon hearing it gave them hope it would not be rejected. She was borne from the room, therefore, in her mother's arms, in quest of this specific; and as the two boys chose to follow, though earnestly entreated to remain, the four young ladies were left in a tranquility the room had not known for hours.

--- sense_and_sensibility | Love and Heartbreak | Paragraph 10 ---
"I have a notion," said Lucy, "that you think the little Middletons somewhat over-indulged; perhaps they *are* the outside of enough; but it is so natural in Lady Middleton. For my part, I love to see children full of life and spirits; I cannot abide them if they are tame and quiet."

--- sense_and_sensibility | Love and Heartbreak | Paragraph 11 ---
"Certainly," answered Elinor, scarce knowing what she uttered; but after a moment's reflection, she added, with a revived conviction of Edward's honor and affection, and her companion's falsehood—"Engaged to Mr. Edward Ferrars! I confess myself so totally astonished at your intelligence that—I beg your pardon; but surely there must be some mistake of person or name. We cannot possibly mean the same Mr. Ferrars."

--- sense_and_sensibility | Love and Heartbreak | Paragraph 12 ---
"Yes; and heaven knows how much longer we may be condemned to wait. Poor Edward! It quite disheartens him." Then producing a small miniature from her pocket, she continued, "To obviate all possibility of mistake, be so good as to regard this face. It does him scant justice, to be sure, yet I think you cannot be deceived as to the person for whom it was executed. I have possessed it above these three years."

--- sense_and_sensibility | Love and Heartbreak | Paragraph 13 ---
"I feared you would think I took a great liberty," said she, "in imparting all this. I have not known you long, to be sure, personally at least, but I have known you and all your family by description a great while; and the moment I saw you, I felt almost as if you were an old acquaintance. Besides, in the present case, I really thought some explanation due to you after my making such particular inquiries concerning Edward's mother. And I am so unfortunate as to have not a creature whose counsel I may ask. Anne is the only person privy to it, and she has no judgment whatever; indeed, she does me a great deal more harm than good, for I live in constant dread of her betraying me. She knows not how to hold her tongue, as you must perceive, and I am sure I was in the greatest fright imaginable t'other day when Edward's name was mentioned by Sir John, lest she should blurt it all out. You cannot conceive how much I endure in my mind from it altogether. I only wonder I am alive after what I have suffered for Edward's sake these last four years. Everything in such suspense and uncertainty; and seeing him so seldom—we can scarcely meet above twice a year. I am sure I wonder my heart is not quite broken."

--- sense_and_sensibility | Love and Heartbreak | Paragraph 14 ---
"Sometimes," continued Lucy, having dried her eyes, "I think whether it would not be better for us both to break off the matter entirely." As she spoke, she fixed her gaze directly upon her companion. "But then, at other times, I have not resolution enough for it. I cannot bear the thought of making him so miserable as I know the mere mention of such a thing would do. And on my own account too—so dear as he is to me—I don't think I could be equal to it. What would you advise me to do in such a case, Miss Dashwood? What would *you* do yourself?"

--- sense_and_sensibility | Love and Heartbreak | Paragraph 15 ---
Elinor recognized the handwriting; doubt was no longer possible. This picture, she had permitted herself to hope, might have been accidentally obtained; it might not have been Edward's gift. But a correspondence by letter could subsist only under a positive engagement, authorized by nothing else. For a few moments she was nearly overcome—her heart sank within her, and she could scarcely stand; but exertion was indispensable. She struggled so resolutely against the oppression of her feelings that her success was speedy and, for the time, complete.

--- sense_and_sensibility | Love and Heartbreak | Paragraph 16 ---
However slight Elinor's general reliance on Lucy's veracity might be, it was impossible, upon serious reflection, to suspect it in the present instance, where no temptation could answer for the folly of inventing a falsehood of such a nature. What Lucy had asserted as truth, therefore, Elinor could not, dared not, longer doubt; supported as it was on every side by such probabilities and proofs, and contradicted by nothing but her own wishes. Their opportunity of acquaintance in Mr. Pratt's house was a foundation for the rest at once indisputable and alarming; and Edward's visit near Plymouth, his melancholy state of mind, his dissatisfaction with his own prospects, his uncertain behavior toward herself, the intimate knowledge of the Miss Steeles concerning Norland and their family connections—which had often surprised her—the picture, the letter, the ring, formed altogether such a body of evidence as overcame every fear of condemning him unfairly, and established as a fact which no partiality could set aside his ill-treatment of herself. Her resentment of such behavior, her indignation at having been its dupe, for a short time made her feel only for herself; but other ideas, other considerations, soon arose. Had Edward been intentionally deceiving her? Had he feigned a regard he did not feel? Was his engagement to Lucy an engagement of the heart? No; whatever it might once have been, she could not believe it such at present. His affection was all her own. She could not be deceived in that. Her mother, sisters, Fanny, all had been conscious of his regard for her at Norland; it was no illusion of her own vanity. He certainly loved her. What a softener of the heart was this persuasion! How much could it not tempt her to forgive! He had been blamable, highly blamable, in remaining at Norland after he first felt her influence over him to be more than it ought to be. In that, he could not be defended; but if he had injured her, how much more had he injured himself; if her case were pitiable, his was hopeless. His imprudence had made her miserable for a season; but it seemed to have deprived *himself* of all chance of ever being otherwise. She might in time regain tranquillity; but *he*—what had *he* to look forward to? Could he ever be tolerably happy with Lucy Steele; could he, were his affection for herself out of the question, with his integrity, his delicacy, and well-informed mind, be satisfied with a wife like her—illiterate, artful, and selfish?

--- sense_and_sensibility | Love and Heartbreak | Paragraph 17 ---
If, in the supposition of his seeking to marry herself, his difficulties from his mother had seemed great, how much greater were they now likely to be, when the object of his engagement was undoubtedly inferior in connections, and probably inferior in fortune to herself? These difficulties, indeed, with a heart so alienated from Lucy, might not press very hard upon his patience; but melancholy was the state of the person by whom the expectation of family opposition and unkindness could be felt as a relief!

--- sense_and_sensibility | Love and Heartbreak | Paragraph 18 ---
As these considerations crowded upon her in painful succession, she wept for *him*, more than for herself. Supported by the conviction of having done nothing to merit her present unhappiness, and consoled by the belief that Edward had done nothing to forfeit her esteem, she thought she could even now, under the first smart of the heavy blow, command herself enough to guard every suspicion of the truth from her mother and sisters. And so well did she answer her own expectations that when she joined them at dinner only two hours after she had first suffered the extinction of all her dearest hopes, no one would have supposed from the appearance of the sisters that Elinor was mourning in secret over obstacles which must divide her forever from the object of her love, and that Marianne was internally dwelling on the perfections of a man of whose whole heart she felt thoroughly possessed, and whom she expected to see in every carriage that drove near their house.

--- sense_and_sensibility | Love and Heartbreak | Paragraph 19 ---
The necessity of concealing from her mother and Marianne what had been entrusted to her in confidence, though it obliged her to unceasing exertion, was no aggravation of Elinor's distress. On the contrary, it was a relief to be spared the communication of what would give such affliction to them, and to be saved likewise from hearing that condemnation of Edward which would probably flow from the excess of their partial affection for herself, and which was more than she felt equal to support.

--- sense_and_sensibility | Love and Heartbreak | Paragraph 20 ---
From their counsel or their conversation she knew she could receive no assistance; their tenderness and sorrow must add to her distress, while her self-command would neither receive encouragement from their example nor from their praise. She was stronger alone; and her own good sense so well supported her that her firmness was as unshaken, her appearance of cheerfulness as invariable, as with regrets so poignant and so fresh it was possible for them to be.

--- sense_and_sensibility | Love and Heartbreak | Paragraph 21 ---
Much as she had suffered from her first conversation with Lucy on the subject, she soon felt an earnest wish of renewing it; and this for more reasons than one. She desired to hear many particulars of their engagement repeated, she desired more clearly to understand what Lucy *really* felt for Edward, whether there were any sincerity in her declaration of tender regard for him, and she particularly desired to convince Lucy, by her readiness to enter on the matter again and her calmness in conversing on it, that she was no otherwise interested in it than as a friend—which she very much feared her involuntary agitation in their morning discourse must have left at least doubtful. That Lucy was disposed to be jealous of her appeared very probable. It was plain that Edward had always spoken highly in her praise, not merely from Lucy's assertion, but from her venturing to trust her, on so short a personal acquaintance, with a secret so confessedly and evidently important. And even Sir John's joking intelligence must have had some weight. But indeed, while Elinor remained so well assured within herself of being really beloved by Edward, it required no other consideration of probabilities to make it natural that Lucy should be jealous; and that she was so, her very confidence was a proof. What other reason for the disclosure of the affair could there be, but that Elinor might be informed by it of Lucy's superior claims on Edward, and be taught to avoid him in future? She had little difficulty in penetrating thus much of her rival's intentions, and while she was firmly resolved to act toward her as every principle of honor and honesty directed—to combat her own affection for Edward and to see him as little as possible—she could not deny herself the comfort of endeavoring to convince Lucy that her heart was unwounded. And as she could now have nothing more painful to hear on the subject than had already been told, she did not mistrust her own ability of going through a repetition of particulars with composure.

--- sense_and_sensibility | Love and Heartbreak | Paragraph 27 ---

--- sense_and_sensibility | Love and Heartbreak | Paragraph 29 ---

--- sense_and_sensibility | Love and Heartbreak | Paragraph 30 ---
Elinor was soon summoned to the card-table by the conclusion of the first rubber, and the confidential discourse of the two ladies was therefore at an end, to which both submitted without reluctance; for nothing had been said on either side to make them dislike each other less than they had done before. And Elinor sat down to the card-table with the melancholy persuasion that Edward was not only without affection for the person who was to be his wife, but that he had not even the chance of being tolerably happy in marriage, which sincere affection on *her* side would have given; for self-interest alone could induce a woman to keep a man to an engagement of which she seemed so thoroughly aware that he was weary.

--- sense_and_sensibility | Love and Heartbreak | Paragraph 31 ---

We three shall be able to go very well in my chaise; and when we are in town, if you do not like to go wherever I do, well and good, you may always go with one of my daughters. --- sense_and_sensibility | Love and Heartbreak | Paragraph 36 ---

--- sense_and_sensibility | Love and Heartbreak | Paragraph 37 ---
These words, which conveyed to Elinor a direct avowal of his love for her sister, affected her very much. She was not immediately able to speak, and even when her spirits were recovered, she debated for a short time on the answer it would be most proper to give. The real state of things between Willoughby and her sister was so little known to herself that in endeavoring to explain it she might be as liable to say too much as too little. Yet as she was convinced that Marianne's affection for Willoughby could leave no hope of Colonel Brandon's success, whatever the event of that affection might be, and at the same time wished to shield her conduct from censure, she thought it most prudent and kind, after some consideration, to say more than she really knew or believed. She acknowledged, therefore, that though she had never been informed by themselves of the terms on which they stood with each other, of their mutual affection she had no doubt, and of their correspondence she was not astonished to hear.

--- sense_and_sensibility | Love and Heartbreak | Paragraph 38 ---
At last he turned round again and regarded them both. She started up, and pronouncing his name in a tone of affection, held out her hand to him. --- sense_and_sensibility | Love and Heartbreak | Paragraph 43 ---

--- emma | Social Status | Paragraph 1 ---
Emma Woodhouse, endued with such attractions as beauty and intelligence, and blessed with considerable fortune, found herself in possession of many of life’s most desirable advantages. In her twenty-first year, she had enjoyed so few vexations or cares that one might almost conjecture her days were but lightly spent.

--- emma | Social Status | Paragraph 2 ---
Yet her real imperfections lay in the excess of liberty she possessed, and a sanguine self-regard bordering on vanity; these shortcomings, however, were wholly invisible to herself, and thus remained unacknowledged as sources of blemish in her existence.

--- emma | Social Status | Paragraph 5 ---

When engaged in needlework, she takes care to turn the door-latch correctly and never bangs it—qualities that bespeak a mind not unacquainted with propriety. Thus, she would solace poor Miss Taylor’s loneliness, and her presence would be greatly valued by her brother James, who frequently visits from London, sharing news of our household.’

--- emma | Social Status | Paragraph 6 ---
Mr. Knightley, a gentleman of sound judgment and advanced years, was not merely a cherished ally but also closely connected to the family through his sister’s marriage. His inquiries about ‘poor Isabella’ and her children were richly answered, and Mr. Woodhouse, ever grateful for such kindness, remarked, ‘It is most exceeding kindness of you, Sir, to trouble yourself on our account. You must have endured a severe walk in coming hither.’

--- emma | Social Status | Paragraph 9 ---

--- emma | Social Status | Paragraph 10 ---

--- emma | Social Status | Paragraph 11 ---

Miss Bates’s singular charm lay in her unassuming popularity; her lack of intellect or beauty, indeed, left her vulnerable to censure, yet her fervent affection and constant gratitude rendered her beloved by all.

Not a mind of surpassing wit lit Miss Smith’s discourse, yet she was so charmingly modest, so gracious in her deference, so appreciative in her wonder at Hartfield’s splendour—that Emma concluded she possessed keen wit, and must be encouraged. Her beauty and sensibility deserved a loftier sphere than Highbury’s common run. Emma would draw her forth, elevate her tastes, and place her amid society’s elites—her undertaking most becoming!

--- emma | Social Status | Paragraph 46 ---
Lost in admiration of Miss Smith’s eyes and charms, Emma’s evening raced swift. When the supper table, long her watchword, arrived, she swept through the feast with such zeal as even her father’s early hour permitted, presenting delicacies with fervour most welcome.

--- emma | Social Status | Paragraph 47 ---
Hartfield’s school and its matron dictated much discourse—had not the Martins of Abbey-Mill Farm intruded, the topic might have consumed the entire night. Emma, ever eager, encouraged Miss Goddard’s tales of Mrs. Martin’s “two parlours, two great ones!”, her maid of twenty-five years, and their eight cows, including an Alderney and a Welch heifer, adored as her own. The summerhouse planned for summer tea-parties brightened Emma’s thoughts.

--- emma | Social Status | Paragraph 48 ---
‘But pray, what is Mr. Martin’s countenance like?’

--- emma | Social Status | Paragraph 49 ---
Captain Weston’s universal popularity was no marvel; his union with Miss Churchill, daughter of Yorkshire nobility, though contrary to her brother’s wishes, drew no surprise—for all knew his amiability and the respect he owed.

--- emma | Social Status | Paragraph 50 ---
Mrs. Goddard managed a school—not a pretentious seminary, but a honest, venerable boarding house where girls earned meagre accomplishments at modest cost, escaping the perils of fancy and pride. Her venue, famed for health, offered wholesome fare, liberty in summer, and home-based cures for chilblains. Such repute drew ten young couples to her door daily. A motherly soul, unpretentious yet beloved, she owed debts of gratitude to Mr. Woodhouse, whose kindness to her youth had earned her frequent visits to his parlour, where she’d win or lose a shilling by his fireside.

--- emma | Female Independence | Paragraph 1 ---
She was the youngest of the two daughters of an indulgent, affectionate father; and, by the very early marriage of her elder sister, she had successively become the mistress of his house. Her mother, long deceased, left her only a dim recollection of tender caresses, which the place of a mother supplied by an excellent governess, whose affection approached that of a parent.

--- emma | Female Independence | Paragraph 2 ---
The event held out every promise of felicity to her friend. Mr. Weston, a man of unimpeachable character, ample fortune, fitting age, and agreeable manners, seemed the very embodiment of what she had long desired and cultivated. Yet the prospect proved a somber morning for her, for the absence of Miss Taylor would be felt at every hour of the day. She called to mind the kindness of sixteen years—the affection, the careful instruction, the diversions of childhood—how Miss Taylor had devoted herself to the little girl’s health and comfort, and had nursed her through the various ailments of infancy. A deep debt of gratitude was owed, but the friendship of the preceding seven years, the easy intimacy and perfect unreserve that followed after Isabella’s marriage, was still the dearer and more tender recollection. Miss Taylor had been a companion such as few ever possess: sensible, well‑informed, useful, gentle, intimately acquainted with every domestic concern, and especially devoted to her own pleasures and schemes—one to whom she might pour forth every thought as it arose, and whose affection for her was incapable of finding fault.

--- emma | Female Independence | Paragraph 3 ---
“I pledge you, sir, to make no match for myself,” she said, “but I must for others. It is the most delightful amusement in the world! And after such success—my dear papa—every body declared that Mr. Weston would never marry again. Oh dear, no! Mr. Weston, who had long been a widower, and who seemed perfectly content without a wife, who was always occupied either in his business or among his friends, always agreeable wherever he went, always cheerful—Mr. Weston need never spend a single evening alone if he chose otherwise. No! Mr. Weston will certainly never marry again. Some talk of a promise made to his late wife, others of the son and uncle refusing him; all sorts of solemn nonsense is spoken, but I believe none of it.”

--- emma | Female Independence | Paragraph 4 ---
“Only one more, sir,” she added, “only for Mr. Elton. Poor Mr. Elton! You like Mr. Elton, sir,—I must look about for a wife for him. There is no one in Highbury who deserves him—and he has been here a whole year, and has fitted up his house so comfortably, that it would be a pity to let him remain single any longer—and I thought when he was joining their hands today he looked so very much as if he would like to have the same kind office done for him! I think very highly of Mr. Elton, and this is the only way I have of doing him a service.”

--- emma | Female Independence | Paragraph 5 ---
Miss Churchill, being of age and in full command of her fortune—though that fortune bore no proportion to the family estate—was not to be dissuaded from marrying, and the marriage took place to the infinite mortification of Mr. and Mrs. Churchill, who cast her off with due decorum. It was an unsuitable connection and produced little happiness. Mrs. Weston ought to have found more in it, for she had a husband whose warm heart and sweet temper made him think of everything due to her in return for the singular goodness of loving him; yet, though she possessed a certain spirit, she lacked the best. She possessed resolution enough to pursue her own will in spite of her brother, but not enough to refrain from unreasonable regrets at his unreasonable anger, nor from missing the luxuries of her former home. They lived beyond their means, yet it was nothing compared with Enscombe: she never ceased to love her husband, but she longed instantly to be the wife of Captain Weston, and Miss Churchill of Enscombe.

--- emma | Female Independence | Paragraph 6 ---
It was now some time since Miss Taylor had begun to influence his schemes; however, it was not the tyrannical influence of youth over youth that had shaken his resolution to settle only when he could purchase Randalls, and the sale of Randalls was long looked forward to; yet he advanced steadily toward that object until it was accomplished. He had made his fortune, bought his house, obtained his wife, and was beginning a new period of existence, with every probability of greater happiness than any that had hitherto passed. He had never been an unhappy man; his temper had secured him from such a fate even in his first marriage, but his second marriage would demonstrate how delightful a well‑judging, truly amiable woman could be, and would afford him the pleasantest proof that it is better to choose than to be chosen, to excite gratitude than to feel it.

--- emma | Female Independence | Paragraph 7 ---
He had only himself to please in his choice: his fortune was his own; for, as to Frank, it was more than being tacitly brought up as his uncle’s heir—it had become an avowed adoption, whereby he was to assume the name of Churchill on coming of age. It was therefore most unlikely that he would ever seek his father’s assistance. His father had no apprehension of it. The aunt was a capricious woman, and governed her husband entirely; yet it was not in Mr. Weston’s nature to imagine that any caprice could be strong enough to affect one so dear, and, as he believed, so deservedly dear. He saw his son each year in London, and was proud of him; and his favorable report of him as a very fine young man had made Highbury feel a sort of pride in him too. He was regarded as sufficiently belonging to the place to make his merits and prospects a common concern.

--- emma | Female Independence | Paragraph 8 ---
She knew that, at times, she must be missed; and she could not think, without pain, of Emma losing a single pleasure, or suffering an hour’s ennui, for want of her companionable presence. Yet dear Emma was no feeble creature; she was more equal to her situation than most girls would have been, and possessed sense, energy, and spirits that might be hoped would carry her well and happily through its little difficulties and privations. Moreover, the very easy distance of Randalls from Hartfield afforded convenient walks for a solitary female, and Mr. Weston’s disposition and circumstances would make the approaching season no hindrance to his spending half the evenings of the week together.

--- emma | Female Independence | Paragraph 9 ---
Mr. Woodhouse was fond of society in his own way. He much enjoyed the arrival of his friends, and, by a variety of circumstances—his long residence at Hartfield, his good nature, his fortune, his house, and his daughter—he could, in a great measure, command the visits of his own little circle as he liked. He had little intercourse with families beyond that circle; his horror of late hours and large dinner‑parties rendered him unfit for any acquaintanceship but such as would visit him on his own terms. Fortunately for him, Highbury, encompassing Randalls in the same parish and Donwell Abbey in the adjoining one, the seat of Mr. Knightley, comprised many such acquaintances. Not unfrequently, through Emma’s persuasion, some of the best and most chosen families dined with him; but evening parties were what he preferred, and unless he fancied himself unequal to company, there was scarcely an evening in the week in which Emma could not form a card‑table for him.

--- emma | Female Independence | Paragraph 10 ---
Real, long‑standing regard brought the Westons and Mr. Knightley together; and, by Mr. Elton, a young man living alone without liking it, the privilege of exchanging any vacant evening of his own blank solitude for the elegancies and society of Mr. Woodhouse’s drawing‑room, and the smiles of his lovely daughter, was in no danger of being thrown away.

--- emma | Female Independence | Paragraph 11 ---
Mrs. Bates, the widow of a former vicar of Highbury, was a very old lady, almost past everything but tea and quadrille. She lived with her single daughter in a very narrow way, and was considered with all the regard and respect which a harmless old lady, under such untoward circumstances, can excite. Her daughter enjoyed a most uncommon degree of popularity for a woman neither young, handsome, rich, nor married. Miss Bates stood in the very worst predicament in the world for having much of the public favour; and she possessed no intellectual superiority to make atonement to herself, or to frighten those who might hate her into outward respect. She had never boasted either beauty or cleverness. Her youth passed without distinction, and her middle age was devoted to the care of a failing mother, and to the endeavour of making a small income stretch as far as possible. Yet she was a happy woman, and one whom no one named without good‑will. It was her universal good‑will and contented temper that worked such wonders. She loved everybody, was interested in everybody’s happiness, quick‑sighted to everybody’s merits; thought herself the most fortunate creature, and surrounded with blessings—an excellent mother, many good neighbours and friends, and a home that wanted for nothing. The simplicity and cheerfulness of her nature, her contented and grateful spirit, were a recommendation to everybody, and a mine of felicity to herself. She was a great talker upon little matters, which exactly suited Mr. Woodhouse, full of trivial communications and harmless gossip.

--- emma | Female Independence | Paragraph 12 ---
Mrs. Goddard was the mistress of a school—not a seminary or an establishment that, in florid nonsense, professed to combine liberal acquirements with elegant morality upon new principles and systems—nor a place where young ladies, for enormous pay, were screwed out of health and into vanity—but a genuine, old‑fashioned board‑school, where a reasonable quantity of accomplishments was sold at a reasonable price, and where girls might be sent to be out of the way, and scramble themselves into a little education, without any danger of returning as prodigies. Mrs. Goddard’s school was in high repute—and well deserved; for Highbury was reckoned a particularly healthy spot: she possessed an ample house and garden, gave the children plenty of wholesome food, let them run about a great deal in summer, and in winter dressed their chilblains with her own hands. No wonder that a train of twenty young couples now walked after her to church. She was a plain, motherly sort of woman, who had worked hard in youth, and now thought herself entitled to the occasional holiday of a tea‑visit; and, having once owed much to Mr. Woodhouse’s kindness, felt his particular claim on her to leave her neat parlour, hung round with fancy‑work, whenever she could, and win or lose a few sixpences by his fireside.

--- emma | Female Independence | Paragraph 13 ---
As she sat one morning, looking forward to exactly such a close of the present day, a note was brought from Mrs. Goddard, requesting, in the most respectful terms, to be allowed to bring Miss Smith with her—a most welcome request: for Miss Smith was a girl of seventeen, whom Emma knew very well by sight, and had long felt an interest in, on account of her beauty. A very gracious invitation was returned, and the evening was no longer dreaded by the fair mistress of the mansion.

--- emma | Female Independence | Paragraph 14 ---
Harriet Smith’s intimacy at Hartfield soon became a settled thing. Quick and decided in her ways, Emma lost no time in inviting, encouraging, and telling her to come very often; and as their acquaintance increased, so did their satisfaction in each other. As a walking companion, Emma had very early foreseen how useful she might find her. In that respect Mrs. Weston’s loss had been important. Her father never went beyond the shrubbery, where two divisions of the ground sufficed him for his long walk, or for his short one, as the season varied; and since Mrs. Weston’s marriage her exercise had been too much confined. She had ventured once alone to Randalls, but it was not pleasant; and a Harriet Smith, therefore, one whom she could summon at any time to a walk, would be a valuable addition to her privileges. But in every respect, as she saw more of her, she approved her, and was confirmed in all her kind designs.

--- emma | Female Independence | Paragraph 15 ---
Harriet certainly was not clever, but she possessed a sweet, docile, grateful disposition, was altogether free from conceit, and only desired to be guided by anyone she looked up to. Her early attachment to herself was very amiable; and her inclination for good company, and power of appreciating what was elegant and clever, showed that there was no want of taste, though strength of understanding could not be expected. Altogether she was quite convinced of Harriet Smith’s being exactly the young friend she wanted—exactly the something which her home required. Such a friend as Mrs. Weston was out of the question. Two such could never be granted. Two such she did not want. It was a different sort of thing, a sentiment distinct and independent. Mrs. Weston was the object of a regard founded on gratitude and esteem. Harriet would be loved as one to whom she could be useful. For Mrs. Weston there was nothing to be done; for Harriet everything.

--- emma | Female Independence | Paragraph 16 ---
For some time she was amused, without thinking beyond the immediate cause; but as she came to understand the family better, other feelings arose. She had taken up a wrong notion, fancying it was a mother and daughter, a son and son’s wife, who all lived together; but when it appeared that Mr. Martin, who bore a part in the narrative and was always mentioned with approbation for his great good‑nature in doing something or other, was a single man; that there was no young Mrs. Martin, no wife in the case; she began to suspect danger to her poor little friend from all this hospitality and kindness, and that, if she were not taken care of, she might be required to sink herself forever.

--- emma | Female Independence | Paragraph 17 ---
“Well, and that is as early as most men can afford to marry, who are not born to an independence,” she added. “Mr. Martin, I imagine, has his fortune entirely to make—cannot be at all before the world. Whatever money he may come into when his father dies, whatever his share of the family property, it is, I dare say, all afloat, all employed in his stock, and so forth; and though, with diligence and good luck, he may be rich in time, it is next to impossible that he should have realised anything yet.”

--- emma | Female Independence | Paragraph 18 ---
“You understand the force of influence pretty well, Harriet; but I would have you so firmly established in good society, as to be independent even of Hartfield and Miss Woodhouse. --- emma | Female Independence | Paragraph 19 ---
“Ah! poor Miss Taylor! ’Tis a sad business.”

--- emma | Female Independence | Paragraph 20 ---
Emma turned away her head, divided between tears and smiles. “It is impossible that Emma should not miss such a companion,” said Mr. Knightley. “We should not like her so well as we do, sir, if we could suppose it; but she knows how much the marriage is to Miss Taylor’s advantage; she knows how very acceptable it must be, at Miss Taylor’s time of life, to be settled in a home of her own, and how important to her to be secure of a comfortable provision, and therefore cannot allow herself to feel so much pain as pleasure. Every friend of Miss Taylor must be glad to have her so happily married.”

--- emma | Female Independence | Paragraph 21 ---
Sorrow came—a gentle sorrow—but not in the shape of any disagreeable consciousness.—Miss Taylor married. It was Miss Taylor’s loss which first brought grief. It was on the wedding‑day of this beloved friend that Emma first sat in mournful thought of any continuation. The wedding over, and the bride‑people gone, her father and herself were left to dine together, with no prospect of a third to cheer a long evening. Her father composed himself to sleep after dinner, as usual, and she then had only to sit and think of what she had lost.

--- emma | Female Independence | Paragraph 22 ---
Her situation was altogether the subject of hours of gratitude to Mrs. Weston, and of moments only of regret; and her satisfaction—her more than satisfaction—her cheerful enjoyment, was so just and so apparent, that Emma, well as she knew her father, was sometimes taken by surprise at his being still able to pity “poor Miss Taylor,” when they left her at Randalls in the centre of every domestic comfort, or saw her go away in the evening attended by her pleasant husband to a carriage of her own. But never did she go without Mr. Woodhouse’s giving a gentle sigh, and saying, “Ah, poor Miss Taylor! She would be very glad to stay.”

--- emma | Female Independence | Paragraph 23 ---
How was she to bear the change?—It was true that her friend was going only half a mile from them; but Emma was aware that great must be the difference between a Mrs. Weston, only half a mile from them, and a Miss Taylor in the house; and with all her advantages, natural and domestic, she was now in great danger of suffering from intellectual solitude. She dearly loved her father, but he was no companion for her. He could not meet her in conversation, rational or playful.

--- emma | Female Independence | Paragraph 26 ---
She was so busy in admiring those soft blue eyes, in talking and listening, and forming all these schemes in the in‑betweens, that the evening flew away at a very unusual rate; and the supper‑table, which always closed such parties, and for which she had been used to sit and watch the due time, was all set out and ready, and moved forwards to the fire, before she was aware. With an alacrity beyond the common impulse of a spirit which yet was never indifferent to the credit of doing everything well and attentively, with the real good‑will of a mind delighted with its own ideas, did she then do all the honours of the meal, and help and recommend the minced chicken and scalloped oysters, with an urgency which she knew would be acceptable to the early hours and civil scruples of their guests.

The misfortune of your birth ought to make you particularly careful as to your associates. There can be no doubt of your being a gentleman’s daughter, and you must support your claim to that station by every thing within your own power, or there will be plenty of people who would take pleasure in degrading you.”

CHAPTER IV

--- emma | Female Independence | Paragraph 31 ---
“Emma never thinks of herself, if she can do good to others,” rejoined Mr. Woodhouse, understanding but in part. “But, my dear, pray do not make any more matches; they are silly things, and break up one’s family circle grievously.”

--- emma | Female Independence | Paragraph 32 ---
Harriet Smith was the natural daughter of somebody. Somebody had placed her, several years back, at Mrs. Goddard’s school, and somebody had lately raised her from the condition of scholar to that of parlour‑boarder. This was all that was generally known of her history. She had no visible friends but what had been acquired at Highbury, and was now just returned from a long visit in the country to some young ladies who had been at school there with her.

--- emma | Female Independence | Paragraph 33 ---

CHAPTER II

--- emma | Female Independence | Paragraph 35 ---
“My dear, how am I to get so far? Randalls is such a distance. I could not walk half so far.”

--- emma | Female Independence | Paragraph 36 ---
She was not struck by any thing remarkably clever in Miss Smith’s conversation, but she found her altogether very engaging—not inconveniently shy, not unwilling to talk—and yet so far from pushing, shewing so proper and becoming a deference, seeming so pleasantly grateful for being admitted to Hartfield, and so artlessly impressed by the appearance of every thing in so superior a style to what she had been used to, that she must have good sense, and deserve encouragement. Encouragement should be given. Those soft blue eyes, and all those natural graces, should not be wasted on the inferior society of Highbury and its connexions. The acquaintance she had already formed were unworthy of her. The friends from whom she had just parted, though very good sort of people, must be doing her harm. They were a family of the name of Martin, whom Emma well knew by character, as renting a large farm of Mr. Knightley, and residing in the parish of Donwell—very creditably, she believed—she knew Mr. Knightley thought highly of them—but they must be coarse and unpolished, and very unfit to be the intimates of a girl who wanted only a little more knowledge and elegance to be quite perfect. _She_ would notice her; she would improve her; she would detach her from her bad acquaintance, and introduce her into good society; she would form her opinions and her manners. It would be an interesting, and certainly a very kind undertaking; highly becoming her own situation in life, her leisure, and powers.

--- emma | Female Independence | Paragraph 37 ---
Emma Woodhouse, handsome, clever, and rich, with a comfortable home and happy disposition, seemed to unite some of the best blessings of existence; and had lived nearly twenty‑one years in the world with very little to distress or vex her.

Where is your merit? What are you proud of? You made a lucky guess; and that is all that can be said.”

But the Martins occupied her thoughts a good deal; she had spent two very happy months with them, and now loved to talk of the pleasures of her visit, and describe the many comforts and wonders of the place. Emma encouraged her talkativeness—amused by such a picture of another set of beings, and enjoying the youthful simplicity which could speak with so much exultation of Mrs. Martin’s having “_two_ parlours, two very good parlours, indeed; one of them quite as large as Mrs. Goddard’s drawing‑room; and of her having an upper maid who had lived five‑and‑twenty years with her; and of their having eight cows, two of them Alderneys, and one a little Welch cow, a very pretty little Welch cow indeed; and of Mrs. Martin’s saying as she was so fond of it, it should be called _her_ cow; and of their having a very handsome summer‑house in their garden, where some day next year they were all to drink tea:—a very handsome summer‑house, large enough to hold a dozen people.”

--- emma | Female Independence | Paragraph 43 ---
Mr. Weston was a native of Highbury, and born of a respectable family, which for the last two or three generations had been rising into gentility and property. He had received a good education, but, on succeeding early in life to a small independence, had become indisposed for any of the more homely pursuits in which his brothers were engaged, and had satisfied an active, cheerful mind and social temper by entering into the militia of his county, then embodied.

Mr. Knightley, a sensible man about seven or eight‑and‑thirty, was not only a very old and intimate friend of the family, but particularly connected with it, as the elder brother of Isabella’s husband. He lived about a mile from Highbury, was a frequent visitor, and always welcome, and at this time more welcome than usual, as coming directly from their mutual connexions in London. He had returned to a late dinner, after some days’ absence, and now walked up to Hartfield to say that all were well in Brunswick Square. It was a happy circumstance, and animated Mr. Woodhouse for some time. Mr. Knightley had a cheerful manner, which always did him good; and his many inquiries after “poor Isabella” and her children were answered most satisfactorily.
