--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 1 ---
Emma, the younger of Mr. Woodhouse’s two daughters, had been raised chiefly by an affectionate father after her sister’s marriage left her mistress of the house early; her mother’s memory was faint, and a devoted governess had filled the maternal role with almost equal tenderness.

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 2 ---
For sixteen years Miss Taylor had resided in the Woodhouse household, less as a governess than as a cherished friend, especially to Emma; their bond resembled that of sisters, and even before her formal role ended her gentle nature allowed her to impose scarcely any restraint, so that they lived together as mutually affectionate friends, Emma deferring to Miss Taylor’s judgment yet following her own inclinations.

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 3 ---
A quiet sorrow settled over Emma when Miss Taylor married; the loss of her beloved friend first stirred grief. On the wedding day Emma sat in mournful contemplation of what lay ahead; after the ceremony and departure of the bridal party, she and her father dined alone, with no third to enliven the evening. Her father soon fell into his usual post‑dinner slumber, leaving Emma to sit and muse upon what she had lost.

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 4 ---
The marriage promised happiness for Miss Taylor, whose husband Mr. Weston possessed an unblemished character, modest fortune, suitable age, and agreeable manners; Emma could take some comfort in knowing she had long wished and encouraged the match, yet the morning felt dark. The absence of Miss Taylor would be felt each hour. She recalled the sixteen years of kindness—how Miss Taylor had taught, played with, and tended her from childhood, devoting herself to Emma’s health and amusement. Though a great debt of gratitude existed, the recent seven years of equal, unreserved companionship—begun after Isabella’s marriage—were even dearer. Miss Taylor had been a friend and companion rare in her intelligence, information, usefulness, gentleness, and intimate knowledge of the family; she shared Emma’s pleasures and plans, and her affection for Emma was unfailing.

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 5 ---
How could Emma endure the change? Though Miss Taylor would reside only half a mile away, Emma understood that the distinction between a neighbouring Mrs. Weston and an in‑house Miss Taylor was great; despite her natural and domestic advantages, she now faced the peril of intellectual solitude. She loved her father dearly, yet he could not serve as a companion, lacking the capacity for rational or playful conversation.

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 6 ---
Highbury, a sizable village nearing town status, to which Hartfield—despite its private lawn, shrubberies, and name—truly belonged, offered Emma no equals. The Woodhouses stood first in consequence there; all looked up to them. She possessed many acquaintances, for her father was universally civil, yet none could substitute for Miss Taylor, even for half a day. The change was melancholy; Emma could not help sighing and wishing for impossibilities until her father awoke and demanded cheerfulness, for his spirits needed support. He was a nervous man, prone to depression, fond of those he knew, averse to parting, and opposed to any change; matrimony, as a source of change, ever displeased him, and he remained unreconciled to his own daughter’s marriage, speaking of it only with compassion, despite its affectionate nature. Now, obliged to part with Miss Taylor as well, his habit of gentle selfishness and his inability to imagine others feeling differently led him to suppose that Miss Taylor had done as sad a thing for herself as for them, and that she would have been happier remaining at Hartfield. Emma endeavoured to keep his thoughts bright with cheerful chat, but at tea he inevitably repeated his dinner‑time lament.

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 7 ---
“A house of her own!—But what advantage does a house of her own bring? This one is three times as large.—And you never indulge in odd whims, my dear.”

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 8 ---
“My dear, how am I to walk so far? Randalls lies at such a distance; I could not manage half the walk.”

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 9 ---
Mr. Knightley, a sensible gentleman of about seven or eight‑and‑thirty, was not only a long‑standing and intimate friend of the family but also closely linked through being the elder brother of Isabella’s husband. Residing roughly a mile from Highbury, he called frequently and was always welcomed; at this moment his arrival was especially welcome, coming directly from their mutual connections in London. He had returned to a late dinner after several days away and now proceeded to Hartfield to report that all were well in Brunswick Square. This happy circumstance animated Mr. Woodhouse for a time. Mr. Knightley’s cheerful manner ever did him good, and his numerous inquiries after “poor Isabella” and her children were answered most satisfactorily. Upon concluding, Mr. Woodhouse gratefully observed, “It is very kind of you, Mr. Knightley, to call upon us at this late hour; I fear you must have endured a shocking walk.”

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 10 ---
“I believe it is quite true, my dear,” said Mr. Woodhouse, sighing. “I fear I am sometimes rather fanciful and troublesome.”

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 11 ---
“Dear Emma bears everything so well,” her father remarked. “But, Mr. Knightley, she is truly sorry to lose poor Miss Taylor, and I am certain she will miss her more than she imagines.”

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 12 ---
Emma turned her head, torn between tears and smiles. “It is impossible for Emma not to miss such a companion,” declared Mr. Knightley. “We should not esteem her as we do, sir, if we could suppose otherwise; but she recognises how advantageous the marriage is for Miss Taylor, how welcome it must be at her stage of life to be settled in her own home, and how important it is for her to secure a comfortable provision; therefore she cannot allow herself to feel more pain than pleasure. Every friend of Miss Taylor ought to rejoice in her happy marriage.”

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 13 ---
Mr. Knightley shook his head at her. Her father fondly replied, “Ah! my dear, I wish you would refrain from making matches and foretelling events, for whatever you say invariably comes to pass. Pray, make no more matches.”

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 14 ---
“I promise you I will make none for myself, papa; but I must, indeed, make them for others. It is the greatest amusement in the world! And after such success, you know!—Everybody declared that Mr. Weston would never marry again. Oh dear, no! Mr. Weston, who had been a widower so long and seemed perfectly content without a wife, constantly occupied either by his town business or among his friends here, always welcome wherever he went, always cheerful—Mr. Weston need not spend a single evening in the year alone if he did not wish it. Oh no! Mr. Weston certainly would never marry again. Some even spoke of a promise to his wife on her deathbed, others of the son and the uncle preventing him. All manner of solemn nonsense was discussed on the subject, yet I believed none of it.”

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 15 ---
“Ever since the day—about four years ago—that Miss Taylor and I encountered him in Broadway Lane, when, as it began to drizzle, he gallantly darted away and borrowed two umbrellas for us from Farmer Mitchell’s, I formed my opinion on the matter. I planned the match from that hour; and now that such success has blessed me in this instance, dear papa, you cannot imagine that I shall abandon match‑making.”

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 16 ---
“Emma never thinks of herself if she can do good to others,” rejoined Mr. Woodhouse, understanding only in part. “But, my dear, pray do not make any more matches; they are foolish things, and they grievously disturb one’s family circle.”

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 17 ---
“Mr. Elton is a very pretty young man, to be sure, and a very good young man, and I hold him in high regard. But if you wish to show him any attention, my dear, invite him to dine with us some day. That would be far more suitable. I dare say Mr. Knightley will be so kind as to meet him.”

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 18 ---
He had only himself to please in his choice, for his fortune was his own; as for Frank, it was more than being tacitly raised as his uncle’s heir—it had become an avowed adoption, so that upon coming of age he assumed the name of Churchill. Consequently, it was most unlikely that he would ever require his father’s assistance. His father entertained no apprehension of that. The aunt was a capricious woman, ruling her husband entirely; yet it was not in Mr. Weston’s nature to imagine that any caprice could be strong enough to affect one so dear, and, as he believed, so deservedly dear. He saw his son each year in London, and was proud of him; his favourable report of him as a remarkably fine young man had caused Highbury to feel a certain pride in him as well. He was regarded as sufficiently belonging to the place to make his merits and prospects a kind of common concern.

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 19 ---
She knew that at times she would be missed, and could not contemplate, without pain, Emma’s loss of a single pleasure or an hour’s ennui from the want of her companionableness; yet dear Emma was of no feeble character—she was more equal to her situation than most girls would have been, possessing sense, energy, and spirits that might be hoped would carry her well and happily through its modest difficulties and privations. Moreover, there was great comfort in the very easy distance of Randalls from Hartfield, convenient for even solitary female walks, and in Mr. Weston’s disposition and circumstances, which would make the approaching season no hindrance to their spending half the week’s evenings together.

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 20 ---
Harriet Smith’s intimacy at Hartfield soon became a settled arrangement. Quick and decisive in her ways, Emma lost no time in inviting, encouraging, and urging her to come very often; as their acquaintance grew, so did their mutual satisfaction. As a walking companion, Emma had early perceived how useful Harriet might prove. In that respect Mrs. Weston’s loss had been significant. Her father never ventured beyond the shrubbery, where two divisions of the ground sufficed him for his long or short walk, according to the season; and since Mrs. Weston’s marriage her exercise had been overly confined. She had once attempted the walk to Randalls alone, but found it unpleasant; thus a Harriet Smith, whom she could summon at any time for a walk, would be a valuable addition to her privileges. Yet, as she came to know Harriet better, she approved her in every respect and felt confirmed in all her kindly designs.

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 21 ---
Harriet certainly was not clever, but she possessed a sweet, docile, grateful disposition, was wholly free of conceit, and only desired to be guided by anyone she looked up to. Her early attachment to Emma was most amiable; and her inclination for good company, together with her power to appreciate what was elegant and clever, showed that there was no lack of taste, though strength of understanding need not be expected. Altogether Emma was quite convinced that Harriet Smith was exactly the young friend she wanted—exactly the something her home required. Such a friend as Mrs. Weston was out of the question; two such could never be granted, and two such she did not desire. It was quite a different sort of feeling, distinct and independent. Mrs. Weston was the object of regard founded on gratitude and esteem; Harriet would be loved as one to whom Emma could be useful. For Mrs. Weston there was nothing to be done; for Harriet everything.

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 22 ---
For a time she was amused, without reflecting beyond the immediate cause; but as she came to understand the family better, other feelings arose. She had taken up a mistaken notion, imagining it to be a mother and daughter, a son and son’s wife living together; but when it appeared that the Mr. Martin who figured in the narrative, and was always mentioned with approval for his great good‑nature in doing something or other, was a single man, that there was no young Mrs. Martin, no wife in the case, she began to suspect danger to her poor little friend from all this hospitality and kindness, and that, if she were not looked after, she might be required to sink herself forever.

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 23 ---
“Six years hence! Dear Miss Woodhouse, he will be thirty years old!”

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 24 ---
They encountered Mr. Martin the very next day, as they walked along the Donwell road. He was on foot, and after looking very respectfully at her, he turned his gaze with unfeigned satisfaction toward her companion. Emma was not sorry to have such an opportunity for observation; and, walking a few yards ahead while they conversed, she soon made her keen eye sufficiently acquainted with Mr. Robert Martin. His appearance was very neat, and he resembled a sensible young man, but his person offered no other advantage; and when she compared him with gentlemen, she thought he must forfeit all the ground he had gained in Harriet’s esteem. Harriet was not insensible to manners; she had voluntarily noticed her father’s gentleness with admiration as well as wonder. Mr. Martin seemed unaware of what manner entailed.

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 25 ---
On such occasions poor Mr. Woodhouse’s feelings were in sad conflict. He loved to have the cloth laid, because it had been the fashion of his youth, yet his conviction that suppers were very unwholesome made him rather sorry to see anything placed upon it; and while his hospitality would have welcomed his visitors to everything, his concern for their health caused him to grieve that they would eat.

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 26 ---
“What sort of looking man is Mr. Martin?”

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 27 ---
Genuine, long‑standing regard united the Westons and Mr. Knightley; and through Mr. Elton, a young man living alone who disliked solitude, the privilege of exchanging any vacant evening of his own blank solitude for the elegancies and society of Mr. Woodhouse’s drawing‑room, and the smiles of his lovely daughter, was in no danger of being discarded.

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 28 ---
Her situation was altogether the subject of hours of gratitude to Mrs. Weston, and of moments only of regret; and her satisfaction—her more than satisfaction—her cheerful enjoyment, was so just and so apparent that Emma, who knew her father well, was sometimes taken by surprise at his still being able to pity ‘poor Miss Taylor,’ when they left her at Randalls amid every domestic comfort, or saw her depart in the evening attended by her pleasant husband to a carriage of her own. Yet never did she depart without Mr. Woodhouse’s gentle sigh and the words, “Ah, poor Miss Taylor! She would be very glad to stay.”

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 29 ---
Now, upon his father’s marriage, it was very generally proposed, as a most proper attention, that the visit should take place. There was not a dissenting voice on the subject, whether Mrs. Perry drank tea with Mrs. and Miss Bates, or when Mrs. and Miss Bates returned the visit. Now was the time for Mr. Frank Churchill to come among them; and the hope strengthened when it was learned that he had written to his new mother on the occasion. For a few days, every morning visit in Highbury included some mention of the handsome letter Mrs. Weston had received. “I suppose you have heard of the handsome letter Mr. Frank Churchill has written to Mrs. Weston? I understand it was a very handsome letter indeed. Mr. Woodhouse told me of it. Mr. Woodhouse saw the letter, and he says he never saw such a handsome letter in his life.”

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 30 ---
They remained but a few minutes together, as Miss Woodhouse must not be kept waiting; and Harriet then came running to her with a smiling face, and in a flutter of spirits, which Miss Woodhouse hoped would soon be composed.

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 31 ---
“Emma knows I never flatter her,” said Mr. Knightley, “but I meant no reflection on anybody. Miss Taylor has been accustomed to having two persons to please; she will now have but one. The chances are that she must be a gainer.”

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 32 ---
Captain Weston, who had been regarded, especially by the Churchills, as having made an amazing match, proved to have received the worse end of the bargain; for when his wife died after a three‑year marriage, he was rather poorer than at first, and with a child to maintain. From the expense of the child, however, he was soon relieved. The boy had, aided by the additional softening claim of his mother’s lingering illness, been the means of a sort of reconciliation; and Mr. and Mrs. Churchill, having no children of their own nor any other young creature of equal kindred to care for, offered to assume the entire charge of little Frank soon after her decease. Some scruples and some reluctance the widower‑father might be supposed to have felt; but as they were overcome by other considerations, the child was given up to the care and wealth of the Churchills, leaving him only his own comfort to seek and his own situation to improve as he could.

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 33 ---
“Mr. Martin, I suppose, is not a man of information beyond the line of his own business? He does not read?”

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 34 ---
“There is no saying, indeed,” replied Harriet rather solemnly.

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 35 ---
CHAPTER IV

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 36 ---
Mr. Knightley was, in fact, one of the few who could discern faults in Emma Woodhouse, and the sole individual who ever ventured to tell her of them; and though this was not particularly pleasing to Emma herself, she knew it would be far less agreeable to her father, and therefore she would not have him truly suspect such a circumstance as her not being thought perfect by everyone.

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 37 ---
“To be sure. Yes. Not that I think Mr. Martin would ever marry anyone but what had received some education—and been very well brought up. However, I do not mean to set up my opinion against yours—and I am sure I shall not wish for the acquaintance of his wife. I shall always have a great regard for the Miss Martins, especially Elizabeth, and should be very sorry to give them up, for they are quite as well educated as I. But if he marries a very ignorant, vulgar woman, certainly I had better not visit her, if I can help it.”

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 38 ---
Mrs. Goddard presided over a School—not a seminary, nor an establishment, nor anything that claimed, in long sentences of refined nonsense, to combine liberal acquirements with elegant morality upon new principles and systems—but a genuine, honest, old‑fashioned boarding‑school, where a reasonable quantity of accomplishments were sold at a reasonable price, and where girls might be sent to be out of the way and scramble themselves into a little education, without any danger of returning as prodigies. Mrs. Goddard’s school enjoyed high repute—and very deservedly; for Highbury was reckoned a particularly healthy spot: she possessed an ample house and garden, provided the children with plenty of wholesome food, allowed them to run about a great deal in the summer, and in winter dressed their chilblains with her own hands. It was no wonder that a train of twenty young couples now walked after her to church. She was a plain, motherly sort of woman, who had laboured hard in her youth and now considered herself entitled to the occasional holiday of a tea‑visit; and having formerly owed much to Mr. Woodhouse’s kindness, she felt his particular claim on her to leave her neat parlour, hung round with fancy‑work, whenever she could, and win or lose a few sixpences by his fireside.

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 39 ---
“He was four‑and‑twenty on the 8th of last June, and my birthday falls on the 23rd—just a fortnight and a day apart—which is rather odd.”

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 40 ---
“Certainly, he is not like Mr. Knightley. He lacks that fine air and manner of walking which Mr. Knightley possesses. I see the difference plainly. But Mr. Knightley is indeed a very fine gentleman!”

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 41 ---
“Well done, Mrs. Martin!” thought Emma. “You know exactly what you are about.”

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 42 ---
“Well, and that is as early as most men can afford to marry, who are not born to independence. Mr. Martin, I imagine, has his fortune entirely to make—he cannot be at all beforehand with the world. Whatever money he might inherit upon his father’s death, whatever his share of the family property, I dare say it is all afloat, all employed in his stock, and so forth; and although, with diligence and good luck, he may become rich in time, it is next to impossible that he should have realised anything yet.”

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 43 ---
“My dearest papa! You do not think I could mean _you_, or suppose Mr. Knightley to mean _you_. What a horrible idea! Oh no! I meant only myself. Mr. Knightley loves to find fault with me, you know—in a joke—it is all a joke. We always say what we like to one another.”

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 44 ---
“Oh! not handsome—not at all handsome. I thought him very plain at first, but I do not think him so plain now. One does not, you know, after a time. But did you never see him? He is in Highbury every now and then, and he is sure to ride through every week on his way to Kingston. He has passed you very often.”

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 45 ---
Her first attempts at usefulness were in an endeavour to discover who were the parents, but Harriet could not tell. She was ready to divulge everything within her power, yet on this subject questions were vain. Emma was obliged to conjecture what she liked—but she could never believe that, in the same situation, she herself would not have discovered the truth. Harriet possessed no penetration. She had been satisfied to hear and believe just what Mrs. Goddard chose to tell her, and looked no farther.

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 46 ---
“With a great deal of pleasure, sir, at any time,” said Mr. Knightley, laughing, “and I agree with you entirely that it will be a much better thing. Invite him to dinner, Emma, and help him to the best of the fish and the chicken, but leave him to choose his own wife. Depend upon it, a man of six or seven‑and‑twenty can take care of himself.”

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 47 ---
“To be sure, so it is. But they live very comfortably. They have no indoor servant, else they do not want for anything; and Mrs. Martin talks of taking on a boy another year.”

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 48 ---
“I wonder he did not remember the book”—was all Harriet’s answer, spoken with a degree of grave displeasure which Emma thought might safely be left to itself. She therefore said no more for some time. Her next beginning was,

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 49 ---
“Only one more, papa; only for Mr. Elton. Poor Mr. Elton! You like Mr. Elton, papa—I must look about for a wife for him. There is nobody in Highbury who deserves him—and he has been here a whole year, and has fitted up his house so comfortably, that it would be a shame to have him remain single any longer—and I thought when he was joining their hands today, he looked so very much as if he would like to have the same kind office done for him! I think very well of Mr. Elton, and this is the only way I have of doing him a service.”

--- emma | Friendship | Paragraph 50 ---
“Well,” said Emma, willing to let it pass—“you want to hear about the wedding; and I shall be happy to tell you, for we all behaved charmingly. Every body was punctual, every body appeared in their best looks: not a tear, and hardly a long face to be seen. Oh no; we all felt that we were going to be only half a mile apart, and were sure of meeting every day.”

--- emma | Self-Delusion | Paragraph 1 ---
Mr. Knightley, with his accustomed gravity, inquired, ‘I confess I do not comprehend what you intend by the word “success.” Success, sir, presupposes endeavour. If you have, for these past four years, devoted your time properly and delicately to the furtherance of this union, then your occupation is surely commendable for a young lady’s mind. Yet, if, as I suspect, your so‑called making of the match amounts merely to a fleeting fancy—an idle thought one day that Miss Taylor would fare well were Mr. Weston to become her husband, and a repetition of that notion thereafter—then where lies the merit of which you boast? What cause have you for pride? You have merely made a fortunate conjecture, and that is all that can be said.’

--- emma | Self-Delusion | Paragraph 2 ---
Frank Churchill possessed no need to consult another in the choice of his wife, for his fortune was entirely his own; indeed, his upbringing as the tacit heir of his uncle had become so openly acknowledged that, upon attaining his majority, he assumed the name of Churchill. Consequently, it seemed most improbable that he would ever require his father’s assistance, a notion his father never entertained. The aunt, a woman of capricious temperament, held sway over her husband, yet Mr. Weston could not persuade himself that any whim could be potent enough to disturb one so dear, and, as he believed, so justly cherished. He saw his son each year in London, took pride in him, and his favourable report caused the inhabitants of Highbury to share a sense of pride in the young man, regarding him as sufficiently belonging to the locality that his merits and prospects became a matter of common concern.

--- emma | Self-Delusion | Paragraph 3 ---
Mrs. Goddard presided over a school—not a seminary, nor an establishment professing, in lengthy sentences of refined nonsense, to unite liberal attainments with elegant morality upon novel principles—but a genuine, honest, old‑fashioned boarding‑school, where a reasonable quantity of accomplishments was offered at a reasonable price, and where girls might be sent to be removed from the world, to acquire a modest education without the risk of returning as prodigies. Her establishment enjoyed high repute, and justly so, for Highbury was accounted a particularly salubrious neighbourhood: she possessed an ample house and garden, supplied the children with abundant wholesome nourishment, allowed them ample liberty to run about in the summer, and in the winter tended to their chilblains with her own hands. It was therefore no surprise that a procession of twenty young couples now followed her to church. She was a plain, matronly sort of woman, who had laboured diligently in her youth and now deemed herself entitled to the occasional respite of a tea‑visit; moreover, having formerly been indebted to Mr. Woodhouse’s kindness, she felt a particular obligation to leave her neat parlour, adorned with fancy‑work, whenever opportunity presented, and to try her luck at his fireside for a few sixpences.

--- emma | Self-Delusion | Paragraph 4 ---
Emma’s initial endeavour to be of service lay in discovering Harriet’s parentage, yet Harriet could furnish no information. She was prepared to disclose everything within her power, but on this point interrogation proved futile. Emma was compelled to imagine what she preferred, though she could never convince herself that, placed in the same circumstances, she would not have uncovered the truth. Harriet lacked discernment; she had been content to hear and believe whatever Mrs. Goddard chose to impart, and she sought no further.

--- emma | Self-Delusion | Paragraph 5 ---
'I entertain no doubt that he is a very respectable young man,' Emma affirmed; 'indeed, I know him to be such, and, as such, I wish him well. Pray, what do you suppose his age to be?'

--- emma | Self-Delusion | Paragraph 6 ---
'Well, that is as early as most men can afford to marry, provided they are not born to independence. I imagine Mr. Martin’s fortune remains wholly to be made; he cannot be ahead of the world. Whatever monies he might inherit upon his father’s decease, whatever his portion of the family estate, I dare say it is all afloat, all employed in his stock and the like; and though, with diligence and good fortune, he may attain wealth in time, it is next to impossible that he should have realised anything of substance thus far.'

--- emma | Self-Delusion | Paragraph 7 ---
'Especially when one of those two is such a fanciful, troublesome creature!' Emma remarked playfully. 'That is precisely what occupies your thoughts, I know—and what you would certainly utter were my father not present.'

--- emma | Self-Delusion | Paragraph 8 ---
Harriet Smith’s intimacy at Hartfield soon became an established habit. Quick and decisive in her manners, Emma lost no time in inviting, encouraging, and urging her to come very often; as their acquaintance deepened, so did their mutual satisfaction. As a walking companion, Emma had early perceived how useful Harriet might prove. In that regard, Mrs. Weston’s absence had been felt, for her father never ventured beyond the shrubbery, where two divisions of the ground sufficed for his long or short walk according to the season; and since Mrs. Weston’s marriage her exercise had been overly confined. She had once ventured alone to Randalls, but the experience had not been agreeable; thus a Harriet Smith, whom Emma could summon at any moment for a walk, would be a valuable addition to her privileges. Yet, in every respect, as Emma observed her more closely, she approved her and was confirmed in all her benevolent designs.

--- emma | Self-Delusion | Paragraph 9 ---
'You comprehend the force of influence well, Harriet; nevertheless I would have you so firmly settled in good society as to be independent even of Hartfield and Miss Woodhouse. I desire to see you permanently well connected, and to that end it would be advisable to keep the number of odd acquaintances as low as possible; therefore I say that, should you still reside in this neighbourhood when Mr. Martin marries, I hope you will not be drawn into acquaintance with his sisters, lest you be led to know the wife, who will likely be a mere farmer’s daughter, devoid of education.'

--- emma | Self-Delusion | Paragraph 10 ---
Emma turned her head, torn between tears and smiles. ‘It is impossible that Emma should not miss such a companion,’ observed Mr. Knightley. ‘We should not esteem her so highly as we do, sir, if we could suppose it; but she knows how advantageous this marriage is to Miss Taylor, how exceedingly acceptable it must be, at Miss Taylor’s stage of life, to be settled in a home of her own, and how vital it is for her to secure a comfortable provision; therefore she cannot allow herself to feel so much pain as pleasure. Every friend of Miss Taylor must rejoice in her happy marriage.'

--- emma | Self-Delusion | Paragraph 11 ---
'Only think of our happening to meet him!—How exceedingly odd! It was quite a chance, he remarked, that he had not taken the route via Randalls. He did not imagine we ever walked this road; he supposed we usually walked towards Randalls. He has not yet been able to obtain the Romance of the Forest. He was so occupied the last time he was at Kingston that he quite forgot it, but he intends to go again tomorrow. Thus it is very odd that we should encounter him thus. Well, Miss Woodhouse, is he as you anticipated? What is your impression of him? Do you deem him very plain?'

--- emma | Self-Delusion | Paragraph 12 ---
'To be sure,' Harriet replied in a mortified tone, 'he is not so genteel as true gentlemen.'

--- emma | Self-Delusion | Paragraph 13 ---
A complete alteration of life became desirable. He relinquished the militia and entered trade, having brothers already established in a respectable manner in London, which afforded him a favourable opening. It was a concern that provided just sufficient employment. He retained a modest dwelling in Highbury, where most of his leisure hours were spent; and between useful occupation and the enjoyments of society, the ensuing eighteen or twenty years of his existence passed pleasantly. By that time he had acquired an easy competence—enough to secure the purchase of a little estate adjoining Highbury, which he had long coveted—enough to marry a woman as portionless as Miss Taylor, and to live according to the inclinations of his own amiable and sociable disposition.

--- emma | Self-Delusion | Paragraph 14 ---
Emma permitted her father to speak, yet she attended her visitors in a far more satisfactory manner, and on the present evening she took particular pleasure in sending them away contented. Harriet’s happiness equalled Emma’s intentions. Miss Woodhouse was so eminent a figure in Highbury that the prospect of her introduction had provoked as much alarm as delight; but the humble, grateful little girl departed with highly gratified feelings, delighted by the affability with which Miss Woodhouse had treated her throughout the evening, and even shaking hands with her at last.

--- emma | Self-Delusion | Paragraph 15 ---
For a time Emma was amused, without reflecting beyond the immediate cause; but as she grew to understand the family better, other sentiments arose. She had embraced a mistaken notion, imagining that the household comprised a mother and daughter, a son and his son’s wife, all dwelling together; yet when it appeared that the Mr. Martin who figured in the narrative, and was ever mentioned with approbation for his great good‑nature in performing some task or other, was in fact a bachelor—there being no young Mrs. Martin, no wife in the case—she began to suspect danger for her poor little friend amid all this hospitality and kindness, and to fear that, if Harriet were not looked after, she might be compelled to sink herself into perpetual obscurity.

--- emma | Self-Delusion | Paragraph 16 ---
'To be sure,' Harriet conceded, 'I do not imagine Mr. Martin would ever wed anyone who had not received some education and been well brought up. However, I do not intend to oppose my opinion to yours, and I am certain I shall not desire the acquaintance of his wife. I shall always retain a great regard for the Miss Martins, especially Elizabeth, and should be sorry to part with them, for they are as well educated as I. But if he should marry a very ignorant, vulgar woman, I had certainly better avoid her visitation, if I can prevent it.'

--- emma | Self-Delusion | Paragraph 17 ---
Miss Churchill, however, being of age and possessed of the full command of her fortune—though her fortune bore no proportion to the family estate—was not to be dissuaded from the marriage, and it took place, to the infinite mortification of Mr. and Mrs. Churchill, who withdrew their regard with due decorum. It proved an unsuitable alliance and yielded little happiness. Mrs. Weston ought to have found more satisfaction in it, for she possessed a husband whose warm heart and sweet temper led him to deem everything due to her in return for her great goodness in loving him; yet, although she possessed one sort of spirit, she lacked the finest. She had sufficient resolution to pursue her own will despite her brother, but not enough to refrain from unreasonable regrets at his unreasonable anger, nor to forego longing for the luxuries of her former home. They lived beyond their income, yet still it was nothing in comparison with Enscombe; she did not cease to love her husband, but she wished simultaneously to be the wife of Captain Weston and Miss Churchill of Enscombe.

--- emma | Self-Delusion | Paragraph 27 ---
There was no recovering Miss Taylor—nor much likelihood of ceasing to pity her; but a few weeks brought some alleviation to Mr. Woodhouse. The compliments of his neighbours were at an end; he was no longer teased by being wished joy of so sorrowful an event; and the wedding‑cake, which had been a great distress to him, was entirely consumed. His own stomach could tolerate nothing rich, and he could never believe other people to differ from himself. What was unwholesome to him he regarded as unfit for any body; and he had, therefore, earnestly endeavoured to dissuade them from having any wedding‑cake at all, and when that proved vain, as earnestly endeavoured to prevent anyone from eating it. He had taken the trouble to consult Mr. Perry, the apothecary, whose frequent visits were among the comforts of Mr. Woodhouse’s life; and upon being applied to, Mr. Perry could not but acknowledge (though it seemed contrary to his own inclination) that wedding‑cake might certainly disagree with many—perhaps with most people—unless taken in moderation. With such an opinion, confirming his own, Mr. Woodhouse hoped to influence every visitor of the newly married pair; yet the cake was still eaten, and there was no respite for his benevolent nerves until it had all disappeared.

--- emma | Self-Delusion | Paragraph 28 ---
It was, indeed, a highly prized letter. Mrs. Weston had, of course, formed a very favourable opinion of the young man; and such a pleasing attention was an irresistible proof of his excellent good sense, and a most welcome addition to every source and every expression of congratulation which her marriage had already secured. She felt herself a most fortunate woman; and she had lived long enough to know how fortunate she might well be thought, where the only regret was a partial separation from friends whose friendship for her had never cooled, and who could ill bear to part with her.

She was a very pretty girl, and her beauty happened to be of a sort which Emma particularly admired. --- emma | Self-Delusion | Paragraph 32 ---
Highbury, a large and populous village, almost amounting to a town, to which Hartfield—despite its separate lawn, shrubberies, and name—did indeed belong, afforded her no equals. The Woodhouses held the first rank in consequence there. All looked up to them. She possessed many acquaintances in the place, for her father was universally civil, yet not one among them who could be accepted in lieu of Miss Taylor, even for half a day. It was a melancholy change; and Emma could not but sigh over it, and wish for impossible things, until her father awoke and made it necessary to be cheerful. His spirits required support. He was a nervous man, easily depressed; fond of everybody he had been accustomed to, and averse to parting with them; averse to change of every kind. Matrimony, as the source of change, was always disagreeable; and he was by no means yet reconciled to his own daughter’s marrying, nor could he ever speak of her but with compassion, although it had been entirely a match of affection, when he was now obliged to part with Miss Taylor too; and from his habits of gentle selfishness, and of being never able to suppose that other people could feel differently from himself, he was very much inclined to think Miss Taylor had done as sad a thing for herself as for them, and would have been far happier had she spent the remainder of her life at Hartfield. Emma smiled and chatted as cheerfully as she could, to keep him from such thoughts; but when tea arrived, it was impossible for him not to say exactly as he had said at dinner.

A degree or two lower, and a creditable appearance might interest me; I might hope to be of use to their families in some way or other. But a farmer can need none of my assistance, and is therefore, in one sense, as far above my notice as in every other respect he lies below it.'

Mr. Weston was a native of Highbury, born of a respectable family which, for the last two or three generations, had been rising into gentility and property. He had received a good education, but, having succeeded early in life to a modest independence, he became disinclined to the more homely pursuits in which his brothers were engaged, and satisfied his active, cheerful mind and social temperament by entering the militia of his county, then embodied.

--- emma | Self-Delusion | Paragraph 42 ---
Mr. Knightley, a sensible man of about seven or eight‑and‑thirty, was not only a very old and intimate friend of the family, but particularly connected with it, as the elder brother of Isabella’s husband. He resided about a mile from Highbury, was a frequent visitor, and always welcome, and at this time more welcome than usual, as coming directly from their mutual connexions in London. He had returned to a late dinner after some days’ absence, and now walked up to Hartfield to announce that all were well in Brunswick Square. It was a happy circumstance, and it animated Mr. Woodhouse for a time. Mr. Knightley possessed a cheerful manner, which ever did him good; and his many inquiries after ‘poor Isabella’ and her children were answered most satisfactorily. When this was over, Mr. Woodhouse gratefully observed, ‘It is very kind of you, Mr. Knightley, to come out at this late hour to call upon us. I am afraid you must have had a shocking walk.'

--- emma | Self-Delusion | Paragraph 46 ---
These were the ladies whom Emma found herself very frequently able to assemble; and happy was she, for her father’s sake, in that power; though, as far as she herself was concerned, it was no remedy for the absence of Mrs. Weston. She was delighted to see her father look comfortable, and very much pleased with herself for having contrived things so well; but the quiet prosings of three such women made her feel that every evening so spent was indeed one of the long evenings she had fearfully anticipated.

--- emma | Self-Delusion | Paragraph 47 ---
With this inspiriting notion, her questions increased in number and meaning; and she particularly led Harriet to speak more of Mr. Martin, and there was evidently no dislike to it. Harriet was very ready to discourse of the share he had had in their moonlight walks and merry evening games; and she dwelt a good deal upon his being so very good‑humoured and obliging. He had traversed three miles round one day in order to bring her some walnuts, because she had declared how fond she was of them, and in every other respect he was so very obliging. He had brought his shepherd’s son into the parlour one night on purpose to sing to her. She was very fond of singing. He could sing a little himself. She believed him to be very clever, and to understand everything. He possessed a very fine flock, and, while she was with them, he had been bid more for his wool than anyone in the country. She believed everyone spoke well of him. His mother and sisters were very fond of him. Mrs. Martin had told her one day (and there was a blush as she uttered it) that it was impossible for anyone to be a better son, and therefore she was sure, whenever he married, he would make a good husband. Not that she desired him to marry. She was in no hurry at all.

--- emma | Self-Delusion | Paragraph 48 ---
